At the center is an oval shield divided quarterly: the first and fourth quarters contain the Wittelsbach lozenges (Bavaria), while the second and third quarters feature a crowned lion rampant (Palatinate). A central inescutcheon displays the Imperial Orb, signifying the electoral office of Arch-Steward <note>The "Elector" was one of the high-ranking princes who had the right to vote for the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>. The shield is encircled by the collar of the Order of the Golden Fleece <note>One of the most prestigious orders of knighthood in Christendom</note>, with the lamb pendant hanging at the base. Above the shield, four winged cherubs (putti) support a large, ornate electoral crown. The entire composition is surrounded by decorative scrolls and garlands of fruit. At the bottom, a large, flowing ribbon contains the printed inscription.</image-desc>

->RY OF THE MOST SERENE<-
->DUKES OF BOTH BAVARIAS.<-
<note>original: "EX ELECTORALI BIBLIOTHECA SERENISS. VTRIVSQ. BAVARIAE DVCVM." The phrase "Both Bavarias" refers to the historical divisions of Upper and Lower Bavaria which were united under the Wittelsbach dynasty.</note>

<meta>This is the title page of the first volume of Martin Delrio's influential work on magic and demonology. This 1603 edition follows the original 1599 publication, featuring expanded content and a complex engraved border.</meta>

# SIX BOOKS OF INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC,
## Divided into Three Volumes.

->*By the Author*<-
->**MARTIN DELRIO, Priest of the SOCIETY**<-
->**OF JESUS <note>The Jesuits</note>, Doctor of SACRED THEO-**<-
->**LOGY, and Professor of Holy Scripture**<-
->**at the Academy of GRAZ.**<-

### Volume One.

->**NOW ENLARGED BY A SECOND REVISION**<-
->*extensively, with many additions inserted throughout:*<-
->*and also corrected, with errors removed.*<-

---

->MAINZ,<-
->At the house of **JOHANN ALBINUS**.<-

---

->IN THE YEAR 1603.<-
->*With the grace and privilege of His Imperial Majesty <note>Refers to the Holy Roman Emperor</note> for twenty years.*<-

<summary>The title page of Martin Delrio's "Disquisitiones Magicae," a foundational Jesuit treatise on magic and the supernatural, printed in Mainz in 1603. The elaborate border illustrations link the theme of "magic" to the biblical plagues of Egypt, framing the work as a theological study of divine versus demonic power.</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal dedication of Martin Delrio's "Disquisitions on Magic." It establishes the patronage of Ernst of Bavaria, a powerful ecclesiastical prince of the Counter-Reformation.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative rectangular woodcut headpiece featuring intricate foliage, scrollwork, and human figures. In the center is a small portrait-like face flanked by two winged cherubs or putti. On the far left and right ends, human figures emerge from the scrolling floral patterns.</image-desc>

# TO THE MOST SERENE
# PRINCE ERNST,
## ARCHBISHOP OF COLOGNE, ARCH-CHANCELLOR
### OF THE HOLY ROMAN EMPIRE THROUGHOUT ITALY
### and Prince-Elector; Bishop of Liège, Administrator of Münster,
### Hildesheim, Freising, and Stavelot; Count Palatine of the Rhine, Duke of both
### Bavarias, Engern, Westphalia, and Bouillon; Marquess of Francimont;
### Count of Looz, Rochefort, Horne, etc.
### Patron of good men and the learned.

---

->MARTIN DELRIO OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS,<-
->PRIEST, WILLINGLY AND DESERVEDLY DEDICATES HIMSELF AND HIS WORKS.<-
<note>original: "SOCIET. IESV PRESBYTER, SE SVAQVE L. M. D. D." The abbreviation L.M.D.D. stands for Libens Merito Dedicat Dicat, a formal Latin formula for a dedication.</note>

---

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'V' set within a square frame. The letter is intertwined with elaborate late Renaissance/Baroque floral and foliate patterns, including stylized flowers and leaves.</image-desc>

**V**NIVERSAL INVESTIGATIONS OF MAGIC; *which, as a part of divine and human wisdom, is the more worthy of being brought to light the more it is hidden;* I humbly offer this *arduous and manifold midnight labor* (MOST SERENE PRINCE). Indeed, I was afraid to undergo the judgment of so learned a Prince; but, because I had to undergo someone’s judgment, I chose him who was actually capable of judging. I love **PROTECTION** no more than I love **CRITICISM**: being learned by nature and practice, I prefer to be taught and advised by the skilled than to be admired and praised by the ignorant. As few **PRINCES** do, you have skillfully and prudently joined to a sharp wit and excellence of judgment a great deal of reading and varied erudition; and to this, a long-standing experience in the **SECRETS OF NATURE**. By this diligence, you have brought back the custom of philosophizing—worthy of a **ROYAL** throne—which had been interrupted by **KINGS** for so many centuries; and by imitating those most praiseworthy **PRINCES** (whose memory shall live and flourish as long as time exists and men live), you have made yourself a sharer in their glory. Those who were once the most powerful and wisest of **KINGS** were called **MAGI** <note>The term "Magi" originally referred to wise men or priest-kings in the ancient East, before the term "magic" took on its modern, often negative, connotations.</note>. This pursuit was taken up by the common people, and consequently the matter became cheapened along with the name, because that Evil One <note>Satan</note> substituted superstition for **WISDOM**. That plague, on account of the desire for knowledge innate to men, soon spread itself through the whole world. Thus the false held sway instead of the true, the harmful instead of the useful, and the hellish instead of the heavenly. So it became a matter of the highest necessity, requiring equal sagacity and the most industrious effort, to separate and uproot the poisonous weeds while keeping the good seed safe. I saw this, and I grieved, and with many people approving, I have attempted to create a medicine for the disease; nor did I fear, with **JESUS** as my leader, to oppose myself to a raging Satan even in this area. I publish a specimen of my labor, and a certain foretaste of the courses—two books out of the six already prepared and written—because, through the [timing] of the Trade Fairs... <note>The text cuts off here, referring to the Frankfurt Book Fair schedules which dictated publishing timelines in the early modern period.</note>

<summary>In this dedication, the Jesuit scholar Martin Delrio presents his monumental work on magic to Prince Ernst of Bavaria, praising the Prince's scientific interests while framing the study of magic as a necessary effort to distinguish true wisdom from demonic superstition.</summary>
<keywords>Ernst of Bavaria, Martin Delrio, Society of Jesus, Disquisitiones Magicae, demonology, history of science, Counter-Reformation, patronage, Magi, superstition</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication of the work, addressing the patron directly with high rhetorical praise typical of late 16th-century scholarly prefaces.</meta>

->DEDICATORY EPISTLE<-

*Because of the looming deadline and various delays from those without whom these pages could not have come to light, I was unable to provide more. I have added various seasonings of ancient and recent histories, so that I might cater to the stomach of this age, which is nauseated by both bile and bitterness. But what <term>Apicius</term><note>Marcus Gavius Apicius, a famous Roman gourmet; the author uses him as a metaphor for a skillful writer catering to diverse tastes.</note> is so diligent or skilled that he can cook for every palate? If this work suits yours (MOST SERENE PRINCE), which is honest and healthy, I shall be secure, for the words of others will simply flow past. Nor do I lack confidence. For VIRTUE always loves to join the MUSES <note>The Muses represent the arts, history, and learning.</note> to herself as companions; and those whose deeds are not unworthy of being written about look with favor upon writing. For others, it would have been more than enough to excel in Nobility, or Pontifical dignity, or Sovereignty; to you, all these together seemed too little. Not content with IMPERIAL and ROYAL lineage—before which no other could deservedly place itself, whether in antiquity or splendor—you knew this to be a sign of another's virtue rather than your own. You were not content with so many Ecclesiastical tiaras <note>Referring to the Prince's high-ranking positions in the Church.</note>, in which you are certainly second to none but the SHEPHERD OF SHEPHERDS <note>The Pope.</note>, and which can sometimes fall to the unworthy. Nor were you content with a most wide and wealthy dominion, which for others would be an enticement to leisure. To these you have added many things bravely and happily achieved in WAR, and dangerous seditions very swiftly suppressed, and the renowned honors of PEACE, won with the greatest goodwill and congratulation of the <term>Eburones</term><note>A Germanic-Belgic tribe; here used archaically to refer to the people of the Prince's territories, likely around Liège.</note> and your other subjects. They rejoice for themselves and consider themselves happy under Prince ERNEST; and they pray that the FATHER OF THE COUNTRY may be granted as many years as are attributed to <term>Nestor</term><note>The legendary long-lived Greek king from the Iliad.</note>, even if those years are taken from their own lives. Furthermore, you devote yourself so entirely to the interests of the CATHOLIC faith and the ROMAN CHURCH, the pillar of truth, that in that Northern region, you seem chosen by God as a PROTECTOR, a WALL, and a WATCHTOWER. For who has undertaken so many sharp contentions for the true and ancient religion with more fervent zeal, or sustained them more constantly once undertaken? Who is more industrious in detecting the snares of enemies, meeting them once detected, and disturbing the plans and attempts of the impious? Finally, which (in my opinion) is a rare example and one to be envied by many Princes: though distracted by so many cares, you find time to spend on the studies of WISDOM. If you deem any small hour worthy of being spent in reading these writings of mine, I hope they will not be entirely displeasing. Certainly, what they cannot achieve through my own talent, they will at least attain from the HAPPY GENIUS of a WISE and LEARNED PRINCE. Then my judgment will be deservedly approved, and I shall find that saying of <term>Cato</term><note>Cato the Elder, Roman statesman known for his moral maxims.</note> to be most true: It makes a very great difference where you place a kindness. "Doubtless because that which is spent on the wicked becomes a misdeed; but that which is bestowed with a good heart upon the good cannot lose its name, nor fail to be most welcome and most certain."*

*LOUVAIN, March 9, 1599.* <note>original: "VII. ID. MART. CIƆ. IƆ. XCIX." using the Roman calendar and archaic numeral forms.</note>

<meta>catchword: IN R. V.</meta>

<summary>The author concludes his dedication to Prince Ernest, praising his military successes, ecclesiastical standing, and defense of Catholicism in Northern Europe while highlighting the Prince's personal devotion to scholarship.</summary>
<keywords>Prince Ernest, dedication, Catholic Church, Counter-Reformation, Louvain, 1599, patronage, Eburones, wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the poetic preface to Martin Delrio's "Disquisitions on Magic." It follows the title page and features a commendatory poem by the famous humanist scholar Justus Lipsius.</meta>

<image-desc>A rectangular decorative woodcut headpiece featuring a central human mask or face flanked by symmetrical scrolling floral and foliate motifs.</image-desc>

->TO THE REVEREND FATHER<-

# MARTIN DELRIO’S
# INVESTIGATIONS
## INTO MAGIC,
### A Poem by Justus Lipsius.

<image-desc>An ornamental drop cap letter 'C' decorated with intricate foliate and vine patterns.</image-desc>

*Whose learned work is this? Which, with its wing,*
*Traverses the depths and the heights, and with a keen mind*
*Reveals the wise-women <note>The Latin "Sagas" refers to female fortune-tellers or hags</note> and the witches of the Styx?*
*Whatever you, O land of Thessaly <note>Thessaly was anciently famed as the home of powerful witches</note>, and you, O Colchians,*
*Have wickedly performed with your infamous incantations;*
*Whatever the Pans, the Satyrs, and the Dusii <note>The "Dusii" were Celtic forest demons or incubi mentioned by St. Augustine</note>*
*Have committed—that wicked and lustful race—*
*All this he unearths, and for what is unearthed, he provides a cure.*
*But who is he? DELRIO, renowned for his erudition*
*And likewise for his piety. Therefore,*
*Here all things are pure and clear; here there is no poison*
*Of false opinions that you need fear.*
*Show favor to this book, and thereby favor yourself,*
*And read it, so that you may know the depths and the heights.*

<image-desc>A triangular decorative woodcut tailpiece featuring complex scrolling arabesque and foliate designs tapering to a point at the bottom.</image-desc>

<summary>This page contains a laudatory poem by the scholar Justus Lipsius praising Martin Delrio's comprehensive study of magic, witchcraft, and demons. Lipsius emphasizes that the work is both intellectually profound and religiously safe for the reader.</summary>
<keywords>Martin Delrio, Justus Lipsius, magic, demonology, witchcraft, Thessaly, Colchis, Satyrs, Dusii, Counter-Reformation scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>The catchword "ΦΕΔΕ-" (PHEDE-) suggests a continuation from a previous Greek or Latin invocation, likely referencing "Faith" or "Federico." This page serves as a poetic introductory parody for the following work on magic.</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut headpiece featuring a central mascaron (human-like face) flanked by symmetrical flourishes of acanthus leaves and scrolls.</image-desc>

# A PARODY ON THE SAME SUBJECT.
<note>The title "In Easdem Parodia" indicates this poem is a stylistic imitation or "parody" of a classical model—likely Catullus 4—repurposed to introduce a treatise on magic.</note>

MAGIC, *which you see held hostage in this book,*
*Claims to have been a treasury of frauds,*
*And asserts that no healer’s art or glory*
*Could ever prevail against her, whether there was*
*A need to kill by poison, or by an evil spell.*
*And she says that the fertile fields of* THESSALY <note>Thessaly was anciently famed as the land of witches and sorcery.</note>
*Do not deny this, nor the noxious* CAUCASUS,
*The rocks of* SCYTHIA, *the complicit* COLCHIANS,
IBERIA, *and your summit, Mount* TAURUS:
*Where that wild herb first flourished,*
*After* MAGIC; *for on the ridge of* PROMETHEUS
*She often soaked the crops in wasting vapor.*
*O* ZARATE <note>Zoroaster, the legendary Persian founder of the Magi.</note> *the Mede, and deadly* DARDANUS; <note>A mythical figure often associated with the transmission of magical mysteries.</note>
*To you, these things were and are most well-known,*
*Says* MAGIC; *from a late-born origin,*
*She says she issued from your lineage,*
*And plowed her treacherous signs with your pen;*
*And from there, through so many hands of rival deceit,*
*She bore her "honor," whether she performed the work*
*With the left hand or the right, or if she had*
*Diligentely given both roles in turn to her master.*
*Nor were any vows to the* HARMFUL GODS
*Made by her, when she came from that most*
*Recent man down to this present age.*
*But these things were in the past: now, while* APOLLO <note>Apollo is the god of light, truth, and healing; his "speaking" represents the light of reason and true religion dispelling magical shadows.</note>
*Speaks, she is silent, and dedicates herself to you,*
APOLLO DELRIO, <note>A complimentary reference to Martin Delrio, the author of the treatise, likening his wisdom to that of the god Apollo.</note> *and to the* APOLLO OF DELRIO.

->HERIBERT ROSWEYDE.<- <note>Rosweyde (1569–1629) was a Jesuit hagiographer and scholar.</note>

<summary>This page contains a Latin poem by Heribert Rosweyde written in imitation of Catullus, personifying Magic as a reformed captive who now submits to the authority of the scholar Martin Delrio. It traces the legendary dark history of magic from ancient Persia and the Caucasus to the present day.</summary>
<keywords>Magic, Martin Delrio, Heribert Rosweyde, Thessaly, Zoroaster, Prometheus, mythology, Jesuit scholarship, Neo-Latin poetry</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page contains a commendatory poem by Federico Jamot, a physician, praising Martin Delrio’s monumental work on magic. The text is presented in parallel Greek and Latin verse, both of which are translated here into English prose that captures the poetic elevation of the original.</meta>

# FEDERICO JAMOT,
## PHYSICIAN, A POEM FOR
### the book of Father Martin Delrio <note>Martin Delrio (1551–1608) was a Jesuit theologian whose "Disquisitionum Magicarum Libri Sex" became the definitive early modern counter-magic manual.</note>
### concerning Magic.

---

In ancient times, those men who won high-minded honor and a name for heaven-built Magic, were preeminent in knowledge and wisdom, and they joined a cooperative foresight to their understanding hearts. Being performers of sacred rites and counselors to Kings, they were blameless trackers of Nature, skilled in the stars, servants of the Muses and of the noble Art of Healing <note>original Greek: *Akestories*; Latin: *Medicas*. Refers to the healing arts.</note>. Such a one was Joseph among foreign tribes; such was Moses, who showed many-turning wonders to the people of Pharaoh; and good Daniel in the Assyrian city; and Solomon, glowing like fire, overseeing the Jews. Such again were the Magi who traveled by the stars to Christ, who was called to a humble cradle on the ground.

After these, the sting of a vengeful Demon <note>The poem shifts here from "natural/divine magic" to "black magic" or sorcery.</note>
->driving<-
wretched men to be subdued by their own recklessness, let loose the deeds of the impious. He devised the many sorrows of all-working Sorcery, and boundless waters of ruin, folly, potions, and most hateful incantations. From those men of old arose the leaders of magical Egypt, and the prophetic Old Woman <note>The Witch of Endor.</note> who spoke the oracle to Saul when he questioned her. And wise Medea, the evil scion of Aeetes. And lawless Simon, who received the surname of "the Magician," and Carpocrates, who taught his hateful art openly, trusting in impudence.

But now the evil-minded blight of Magicians and deceitful Sorcerers—the mournful burdens of the earth—wanders among men, straying in a total mass by the will of the ruling God; who, justly angered by mortal sins, casts a black-winged darkness into the hearts of men and a mist-walking cloud of unproven Magic. And He grants a debt-paying retribution to those who, having turned their blameless faith over to the storm-winds, are yoked to thin and impious lies. And indeed, a dark-veiled mist would have covered the whole earth and obscured the character of many-starred men, had not the noble Delrio, a healer with his medicinal book, pushed away the unutterable gloom and brought the light-bringing radiance of Truth to all.

For he has sealed the types, forms, and all the causes of Magic—of both kinds <note>Referring to the distinction between "natural" and "demonic" magic.</note>—on each side of his famous tablet. Distinguishing lies from the firm word of truth, he has torn up the radiance of misleading error from the roots. O, great is the daring man who, with tireless intentions, bringing more sight to his invincible mind, [challenges the monsters...]

---

<meta>The Latin text below is a contemporary translation/paraphrase of the Greek text above, following the same themes of the history of magic from the Patriarchs to the corruptions of Simon Magus, ending in Delrio's scholarly intervention.</meta>

In ancient times, whoever bore the great glory and name of celestial Magic were renowned for the praise of illustrious Wisdom and learning. They joined cultivated hearts to a skillful mind: once celebrated sacrificers, second only to the King, searchers of Nature, and experts in the stars, cultivating the distinguished Medical arts and the Muses. Such was Joseph, a foster-son of a foreign nation; such was Moses, bringing forth oracles among the people of Pharaoh; such also was Daniel, a sight to behold in the Assyrian city. Such was Solomon, while he held the royal scepter as a guide; and such were the Magi, who sought the Christ lying in a vile cradle from the rising of the Sun.

Meanwhile, the
->sting<-
of a hostile Demon, agitating wretched mortals whom their own senselessness tormented, sent forth crime and frauds, and devised various crafts of hurting and deceiving, and various arts: the prophecies of false-speaking seers, potions, and chants. From here the plague arose; from here the ancient age of our parents brought forth the Priests of raving Egypt, and the fate-telling Old Woman who gave grim responses to Saul. From here came wise Medea, the dire offspring of Aeetes; from here impious Simon, also called "the Magician" by surname; from here also Carpocrates, who, as a public interpreter, handed down the precepts of the Magical art, having cast aside shame.

Now the Magical pests and impostures, the shameful weights of the earth, stalk through the whole world and mock men's minds: but not without the will of the Heavenly Father, who, rightly offended by evils, pours out thick darkness upon human minds, and suddenly binds their eyes with the black cloud of Magic. He exacts well-deserved punishments from men for their crime—both those who left the holy faith for light breezes, and those who submit their impious necks to the yoke of falsehood. And now indeed, a dusky gloom surrounded by a dark headband would overwhelm the human race and the wide lands, were it not for the good Delrio, esteemed in the Medical art, who dispels the shadows with the medicine of a healthy book, and bathes all things in the clear light of truth. For having attempted to mark all the species, forms, and causes of twofold Magic upon a learned tablet, strenuous and simple in distinguishing the true from the false, he has utterly overturned the harms which error brought in. O, man of exceeding boldness, who with a brave heart and arms, provoking the pests of the lands, the immense monsters...

<summary>A commendatory poem by Federico Jamot praising Martin Delrio's work on magic. It traces the history of "good" magic (Joseph, Moses, Solomon) versus "evil" sorcery (Medea, Simon Magus) and positions Delrio's book as a medicinal cure for the world's current delusions.</summary>
<keywords>Martin Delrio, Federico Jamot, history of magic, Moses, Solomon, Magi, Simon Magus, Medea, counter-reformation, demonology</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page contains a bilingual panegyric (poem of praise) honoring prominent Jesuit scholars. The Greek and Latin texts are parallel versions of the same poem. I have translated the sense of the verses into English, maintaining the elevated, classical tone.</meta>

# POEM

->You subdue impious hearts, imitating the brave Hercules <note>original: "son of Zeus/Jupiter the averter of evil"</note> through your fair deeds and following his example. From this, a wise and prudent man might easily discern that a natural strength of mind dwells within your companionship—a spirit that looks toward rugged heights and, through much-toiling labor, pursues eternal works.<-

->But let me name the multitude of God-worthy priests who take their name from the graceful **JESUS**; they who, through their manly deeds, shine preeminent above all in their steadfastness. O **Calliope** <note>The Muse of epic poetry</note>, you reveal each one; for you know the ranks of those many-starred men, whether they dwell toward the dawn (East), the evening (West), or the midday sun (South), and what great wonders they have achieved through their toil. We hear only the rumor of their fame, not knowing it clearly, for we have not reached the furthest boundaries of the world. But you, O Goddess who knows all, grant these pleasing gifts of song to the noble Fathers.<-

->**Bellarmine** <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a central figure of the Counter-Reformation</note> claims the first honors from the Muses, a hero among the purple-clad leaders. From his invincible quiver he has loosed a number of arrows, swift and vocal to those of an enemy mind; quelling divided strife, removing contention and the wavering tumult concerning true worship and holy law. He did not cease to "move every stone" and "every rope" <note>Classical idioms for leaving no stone unturned/exerting every effort</note> until he rendered these matters undisputed for the learned.<-

->Nor was the labor of **Maldonado** <note>Juan Maldonado (1533–1583), Spanish Jesuit exegete</note>, the flower of the Iberian land, any less; he worked so that the echoing message of Christ the All-King—which the four God-inspired prophets <note>The Evangelists</note> sealed in their four-fold book—might be unfolded to all, becoming clearly known to the citizens of Paris and to foreigners alike.<-

->So too **Pererius** <note>Benito Pereira (1535–1610)</note>, stirred by a Pierian sting <note>Inspiration from the Muses</note>, drove away the shadowy mist and murky gloom from the prophetic **Daniel**; with clear words, he laid open the hidden mysteries of the Light.<-

->The great might and divine strength of **Torres** <note>Francisco Torres (c. 1509–1584), Latinized as Turrianus</note>, fighting on behalf of the High Priest's <note>The Pope's</note> divine right and the canons, urged him to appear in the midst of the strife with a bold heart. For this great-souled man, he built a high tower of virtues to ascend, that he might be more visible from there.<-

->How great was the struggle, how great the force of **Possevino** <note>Antonio Possevino (1533–1611), bibliographer and diplomat</note>! Sitting like a judge in the manner of **Aristarchus** <note>A famous critical scholar of antiquity</note>, he marked the dense records of men in his wise and infinite book.<-

->How great was the work of **Peltanus** <note>Theodor Peltanus (1511–1584)</note>, bringing the Latin voice to the pages of the original Greek Fathers! How great was **Ribera** <note>Francisco de Ribera (1537–1591)</note>, when he explained in his own discourse the venerable oracles of the prophets whom they call the "Minor" ones! O, with how much strength did the renowned **Scondoch** <note>Likely a reference to a Jesuit scholar such as G. Sconduto</note> rely...<-

<summary>A bilingual (Greek and Latin) poem praising the intellectual and polemical achievements of famous 16th-century Jesuit scholars, comparing their work to the labors of Hercules and the inspiration of the Muses.</summary>
<keywords>Jesuits, Counter-Reformation, Robert Bellarmine, Juan Maldonado, Biblical exegesis, scholarship, Hagiography, Classical allusion</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page contains commendatory verses (poems of praise) written in both Greek and Latin, likely serving as a preface to a scholarly or theological work. The verses celebrate the author and his Jesuit colleagues, emphasizing their defense of the faith and their classical erudition.</meta>

# ON THE AUTHOR’S BOOK.

| Greek | Latin |
|-------|-------|
| He has warded off from his companions the malice and the slander of those men <note>original: "Ἐργολάϐων" - literally "contractors" or "hired laborers," here used as a slur for those working against the faith</note> who love to insult, surrounding the Roman <note>original: "Αὔζονα" - Ausonian/Italian, meaning Latin</note> beauty of the wise Coster with his own writings, following closely in his footsteps. | He has driven away hatred from his companions, and silenced the bold tongues and false insults of the informers. He has gifted the learned monuments of Coster with a refined Latin style, and as a companion, he has followed in his very footsteps. |
| Who could praise Molina and Suarez in a worthy song? Who could recount the deeds of the wise Vazquez as they deserve, or the famous Valencia? Indeed, look upon the white hair of Servius, and the elder Christides, the teacher of two youths: one of whom adorned Saint-Omer <note>original: "Audomaros"</note>, and the other the noble city of Douai <note>original: "Duaci"</note> with the flowing streams of their eloquent tongues, openly rebuking sinful men in the marketplaces. | Who could extol Molina and the eloquent Suarez with a worthy song? Who has adorned the brave deeds of Vazquez with earned praise, and the famous Valencia? Now look around at the gray hair of Servius, and the elder Christides: to whom the care of the youths was entrusted. One of these graced Saint-Omer, the other the walls of Douai with a river of eloquent speech, openly and everywhere condemning detestable sins. |
| To him as he labored, his dear friend Dausqueius stretched out a helping hand; he who is cherished by the Muses, speaks the joined doctrines of Greece and Rome. He, gazing upon the shining monuments of King Seleucus <note>A reference to the author's work on the writings of Saint Basil of Seleucia</note>, explained them with wise pages, bringing forth into the light whatever was divine from the darkness. | Here the helper stood by and reached out his right hand to his friend in their shared labor: Dausqueius, the greatest care of the Muses, a teacher of the Latin and Greek Muse. He explained the splendid monuments of Basil of Seleucia in learned papers: which, rescued from blind hiding places, he shall immediately bring forth into the nourishing light. |
| Just as Veruecius stood by to help Christides, who governs the reins of the Castalian house <note>A poetic reference to a school or college, Castalia being the spring of the Muses</note> with just laws at Saint-Omer; the leaders placed him as the mourning-loving <note>The Greek "φιλοπενθέα" is likely a pun or specific reference to a somber religious order</note> ruler of youth, whom he leads like a bellwether <note>original: "veruex" - a wether/ram, a pun on the name Veruecius</note> on the path toward the stream of wisdom and the sweet pasture. | So also Veruecius, standing by, assists the elder Christides at the house of Saint-Omer, governing with the equal laws of the Castalian spring. The directors placed him over the teachable youth; he goes before them like a bellwether of the young, and leads them to the fountains of Wisdom and sweet pastures. |
| There are countless others at the ends of the whole earth (may they be favorable and peaceful toward my song!) companions excelling in the splendors of the Order <note>The Society of Jesus (Jesuits)</note>. But let us apply a limit and measure to human thoughts; for no man could grasp these things, as the mind and strength of mortal men is small. | There are others, innumerable in various borders of the world (may the Muses be present, favorable and calm toward my work), companions of the Order famous for their exceptional virtue. But let us add a certain end and measure to human minds; for mortals, who have such small skill of mind, cannot comprehend these men. |
| But you, stout-hearted Delrio, increasing the battle against the ignorant and beastly men with the undaunted impulse of your mind, have gathered ageless glory by your wondrous deeds. Continue to end the strife, bringing true help and certain defense to the Truth which is being fought against; until she takes the victory, and evil Deceit, lifting both hands in surrender and despairing of victory, is brought low. Then the Belgian soil would flourish with truth and celebrate you with everlasting songs. | But you, Delrio, with undaunted and constant force, waging sharp wars against the savage race of beasts, have won immortal glory by your marvelous daring. Proceed now to finish the war with a distinguished triumph: bring help to the Truth, and protect her when she is attacked in war. Until she carries back the praise of the palm of victory; while on the other hand, crafty Deceit, troubling the world with ruinous arts, gives her conquered hands to the victor and leaves the palm behind. Then the land of the Belgians will flourish with truth and justice, and will celebrate you in song for all eternity. |

### ON THE SAME AUTHOR
# IAMBIC TRIMETERS.

> *The Underworld <note>original: "Orcus"</note> dared, through its own demonic art,*
> *And through monstrous magic, to turn the World from its axis:*
> *The World groaned, conquered by the deceit of Hell <note>original: "Tartari"</note>,*
> *And wasted away, sickened by evil incantations.*
> *What is this? Now the Underworld groans, and the turn is reversed;*
> *The World rejoices, freed from the frauds of the Styx <note>the river of the underworld</note>.*
> *Is there even a place for Fate <note>original: "Sorti"</note> in this third kingdom?*
> *No. This is the virtue of a man—but of what man?*
> *DELRIO. A name well known in the Belgian schools,*
> *And known to the Spaniards <note>original: "Iberis"</note>. Do you wish for more? Read the book.*

->JOHN GAUDA.<-

## APPROVAL OF THE SUPERIORS.

I, Oliver Manare <note>Oliverius Manareus (1523–1614), a prominent Jesuit official</note>, Vice-Provincial of the Society of Jesus <note>the Jesuits</note> for the Belgian province; by the power granted to me by the Very Reverend Father Claudio Aquaviva, Superior General of the same Society; grant to Father Martin Delrio, Priest of our Society, the faculty of publishing for the public these six books of *Disquisitions on Magic*; which have been approved by the judgment of grave and learned Theologians: in witness of which permission, I have signed my name to this short document. Given at Liège <note>original: "Leodij"</note>, on the 6th day of the month of July, in the year 1598.

->Oliver Manare.<-

## APPROVAL OF THE CENSOR.

I judge these first books of the *Disquisitions on Magic*, written by the Reverend Father Martin Delrio, Theologian of the Society of Jesus, to be worthy of being sent to the press, because they are filled with manifold and varied erudition, and contain nothing that is contrary to the Catholic faith. Given at Leuven <note>original: "Louany"</note>, February 8th, in the year of our Lord 1599.

->William Fabricius of Nijmegen <note>Guilielmus Fabricius Nouiomagus</note>,<-
->Apostolic and Royal Censor of Books.<-

<meta>This page contains the formal Imperial Privilege (copyright protection) granted by Emperor Rudolf II to the author Martin Del Rio. It follows the title page and establishes the legal authority to print the work.</meta>

# PRIVILEGE.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut drop-cap letter 'R' featuring intricate foliate and floral scrolling patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

RUDOLF the Second, by the favoring divine mercy Elected
Emperor of the Romans, forever August, and King of Germany,
Hungary, Bohemia, Dalmatia, Croatia, Slavonia, etc., Arch-
duke of Austria, Duke of Burgundy, Styria, Carinthia, Carniola,
and Württemberg, etc., Count of Tyrol, etc. We make it known by the
tenor of these presents to all. It has been set forth to us that Martin Del
Rio, a priest of the Society of Jesus <note>The Jesuits</note>, has labored for many years—both formerly when he was a member of the Council in Belgium for the most Serene Catholic King of Spain, Philip the Second, and later when, having joined the Society of Jesus, he devoted himself to the public benefit in various Academies by preaching, teaching, and composing to the best of his ability—in the liberal arts, Jurisprudence, and Theology. These works he has partly published, and partly holds ready or in preparation to be published, or desires to have reprinted with further revisions. He fears, however, as he has already experienced not without some cost to his reputation and his readers, that these works might be printed by others to the fraud of himself and his associates by those for whom it is least expedient; and therefore he humbly asks that we fortify him with our Privilege against such injury and fraud. We, therefore, kindly considering how useful a service to the public literary world is performed by Martin Del Rio himself—who is commended to us for his distinguished piety and learning, as is evident in those things already published by him—do seriously and firmly forbid and inhibit all and singular Printers, Booksellers, and others exercising any book-trading business, that no one presume to reprint the aforementioned works for a period of twenty years, calculated from the first day of publication, within the borders of the Holy Roman Empire and our hereditary Kingdoms and Dominions, in similar or other type or form, whether in whole or in part, or to send them elsewhere to be reprinted, or to bring in, sell, and distribute them from elsewhere even if already printed, secretly or openly, against the will and consent of the aforementioned Martin Del Rio. If anyone, however, by doing otherwise, should attempt to neglect or transgress this our Imperial Privilege, we decree that he shall not only be deprived in fact of the aforementioned books (which the previously named Del Rio or his agents may claim for themselves wherever discovered, either by their own authority or with the help and aid of the local Magistrate) as being wrongly reprinted and imported, but shall also be punished irremissibly by a fine of ten Marks of pure gold, to be paid in equal parts to our Imperial Treasury, the avenger of fraud, and for the use of the often-mentioned Del Rio or his agents. We therefore command all and singular subjects of ours and of the Holy Roman Empire and of our hereditary Kingdoms and Jurisdictions, of whatever state, grade, order, condition, or dignity they may be, both Ecclesiastical and Secular, and especially those who, being established in the Magistracy, exercise the administration of Law and Justice in their own name or that of their superiors, that they do not allow anyone to rashly and with impunity transgress or violate this our Imperial Privilege, but rather see to it that the defiant, if any should be found, are punished with the prescribed penalty and restrained by other suitable means, insofar as they themselves wish to avoid the same fine. In testimony of these letters signed by our hand and fortified by the impression of our Imperial seal. Given in our Royal Castle at Prague on the fifteenth day of the month of June, in the Year of our Lord One Thousand six hundred and two, in the twenty-seventh year of our Roman reign, the thirtieth of our Hungarian, and likewise the twenty-seventh of our Bohemian, etc.

->**RUDOLF**<-

->By the Personal Command of his Sacred Imperial Majesty<-

Rudolf Coraducius, etc.
->Johann Barvitius, etc.<-

<summary>This document is a formal legal privilege granted by Emperor Rudolf II to the Jesuit scholar Martin Del Rio, granting him exclusive printing rights for twenty years. It threatens unauthorized printers with the confiscation of books and a heavy fine in gold.</summary>
<keywords>Rudolf II, Martin Del Rio, Jesuit, printing privilege, copyright law, Prague, Holy Roman Empire, book trade, 1602</keywords>

## MOST DISTINGUISHED SIR.

->THE PEACE OF CHRIST.<-

*The letter you sent to me on the eighteenth of January, Johannes Albinus, I received on the seventeenth of February. It was distressing to learn that you are being troubled by the boasting of vain men. Be of good cheer; these ghosts are not to be feared. They are the* **BOGEYMEN** <note>original: "MORMOLYCIA" — a Greek term for hobgoblins or terrifying phantoms used to frighten children</note> *of an evil spirit who pretends he is being attacked.* **TRUTH** *will eventually prevail over falsehood. I immediately wrote to Prague, to those by whose efforts I obtained the* **IMPERIAL DIPLOMA** <note>A formal charter or decree granting legal privileges or rights</note>, *so that they may oppose these "Lizards" <note>Stellionibus: a legal term for swindlers or those guilty of fraud</note> and drench them with the heat they deserve. The memory of the Privilege granted to me is fresh; I have no fear that these lurkers will creep in or steal in unnoticed; they will find all doors closed. You, for your part, keep silent and press on with what you have begun. To you alone do I permit the printing of the* **INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC**, *enlarged with such a splendid and improved supplement. As for all other printers who acknowledge the* **MAJESTY OF THE AUGUST EMPIRE**, *I forbid and prohibit them, by the power granted to me by our* **MOST INVINCIBLE EMPEROR**, *from touching my work without my knowledge, whether by printing, reprinting, or offering for sale copies printed elsewhere. I urge them not to make a game of this, for they shall pay a heavy price in coin. To you also, because you print my* **INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC** *as enlarged and corrected by me, I give and delegate—as in my own personal cause and by the best right I can—a* **SPECIAL MANDATE AND POWER OF ATTORNEY**; *to demand and pursue, wherever it may be convenient, the confiscation of copies and the collection and receipt of the fine of many marks of gold, as expressed in the diploma. Indeed, I inform all* **READERS** *that I do not consider as my true offspring those editions which either Johann Philipp Steiffius or Johann Theobald Schönwetter (whom you write are attempting such things), or anyone else besides you, may have printed—not only without my knowledge but against my will—by a bad example, with worse faith, by the rite of plagiarism, or with different type or illustrations. And, if they should forge any Privilege, I publicly denounce, declare, and attest in this letter to you that it will be fraudulent, stolen, or even subject to the Cornelian Law <note>Lex Cornelia: a Roman law concerning forgery and falsification</note>. Let them see to it that while they hunt for false and uncertain profit through injury, they do not rightfully fall into a true and certain loss of reputation and money. I permit you to join this indicator of my mind and will to the Imperial Diploma, and I bid you to expect other things from me shortly. Be of high spirit, and do not fear these flies, but brush them away with the fan of speed and constancy. I shall not be lacking, and I will strenuously defend my favor to you as far as I am able. Farewell. Graz, Styria. 17 February 1603.*

->Yours from the heart<-
->**MARTIN DELRIO, OF THE**<-
->**SOCIETY OF JESUS, etc.**<-

**BOOK III.** *On Evil Spells <note>Maleficio</note> and vain observation.*
**BOOK IV.** *On Prophecy, Divination, and Guessing.*
**BOOK V.** *On the office of the Judge and the judicial order in this crime.*
**BOOK VI.** *On the office of the Confessor, and on lawful and unlawful remedies.*

<margin>A</margin> The pride, hatred, and deceits of demons.

### BOOK I. ON MAGIC.

#### Chapter I. On superstition and its types.

<margin>A</margin> The etymology of "superstition" according to Isidore <note>St. Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century encyclopedist</note>, and his explanation of the term.
<margin>B</margin> Superstition is understood in a negative sense by Christians. The passage in Acts 17, verse 22 explained.
Superstition and religion differ, contrary to the Epicureans. A passage of Cicero corrected, and another from Nonius. A saying of Lactantius discussed.
<margin>C</margin> What religion is, which vices are contrary to it, and how there can be "excess" in religion.
<margin>D</margin> Superstition as false worship; the same as idolatry, which is twofold: "silent" (implied) and "expressed." Superstition as undue worship. The passage in 1 Chronicles 16, verse 26 explained, regarding <term>Elil</term> <note>original Hebrew: אליל; refers to "worthless idols" or "nothings"</note>.

#### Chapter II. On the definition and division of magic, and the various names for Magicians.

<margin>A</margin> What magic is in general. It is divided in two ways: by its efficient cause and by its final purpose.
<margin>B</margin> By cause: into Natural, Artificial, and Demonic; by purpose: into good and evil.
<margin>C</margin> A poor definition, and four species. Special magic. Divination. Sorcery <note>original: "Maleficium"</note>.
<margin>D</margin> What "vain observance" is. The various names of Magicians from Holy Scripture. <term>Ob</term> and <term>Yidde'oni</term> <note>original Hebrew: אוב ידעוני</note>, a ventriloquist or <term>engastrimythos</term> <note>original Greek: ἐγγαστρίμυθος; one who speaks from the belly, often associated with mediumship</note>. <note>The text lists several Hebrew terms for sorcerers, charmers, and soothsayers: Chartummim, Mekhashephim, Kesheph, Nachash, Qesem, Onen, Chober, Lachash.</note>
The passage in Exodus 22, verse 18: what is meant by "sorcerers" there? Another in 2 Kings 9, verse 22: Jezebel the sorceress, who infected the Samaritans with this evil. "Samaritan" was often used as a term for an enchanter. The passage in John 8, verses 48 and 49. What <term>Pharmakeus</term> <note>original Greek: Φαρμακεύς; a mixer of potions or poisons</note> signifies. Names used by the Greek Septuagint translators: <term>epoidos</term> (enchanter), <term>mantis</term> (diviner), <term>pharmakos</term> (poisoner/sorcerer), <term>egoes</term> (warlock/howler). Latin names: Magi, poisoners, Thessalians, Chaldean Augurs, Soothsayers, Diviners, Astrologers, Lamiæ, Witches <note>original: "Stryges"</note>, Mascæ, Enchantresses, Wise-women, Fortune-tellers, Lot-casters.

---

#### Chapter III. On natural or physical Magic.

<margin>A</margin> The twofold Magic of the Persians: natural and superstitious. What is the beginning or origin of each?
<margin>B</margin> The definition and examples of natural magic. What kind of magic was used by Solomon, the Three Kings (Magi), and Apollonius. The division of natural magic.

<margin>A judgment on the writings published in this genre: which are permitted, which are forbidden, etc.</margin>

Cardan *On Subtlety and Variety*. Giambattista della Porta, and Antonio Mizaldi’s *Natural Magic*. Certain books of Albertus Magnus. Pomponazzi *On Incantations*. Cecco d'Ascoli on the *Sphere of Sacrobosco*. Giovanni Francesco Pico’s *Magica*. The books of Jean Bodin.

#### Question I. What do the aspect and influence of the heavens contribute to Magical effects?

<margin>A</margin> The foundation of magicians borrowed from astrology, concerning celestial images and their sympathy with lower things, refuted; a response to their arguments.
<margin>B</margin> Their trifles and nonsense detected from their own positions.
<margin>C</margin> The heavens and stars are devoid of souls. A rule for understanding various passages of Holy Scripture which seem to attribute a soul to them.
<margin>D</margin> Whether there is any influence, and of what kind.
<margin>E</margin> What the heavens and stars can do to these lower things. Concerning "Critical Days" <note>astrological days believed to determine the turning point of a disease</note>.

*The article on this matter confirmed by Paris.* <note>Likely referring to the University of Paris (the Sorbonne), a major authority in theological censorship.</note> F

### Question II. Can magic effects of this kind, similar to miracles, arise from a person's natural physical temperament?

*Whether all material things obey the human soul? This is denied, against Avicenna and Pomponazzi.* <note>Pietro Pomponazzi (1462–1525) was a philosopher who sought naturalistic explanations for miracles.</note> *Neither does this come from a "balanced temperament," against certain physicians. No one is born a physician or a "fascinator" (one who casts spells).* A B

### Question III. How great and of what kind is the power of imagination regarding such effects?

*It has the most power over one's own body.* A
*Sleepwalking caused by these things. A wonderful example. What factors must concur for this.*
*Something can occasionally affect a nearby and predisposed body. However, it can do nothing at all to a distant or unprepared body, contrary to many opinions. Imagination cannot move another from their place, heal, or bewitch, nor can it stir up storms or rain. The passage from Calpurnius explained. The passage of Genesis 30.* <note>Refers to Jacob’s striped rods affecting the offspring of the flock.</note> *Concerning the variety of offspring due to imagination either before or after conception. Various examples: And whether the imagination of the other parent is sufficient. How white peacocks are made. A passage from Saint Augustine.* B C D

### Question IV. Can wounds or diseases be healed, and other such wonders be performed naturally, by touch alone, by breath, sight, voice, a kiss, or the application of a bare linen cloth?

*The foundation of those who affirm this. But argued to the contrary: This is not to be attributed to the voice alone. Concerning the bull falling and standing. Concerning the curses of parents. The example of the Westphalian woman rendering a thread immobile by her voice. Nor can sight do this, even with imagination. Vision happens by receiving, not by emitting.* <note>The author supports the "intromission" theory of sight over the "extramission" theory (light coming out of the eyes).</note> *How love is born from a glance; likewise bleary eyes. The infection of mirrors by the gaze of a menstruating woman. How the gaze of old women harms children. How the "galgalus" bird heals jaundice. How wolves cause hoarseness. Whether the ostrich and the tortoise hatch eggs by sight. Why the lion fears the rooster. Concerning the "Zaburiz" of Spain, or the "Lynxes" (sharp-sighted people). Nor is touch alone sufficient for this.* A B C D

---

*Various experiences are explained, concerning:*
*the sting of the cramp-fish (torpedo). the shadow of the hyena. the snake and the oak leaf, or the feather of the ibis. the viper and the beech twig. the bat and the plane-tree leaves. the goat and the eryngo plant. the feathers of the eagle. the sea-hare. strings made of wolf-gut and lamb-gut.* <note>Refers to the folk belief that wolf-gut strings on a lute would destroy the sound of lamb-gut strings.</note>

*Concerning the Echeneis, or remora fish.* <note>A fish believed to be able to stop ships by clinging to them.</note> *One string provoking the ringing of another lute. Concerning the magnet and iron; the bull tied to a fig tree. Concerning the girl nourished on monkshood (poison). Concerning the corpse bleeding in the presence of the killer.* <note>The "cruentation" ordeal.</note> *A passage of Lucretius explained. The foundation of Pomponazzi. The gift of healing is a gift of God and a "grace freely given." Concerning the seventh male child. Concerning the charisma of the Kings of England. Whether Elizabeth has this gift.* <note>Refers to "The King's Evil," the belief that monarchs could heal scrofula by touch.</note> *Tooker’s book discussed: here also pertain the "Friday children" in Belgium. Concerning the Kings of France and England. Concerning King Pyrrhus. Some have occasionally healed by touch. The "Saludadores" (healers) of Spain discussed. A passage of Navarro explained. The foundations of Veracruz discussed. The lies and superstitions of the healers. Concerning the herb "Pigi" and similar things. The foundation of Pomponazzi is overturned. Concerning the Gentiles. Saint Paul. And the military cure falsely ascribed to Saint Anselm. Concerning the two boys of Albertus Magnus. Concerning the Psylli and Marsi (snake-charmers). A passage of Alchimus.* E F G H

## Chapter IV. On Artificial Magic.

*This magic is partly divinatory, partly operative. Operative magic is partly Mathematical, partly Prestigiatory (illusion-based). Various examples and authors of Mathematical magic. The bronze head of Albertus Magnus did not speak, contrary to William of Paris and others. Many examples of Prestigiatory magic. When operative magic is illicit, and when it is natural.* A B C

### Question I. Is there a power in characters, rings, seals, and images, such as the magi contend?

*Various opinions. Three kinds of images, and the power of each. Concerning seals and characters. No power exists in any of these. The seals of Hermes, and the book of Lodovico Dolce noted. The foundations of Cajetan and Pomponazzi are undermined. Whether a shape has any power formally, or as a shape.* A B C D

### Question II. Is there any power in magic numbers?

*How the Fathers philosophized about numbers. The negative view is defended. Concerning the climacteric year—* <note>original: "anno climacterico," a year believed to be a critical turning point in life, often multiples of 7 or 9.</note> A

the <term>climacteric</term> <note>a critical period in life, often thought to occur in multiples of seven or nine</note> and births in the eighth month. Concerning the herb Cinquefoil <note>original: "Pentaphylli"</note>, the seeds of the heliotrope, concerning ligatures <note>magical bindings or knots</note>, concerning pills given in unequal numbers <margin>C</margin>, and concerning musical numbers. In what sense the magi speak of number. Peter Gregory is rebuked, and the Church Fathers defended. The book of Fabio Paolini on the number seven. A very extensive discussion on the power of the music of Orpheus, and Platonic delusions refuted; a passage from Macrobius. The Magnet. Pandantae, Androdamas <note>ancient names for specific stones or minerals</note>. The Automata of Archduke Ferdinand II. The infection of mirrors. The Echo. Selenite, Theriac <note>a famous medical concoction used as an antidote</note>. A rule of Marsilio Ficino noted, and the bite and cure of the Tarantula.

### Q. III. Is there power in words and incantations for healing diseases, or for performing other miraculous feats?

<margin>A</margin> *Various errors and their foundations. The true opinion is explained and proven.* <margin>B</margin> *Concerning the cup of Jealousy. In how many ways natural operation may be understood, explained at length and clearly. The natural, miraculous, and preternatural (or superstitious) order of things: how they differ; likewise what the artificial* <margin>C</margin> *order is, and how much it can achieve. The error of Spondanus. The futile foundations of Pomponazzi. The sayings of Ferrerio, Pico, Peter Gregory, and De Thou discussed. The foolish foundations of the magi;* <margin>D</margin> *Ananias incautiously wrote certain things on this matter. No sympathy exists between celestial things and words. No greater power exists in certain words or names, as such, than in others. Names are not participants in life, nor are they rays of the things they signify. How God calls the names of things in Psalm 146, verse 4. How the names of certain people are written in heaven, Luke 10, verse 19. Names do not signify through celestial influence. There is no greater power in complex speech* <margin>F</margin> *than in simple speech; the arguments of Agrippa are dissolved. The various and vain circumstances which they add.* <margin>G</margin>

### Q. IV. Concerning amulets and charms worn on the body.

<margin>A</margin> *The amulets of the Romans; safeguards against the evil eye. Also the Phylacteries of the Greeks and Jews. Which are permitted to Christians, and which are not.* <margin>B</margin> *Cirnelus noted. What their power is. Concerning Spanish Jet usually hung on children.* <margin>D</margin> *Basil. Incertus, Chrysostom, and Jerome explained. Other matters. The superstitious medals of Magno. Examples of St. Simeon Salus and Lord* <margin>C</margin> *Antoninus. Chapters 1 and 4, question 1 of the 26th distinction are explained. A prayer falsely attributed to Pope Leo. Heretics used these false charms. False things in which there are vain names of angels which Scripture does not know: and the decree of the Roman Council regarding these under Pope Zachary against the heretic Aldebert.* <margin>D</margin>

### Chap. V. The goldsmith's art, which they call Alchemy: to which species of Magic should it be referred?

#### Q. 1. Can true gold be made by this art?

#### Sect. I. What is it, and when was this art invented?

<margin>A</margin> *Alchemy is neither a Greek nor an Arabic word, but Hebrew. Egypt was not called "Chemia," but "Ham" or "Chammia," contrary to Plutarch. A description of Alchemy; why it is called the Spagyric art. A passage* <margin>B</margin> *from Manilius explained. Praises of Chemistry;* <margin>C</margin> *an old inscription explained. What is the origin and antiquity of <term>Chrysopoeia</term> <note>the art of making gold</note>;* <margin>D</margin> *many fables and semi-blasphemies are refuted. Concerning Adam, Noah, Moses and his sister Miriam, and Solomon. Concerning Pandora's cup, the Golden Fleece of Colchis, the golden thigh of Pythagoras, and the sacred work of the earth by Crateuas. A passage from 2 Esdras* <margin>E</margin> *chapter 8, verse 2. This art existed under Caligula and Diocletian. The Greek writers on it, Zosimus and Nicephorus Blemmydes. The principal, though not the first, was Geber the Arab.*

#### Sect. II. Concerning the efficacy of the art in the production of gold.

<margin>A</margin> *The arguments of those thinking that true gold cannot be made; it is shown that these do not prove it. Concerning the names of the Philosopher's Stone, and concerning its material and the method of making it. Concerning the nature of metals. That they are* <margin>B</margin> *inanimate and do not live. Concerning the generation of metals. The improper locutions of the Paracelsians. Gold is more easily produced from metal* <margin>C</margin> *than metal is produced in the veins of the earth. The efficient cause of gold. Concerning realgar, orichalcum, and "stomoma" <note>hardened steel or dross</note>. Concerning the matter of metals,* <margin>D</margin> *especially gold. Double matter: 1. remote and 2. proximate. The Aristotelian doctrine defended; the opinion of the Chemists regarding remote matter explained, defended, and reconciled—namely, regarding proximate matter, how the resolution into these proximate principles occurs. Concerning the power* <margin>E</margin> *and force of art and nature. Is gold the only species* <margin>F</margin> *of metal? The definition of metal. Specific diversity is not well proven by the definition of the genus. Imperfect things can...*

-># SUMMARIES OF THE FIRST BOOK.<-

<margin>G</margin> And even that monsters <note>In this context, "monsters" refers to biological anomalies or unnatural births</note> can be predicated of their inferiors. The proximate matter of inanimate things differing in species can be the same, though arranged in different ways. <margin>H</margin> Why gold may be made more quickly through art <note>human skill/chemistry</note> than through nature. <margin>I</margin> Whether true gems can be produced in this way. The principal agent here is the "seed of gold."

### Sect. III. On the same efficacy.
<margin>A</margin> It is probable that gold can be produced in this manner. The heat of fire is effective for various things, as is natural heat. Experience of various transformations. <margin>B</margin> From the carcass of a wasp come hornets. From the dung of a beetle. From a basilisk come scorpions. From orpiment <note>arsenic trisulfide, a yellow mineral</note> comes gold. In the furnaces of Cairo, chicks are brought forth from eggs. Galena is the vein of lead. Little worms are changed into flies. Salt, bitumen, and ink are made by artifice no less than by nature. Wheat and rye are changed into one another. Darnel <note>a weed</note> changed into wheat. Chalcitis <note>a copper-bearing mineral</note> migrates into Misy <note>a related sulfate mineral</note>. Woods turn to stone through water. The fruit of trees and pieces of ships become ducks in Scotland <note>A reference to the "Barnacle Goose" myth, where geese were thought to grow from driftwood or trees</note>. <margin>C</margin> Demons at least know the methods of transmutating metals.

### Sect. IV. Some have made true gold.
<margin>A</margin> A certain Frenchman. Arnold of Villanova. Bernard Trevisan. Raymond Lull. Anthony of Treviso. Concerning Graculus in the time of Anastasius. <margin>B</margin> A response to those attacking these examples. Explained: the chapter *Spondent* concerning the crime of forgery, in the *Extravagantes* <note>Refers to the Papal decree "Spondent quas non exhibent" (1317) by Pope John XXII, which banned alchemy due to fraud</note>. The testimonies of the poor, or of associates and servants, which are to be admitted. No one reaches the ultimate peak of any art.

### Q. II. Whether Gold-making <note>original: "Chrysopœia"</note> should be held among the number of the noble arts, or counted among the mechanical ones?
<margin>A</margin> In itself it is noble and a part of physics <note>natural philosophy</note>; by accident, it is Mechanical. Which Chemists are mechanical; which are philosophers.

### Q. III. To which species of Magic does Alchemy belong?
<margin>A</margin> It is shown to belong to all of them.
<margin>B</margin> Examples: <image-desc>A curly bracket groups the following four terms to the right of the word "Examples"</image-desc>
- Natural,
- Artificial,
- Demonic,
- And Delusory.

---

### Q. IV. Whether alchemy is lawful or unlawful?
<margin>A</margin> It is not prohibited by common law; explained in the chapter *Episcopi* 26. q. 5. and the chapter *Spondent* concerning the crime of forgery in the common *Extravagantes*.
<margin>B</margin> In the court of conscience, it is permitted to very few on account of various circumstances.
<margin>C</margin> How one sins by a defect of purpose, through greed. In what way it is lawful to use alchemical gold. Not for commerce, nor for medicines; but when for ornament and utensils. It must never be mixed with coinage. It is foolish to seek profit from this. One also sins through curiosity, and how dangerous this is.
<margin>D</margin> How one may sin by reason of the methods or means: if from superstition or a pact with a demon. Many Alchemists are Magi, or at least suspected. A presumption of magic exists against alchemists, and in practice it is done with superstitious elements added; they verge into the evil-doing of love potions or poisons. One also sins when they abuse Holy Scripture with words or sacred pictures.
<margin>E</margin> How one sins by reason of time: ill-spent, or by omitting other opportune duties to which one was bound.
<margin>F</margin> How one sins by reason of the person: because they are not wealthy enough, or are of little judgment or talent. Those who delight in deceiving, or the shifty. The incontinent, such as the lustful and the wrathful. The variable, inconstant, impatient, unlearned, and those unskilled in philosophy. The proud, the curious, the impious, the unjust, those not fearing God, nor loving their neighbor, or the impure.
<margin>G</margin> How everyone ought to examine himself. <margin>H</margin> How a confessor ought to behave with an alchemical penitent. Concerning the opinion of those thinking a true human can be begotten by this art <note>A reference to the "Homunculus" concept</note>.
<margin>I</margin> The Law of England <note>likely referring to the Act Against Multipliers</note> ought to be promulgated and observed everywhere, so that this art is not practiced by subjects except by permission of the Prince.
<margin>K</margin> This art is more easily lawful—indeed, almost peculiar—to Princes or the very wealthy.
<margin>L</margin> That common opinion of Jurists does not excuse one from sin in this matter, and why. What force the decree *Spondent* ought to obtain everywhere.

# PREFACE
## ON THE DIFFICULTY AND NECESSITY OF
### THIS TREATISE.

> *I begin the song of arms, with which, above the golden temples of heaven,*
> *The wicked offspring of God <note>The fallen angels/demons</note> strives to place his throne in the far north;*
> *Forbidden to trust in the paternal kingdom, and thrust down into the lowest caves of Erebus <note>The underworld/Hell</note>,*
> *He grows fierce with hatred, and nourishes wrath in his raging mind;*
> *Nor does he rest, never satisfied by the losses of men.*

---

<margin>A</margin>
It is exactly so. There is no truce, no peace; where there is neither limit nor any end to hatred or envy. The pride of the enemies of God always ascends, <gloss>a</gloss> their malice is increased, and their cruelty grows strong. 
<margin>a *Psalm 73, below.*</margin>
The enemy loves to reap tribulations for himself, provided he may bear thorns for us: he rejoices to increase his own punishments and to sharpen his own torments, as long as he may draw as many men as possible into a share of eternal torture; and mocking this divine image <note>humanity, made in the image of God</note>, he seeks to stain that which was washed white by the precious blood of CHRIST, to hand over to former slavery those asserted into liberty, and to destroy them by a "Cadmean victory" <term>Cadmean victory: a victory involving one's own ruin</term>—turning the blade through the enemy's side into his own vitals. He leaves nothing undared, nothing unattempted. He sees a weak and fearful soul: he circles like a lion and threatens force. He knows a soul to be undaunted and constant: he puts on the fox and lays aside the lion's skin. Alas, how many <gloss>b</gloss> *are and have been the prey of foxes?* 
<margin>b *Psalm 61, below.*</margin>
How many has the deceitful wrestler tripped up, entangled by lures and tricks? He has a thousand arts of harming and uses innumerable weapons. In every age, the chief lure from that same quiver has been the lure of arts as curious as they are superstitious; so many and so foolish are the delusions of demons that madden the minds of mortals; all of which 
<margin>B</margin>
the name of *Magic* encompasses in its scope. Concerning this, I know not whether the future discussion will be of greater difficulty or necessity. For indeed, with a most foul sink-hole of sorceresses and evildoers raging everywhere, it is necessary to apply a remedy; but to apply it to an evil so deep-seated and always breaking out anew is especially difficult and laborious. For although very many have tried to write about Magic: 
<margin>c *Cornelius Agrippa, Peter of Abano, Picatrix the Spaniard, Anselm of Parma, Jean Bodin, Johann Weyer, Georg Godelmann and similar. Also Trithemius's Steganography, and Peucer, Erastus, Daneau, etc.*</margin>
nevertheless, what they have committed to writing on this matter must be rejected, either because it is harmful to faith and morals <gloss>c</gloss> and belongs to forbidden reading; or because it is too little and meager, needing to be multiplied and increased; or because it was delivered obscurely, without method, and with too little firmness, and thus must be proposed more clearly and orderly, and better established by reasons and the authority of witnesses. Truly, I do not remember reading anyone who alone has scrutinized and torn out every fiber of the superstitious arts. Philosophers, Jurists, and Theologians have written; but each only according to his own discipline, so that while he may have satisfied his own school or sect, he certainly seems to have been less useful or consulted for the rest. Indeed, I acknowledge myself a debtor to all, having been engaged in these three faculties <note>Philosophy, Law, and Theology</note> for a long time, if I may say so; nor would I lie, because I speak the truth; nor shall I boast, because I do not claim great progress for myself: the cultivation...

He critiques previous authors like Agrippa and Bodin for being either dangerous, incomplete, or too narrow in their academic focus.</summary>

# PROLOGUE

I profess my labor, but leave the judgment of its fruit to others. <margin>C</margin> Having attempted to advance somewhat, I enter with this work a field of study common to three sciences <note>Likely referring to Theology, Law, and Medicine</note>: for I saw that this foundation and inheritance was common to all three, and not the private property of one alone. Therefore (a matter neglected or less noticed by others), I have diligently investigated the foundations of the superstitious arts, and from the decrees of human and divine wisdom, I have undertaken to undermine them with vigor rather than at great length. I am aware that it is the duty of a Christian man not to be ignorant of these fallacies, and, as Tertullian says, *to know the "spiritual forces of wickedness"* <note>original: "spiritalia nequitiae"</note> *not with a partnering conscience, but with a hostile knowledge; and to handle this multifaceted plague of the human mind—the architect of all error and the destroyer of both salvation and the soul—not with an inviting cooperation, but with a conquering mastery.* Nor have I fashioned anything for the sake of appearance which they might refute; rather, from the secrets and mysteries of the masters of vanity and perfidy themselves, I have brought to light the very nursery-rattles of their ravings, drawn from the most obscure books. Then, in a matter as vast as it is minute, I have placed no small amount of labor into reducing it to art and reason, and, unless I am mistaken, I have arranged it in a convenient method, so that the torn and scattered limbs might coalesce into one body, as it were. Finally, so that they may be easily understood by anyone, I have committed them to public light through the press in a plain and clear style, such as we use in the schools. <margin>D</margin> In doing this, although the desires of many who were demanding it moved me somewhat; <margin>A</margin> I was driven far more strongly by a plague as foul as it is obscene, and so creeping: that of sorcerers and enchanters, which in this latest age spreads itself daily further and wider throughout all Europe. So that one may, with the best right, press upon the ears and minds that word of the resonant and invincible Rock of the Church <note>Referring to St. Peter</note>. <margin>a 1 Peter 5:8</margin>

> *Be sober and vigilant, because your adversary the devil circles like a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour:*

And also the words of the Apostle of the Gentiles <note>St. Paul</note> to the same effect; <margin>b Ephesians 6:12</margin>

> *Put on the armor of God, that you may be able to stand against the snares of the Devil: because our struggle is not (only) against flesh and blood (against men, who consist of flesh and blood, and are therefore fragile and fleeting, easily tired and conquered) but (chiefly) against princes and powers (namely) against the world-rulers of these darknesses, against the spiritual forces of wickedness in high places: that is, against natures not corporeal, but spiritual, which are as much more powerful as they are more crafty than men; and whose habitation is not in these houses of clay, but in the sublime and aerial regions.*

In truth, we must not fight against old wives' tales or the dreams of the unlearned, as some keep saying; but we must now join hands in battle with the demons themselves, by whose help and counsel the magi <note>practitioners of magic</note> operate, as reason and experience teach. These must be held as the authors and sowers, as well as the patrons and propagators of all superstition. These have always taken it with an ill-will that they should be disturbed from the possession of so many souls whom they have subjected, and be driven away by reason and law, as if by an armed hand, from ensnaring others whom they have devoured in hope; they bear it now all the more distressingly because, as the final day approaches and Lucifer is to be loosed, they thirst much more greedily to fulfill their cruelty against the human race. <margin>E</margin> If anyone should unroll the Annals of the previous age with some diligence, he will immediately find that never were so many swarms of evildoers <note>original: "maleficorum"</note> caught everywhere in the provinces as offer themselves today to judges who apply even a slight care of searching, and are found even by those not searching. What wonder is it that perfidy overflows when the true faith withers? There are indeed other sins of mortals that are causes of this divine scourge; but a certain lethargy and contempt for the Catholic faith claims the greatest part of this pestilence for itself. For faith alone is the greatest virtue by which all the counsels, studies, efforts, and all the strengths of the Devil are broken. He shudders at this, he flees from this, and with this he fears to clash. Wisely, therefore, Blessed Peter warns us to cast it before the roaring one like a suit of armor <note>original: "panopliam"</note>; <margin>c above, chapter 5</margin> saying, *resist him, strong in faith.* The invincible athlete agrees—

# PROLOGUE

<margin>
Ephesians chapter 6, verse 13.
</margin>

Paul promises his colleague that we shall extinguish all the fiery darts of the most wicked one with the **Shield of Faith** (this is primarily and above all else). Experience confirms—whether from past or present times—that the armies of Hell have always insulted men more sharply and insolently whenever any weakening or corruption of faith has flourished. Before the saving arrival of CHRIST the Lord, idolatry occupied almost the entire world: was there not then a vast multitude of demons everywhere speaking through idols, frequently visiting gatherings of men, and presenting themselves to be seen in the forms of men and women? When JESUS was finally born, in those places where the faith had not yet been announced, this same plague accompanied idolatry, and all places teemed with sorcerers, as the letters and histories of our associates regarding Indian affairs <note>Refers to Jesuit missionary reports from the East and West Indies</note> are rich witnesses. Truly, in those regions where the preaching of the Gospel, once received, has grown cold, or has become foul with various stains of error, or has utterly languished under the oppression of heresies—as in Africa and Asia among the Mohammedans; in Germany, France, and Britain among the heretics; in Italy and other places among the clearly sluggish Catholics whom they call "Politicos" <note>A term for those prioritizing state policy over religious conviction</note>—in these same places, magical superstition has also grown too strong. Thus, fields once wonderfully fertile with wheat have been contaminated by hemlock, aconite, and other most foul poisons.

<margin>
F
</margin>

Indeed, it is so manifest that magical filth serves as a handmaid to heresies—just as a shadow follows a body—that to deny it is a mark of impudence.

<margin>
b Clement of Rome, book 2 of the *Recognitions*.
c Irenaeus book 1, chapters 21, 24. Eusebius book 2 of the *History*, chapter 20. Epiphanius, *Heresy* 27.
d Sulpicius Severus in the *Sacred History*, book 2.
e Nangiacus in the *Chronicle*.
f *Apology* 2.
g The aforementioned book 1, chapter 8.
</margin>

In former times, the principal heretics were also Mages. From these arts, that Samaritan, Simon Magus, obtained the title "Prince of Heretics." Most grave and truthful writers provide evidence concerning Carpocrates, Menander, Marcus, Euphrates, the Ophites and their diagram, Priscillian, and Berengarius. For, to pass over what Clement has most extensively produced concerning Simon, I shall add what Justin Martyr, their fellow countryman, handed down concerning him and his successor together; he wrote to this effect: *Simon, he says, a certain Samaritan born from the town of Gitta, who under Claudius Caesar, supported by the help of demons and relying on magical arts, was held to be a God in this Royal City [Rome], and honored as a God; a statue was placed in the Tiber between two bridges with this Latin inscription:* TO SIMON THE HOLY GOD, *etc. Then: We have learned that Menander, also a Samaritan and a disciple of this Simon, equipped with demonic powers and having set out for Antioch, deceived many by the magical art.* Furthermore, Saint Irenaeus says briefly of Marcus: *Marcus, most skilled in magical imposture, through which he seduced many men and not a few women, converting them to himself, etc. Soon after: Mixing the tricks of Anaxilaus with the wickedness of those who are called Mages, he is thought to perform miracles through these among those who have no sense and have departed from their minds.* ] To which Irenaeus joins several illusions of this heretic: changing white wine into red; not only by himself, but others through him; prophesying by the work of a <term>Paredros</term> <note>A "familiar spirit" or attendant demon</note> demon, and other things of this sort. Concerning the Carpocratian disciples, Epiphanius says: *Magic was devised among them (he says), and they invented various incantations for every machination, for love and for allurements. Nay, they even draw Paredros demons to themselves for this purpose: so that through many illusions they may rule over all whom they wish with great power, and so that each one may dare to undertake any action whatsoever. To be sure, they deceive themselves unto the perfection of their own blinded minds.* ] Severus writes that Priscillian, who brought the madness of the Gnostics into Spain, was imbued with the teachings of Marcus of Egypt (who was a Mage), and was heard and convicted by a double judgment of <term>maleficium</term> <note>Evil-doing or sorcery</note>, nor did he deny that he had studied obscene doctrines. ]

<margin>
h *Letter to Ctesiphon* against the Pelagians.
</margin>

Saint Jerome says: *Priscillian was a most zealous student of Zoroaster the Mage, and became a Bishop from being a Mage.* ] The same Sulpicius Severus, in the *Life of Saint Martin*, mentions a certain Anatolius of this kind.

Mohammed was a heretic, but also a Mage according to the testimony of Zonaras (volume 3) and Paul the Deacon (book 18). Three historians of the Gauls depict for us heretical Pseudo-Christs and add that they excelled in magical illusions; nor is it a wonder that the forerunners of the Antichrist faked miracles. One was from Bourges, the other from Bordeaux; concerning both, one must read—
