# PREFACE

One should read Gregory of Tours regarding the *former in book 12 of his History, and the latter in book 9.* <note>Gregory of Tours (c. 538–594) was a Gallo-Roman historian; the author is citing him to provide historical precedents for his claims.</note> A third was called Eva, a Briton by birth, about whom Robert reports wonderful things in his *Chronicle*, as does William of Newburgh in *book 1, chapter 19 of English Affairs.* As for Berengarius, the leader of the Sacramentarians <note>Berengarius of Tours (d. 1088) was a theologian who disputed the Real Presence in the Eucharist; "Sacramentarians" was a derogatory term for those holding this view.</note>, let him who wishes read the history by Nangiacus; the book is not at my hand to reproduce the exact words. And what need is there? Heretics have always had great dealings with magicians, as Tertullian noted in *On the Prescription*

<margin>a ch. 43 and following.
b ch. 57.</margin>

*Against Heretics*$^a$ and in his *book on the Soul*$^b$. In the former place he has this to say: *The dealings of heretics with very many magicians, with charlatans, with astrologers, and with philosophers—those devoted to idle curiosity—have also been noted. "Seek and you shall find," they remind everyone everywhere.*] In the latter place, he calls *Magic the mother of heretical opinions.*] And rightly so. For it does not only confirm the ravings of heretics, but adds new ones to the old, and opens new schools of curiosity. We read that after the Saracen flood through Spain, magic grew so strong that, while there was a great scarcity and ignorance of all good literature there, almost only the demonic arts were taught openly at Toledo, Seville, and Salamanca. In this very city <note>Likely referring to Salamanca.</note>, now the mother of good arts, when I was staying there, I was shown a very deep crypt—a vestige of a foul school—which that woman of manly spirit, Queen Isabella, wife of Ferdinand the Catholic, had ordered to be blocked up with mortar and stones scarcely a hundred years ago. First the Hussites invaded Bohemia, then the Lutherans invaded Germany: how great a force of evildoers followed them, Sprenger and Nider,

<margin>c In The Hammer of Witches and in The Ant-Hill.</margin>

$^c$ inquisitors of heretical depravity, have taught. Indeed, those whom the torrents of witches poured into the Northern regions know how those people, as if frozen by this Arctic cold, grew numb with fear. For in those places, there is scarcely anything innocent or immune from these beasts in human form—or should I say demons? Many of advanced age, not only on the rack but even after questioning, confessed to judges in the territory of Trier that they were first imbued with this stain at the time when that foul and hellish pillar of Lutheranism, Albert of Brandenburg—himself famous by the name of Magic—was wasting that province, plundering with fire and sword. As many as inhabit the Alpine regions neighboring the Swiss know that few women there are free from sorcery, and many are notorious for this one crime: nor does any truer or more ready cause occur than that, even to this hour, the foul remnants of the Waldensians <note>A medieval sect deemed heretical by the Catholic Church.</note> have remained hidden there as if in dens. Nothing has propagated this plague through England, Scotland, France, and Belgium more swiftly or abundantly than the dire pestilence of Calvinism. Previously, in few places, an uncertain rumor was spread about only a few lowly people; now, with the heresy itself—like madness with a fever—it has invaded many everywhere who are famous for nobility, learning, wealth, and dignity. Thus the insatiable

<margin>d Job 40:18.
Habakkuk 1:16.</margin>

Behemoth, having already drunk up the sea, hopes that the Jordan will flow into his mouth,$^d$ and having fed on choice food, he will devastate the Lord's own portion. What of the fact that Daneau, a Calvinist minister, relates that a gathering of witches is a regular occurrence at Geneva near the main church, and that in that city within the space of three months more than five hundred persons were punished with death by the sentence of the judges? Read Crespet on *The Hatred of Satan, book 1, discourse 15.* However, as I sought the reasons why magic constantly accompanies heresy, those which my former teacher John Maldonatus, a Priest of the Society of Jesus—a man equally learned and holy—most eloquently set forth when he was teaching publicly about Demons in Paris, conveniently occurred to me. He recounted five causes in these words:

The first cause is that demons have their home in heretics, as they once did in idols. For Jerome is accustomed to say rightly that the devil, when he saw the idols in which he previously dwelt cast down, fashioned worse idols in the minds of heretics out of the Divine Scriptures themselves. Cassian, a grave and ancient author, *Conference 7,*

<column-break/>
chapter 32.

# PROLOGUE

<margin>Chapter 32.</margin> He asserts that he heard a demon confessing that it had published the impiety of sacrilegious doctrine through Arius and Eunomius <note>Arius and Eunomius were early Christian leaders deemed heretics for their views on the nature of the Trinity.</note>. Therefore, it is necessary that—just as when a war is finished, the soldiers scatter in all directions and become robbers, occupying every road—so too the demons, when the heresies that previously flourished are weakened or abolished, seek new seats in other men, as if their temples in which they were worshiped had been overturned. For demons are accustomed to act, when they are forced to depart from a man in whom they dwelt, just as those do who, exhausted by a siege, are forced to surrender the citadel they held. They ask, as a condition of surrender, that they be allowed to withdraw to another place: which we understand by the example of that "Legion" which asked of CHRIST that He permit them to occupy the pigs.

The second cause is that every heresy is at its beginning utterly violent and vehement; but it cannot remain at that same level. This is because, beyond the fact that divine providence does not allow it, nature itself is such that error cannot long imitate the truth. Therefore, it is necessary for every heresy—unless it returns early to that religion from which it departed—to degenerate either into the magical arts or into the extreme impiety of atheism. For since there are chiefly two causes by which men are made heretics; one is the pride of a wanton intellect, believing nothing except what it perceives with its eyes; the other is a certain intemperate curiosity and a desire for novelty. It follows quite necessarily that those who became heretics through pride, when they find that the light they thought they embraced in the heat of their first discovery has turned to darkness as that heat cools, they believe in nothing at all out of despair of finding the truth elsewhere. And those whom curiosity had driven into heresy, when those things which seemed new at the beginning have grown a little old, are driven by that same curiosity both to enter into familiarity with demons and to learn and practice the arts of demons.

The third cause is that it seems so arranged by nature that, just as pestilence follows famine, so various kinds of "curious arts" <note>A common biblical euphemism for magic and the occult.</note> follow heresy. For heresy is a kind of famine of the word of God. <margin>Amos 8.</margin> Indeed, as in a time of high grain prices men are forced to use unhealthy foods, from which it happens that a plague is birthed from corrupted humors; so while heresy flourishes, while men use corrupted senses of the scripture, they finally arrive at Magical arts, which are like diseases of the soul.

The fourth cause is that demons are accustomed to use heretics to deceive men, as if they were beautiful prostitutes. For it is manifest that heresy is called a prostitute in scripture, <margin>Isaiah 1.</margin> *How has the faithful city become a prostitute?* Therefore, just as pimps, when the beauty of a harlot has faded, turn the harlot into a madam; so the demons, when the first appearance of heresy has so perished that men are less enticed into error, make Magicians out of heretics.

The fifth cause is, in my opinion, the negligence of those who rule the Church. For just as locusts are usually born in uncultivated fields, so from the poverty of the word of God, the deceptive arts are born. For locusts in the scripture also signify demons. <margin>Revelation 9.</margin> Therefore, it seems to fit even this time, what Joel says in chapter 1: *What the caterpillar left, the locust has eaten; and what the locust left, the grub has eaten.* For what the heretics had left behind, the sorcerers devastate by the art of demons, and what the sorcerers leave, the atheists destroy. These things were said no less sharply than truly by Father Maldonado <note>Juan Maldonado (1533–1583), a highly influential Spanish Jesuit theologian.</note>.

And would that these things did not run right before our eyes. We once saw the flourishing Belgians, the *Gueux* <note>original: "Geusios" - The "Beggars," a confederacy of Dutch nobles who resisted Spanish rule and promoted the Reformation.</note> of *Calvinism, Lutheranism,* and *Anabaptism*, devouring everything like caterpillars. We saw these three unclean spirits coming out of the mouth of the dragon, and <margin>Revelation 16:13.</margin> *out of the mouth of the beast, and out of the mouth of the False Prophet.* We see them now withering away,

-># P R O L O G U E<-

And with the very passage of time almost expiring; various swarms of "strong locusts" <note>A biblical metaphor for heretics or destructive forces, likely referring to Protestant reformers</note> are devastating the entire North. We see in another part the number of atheists or "politicians" <note>original: "politicorum" — in this context, it refers to those who prioritize statecraft and secular interests over religious truth</note> growing so much that, since so few fervent and true Catholics remain, they seem to be almost none at all due to their very scarcity. Many from our **SOCIETY** <note>The Society of Jesus, or the Jesuits</note> have strenuously opposed those heretics and have remarkably broken their madness with the books they have written.

<margin>
a Turrianus, Bellarminus, Busaeus, Canisius, Costerus, Thyraeus, Valencia, Molina, Suarez, Fonseca, Pererius, Vasquez, Serarius, Possevinus, Maldonatus, and others. <note>A list of prominent Jesuit theologians and scholars of the 16th and 17th centuries</note>
</margin>

Our theologians and philosophers have also frequently routed the atheists—who, like the Lernaean Hydra, sprout new heads—by cutting them back with the fiery sword of truth. These "locusts" <note>referring here to practitioners of superstition or magic</note>, whom those authors passed over, or with whom they only skirmished briefly and lightly; I have now chosen to dispel them professionally with the winnowing fan of disputation, and to scorch them with the steel mirror of truth and charity. I do this with no confidence in my own strength, but relying on the help of the Supreme Divinity. For He, for whose honor and glory I have undertaken this contest, will not, I hope, desert His champion.

Come now, O true and eternal **WISDOM**; whose name the treacherous Magi <note>referring to practitioners of forbidden magic or occultists</note> have denied; help and defend me. You, O **MOST HOLY VIRGIN**; whom they disparage and spit upon more than all the heavenly beings; protect your client and favor him as he fights. You, O Saint **MICHAEL**, with the heavenly legions over whom you preside; who cast down Lucifer with his companions in rebellion from heaven and drove them into the depths of the abyss; assist me as I descend into the arena. And all you inhabitants of Heaven, Blessed Souls, repress this tyranny of the infernal spirits, and avert this most dire plague from the minds of your brothers; and, as is your custom, strike, lay low, and put to flight the often-triumphed-over battle lines of the dark and foul demons <note>original: "cacodaemonum"</note>. Relying on your suggestions and assistance—and may it turn out well for me and for the readers—I approach the matter, after I have first tasted a few necessary things concerning superstition itself for a clearer understanding of what follows.

# BY MARTIN DELRIO, PRIEST OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS,
## BOOK ONE.
### Concerning MAGIC in general, and concerning NATURAL and ARTIFICIAL magic in particular.
#### CHAPTER I.
##### Concerning Superstition, and its types.

<margin>a Book 8 of the Etymologies, chapter 3.</margin>
<margin>A</margin>
<image-desc>Historiated woodcut initial 'E' depicting a scholar, likely Saint Isidore of Seville, seated at a desk or lectern with an open book, surrounded by decorative foliage.</image-desc>
Saint Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an archbishop and encyclopedist</note> examines the ETYMOLOGY <note>The origin and historical development of a word</note> perhaps too meticulously with these words: ^a^ *superstition is so named because it is a superfluous, or "super-established" observation. Others say it comes from the elderly (superstites), because having survived for many years, they become delirious through age and wander into a certain superstition, not knowing what ancient things they worship, or what they adopt in ignorance of the ancients. Lucretius, however, says superstition is named for "standing over" (superstantium) things—that is, celestial or divine things which stand above us—but he speaks poorly.* ] He speaks not poorly if you look at the analogy, but if you look at his intent. For the Epicurean poet <note>Lucretius, who followed the philosophy of Epicurus which taught that gods do not intervene in human affairs</note> believed that God had no care for human affairs, and therefore he called the fear of the supreme deity "superstition," because that fear stands over and hangs over mortals with a horrible gaze. For he alludes to those verses of Lucretius: <margin>b Book 1.</margin> ^b^
> *When human life lay foully on the ground*
> *In the lands, crushed under the weight of religion;*
> *Which showed its head from the regions of heaven,*
> *Threatening mortals from above with a horrible gaze.*

<margin>c On Aeneid Book VIII.</margin>
For to Lucretius, as to other gentiles <note>Non-Christians, specifically pagans</note>, "religion" is used in place of "superstition." Moreover, that etymology also pleased Servius <note>A 4th-century grammarian and commentator on Virgil</note>: to whom, ^c^ *superstition is the vain and superfluous fear of things standing above (that is, celestial and divine things which stand above us).* What the Bishop of Seville <note>Isidore</note> adds regarding the elderly is not his own invention. For so says Donatus <note>A famous Roman grammarian</note>, or whoever the author may be: <margin>d On Terence's Andria.</margin> ^d^ *the "survivors" (superstites) are old men or old women, because having survived to a great age they are now delirious. Whence also the "superstitious," who fear the Gods too much, which is a sign of delirium.*

<margin>B</margin>
<margin>e Acts 17, verse 22.</margin>
Among Christian writers, the name of superstition is usually taken in a bad sense, even in that passage of Saint Paul; ^e^ *Men of Athens, I see that you are in all things as if superstitious.* In Syriac it is: "excessive in the worship of demons"; in Greek <term>deisidaimonesterous</term> <note>original: δεισιδαιμονεστέρους; literally "fearing the spirits/demons more than is right"</note>, more superstitious than is just: namely, because out of a vain fear of demons, they worshiped stones, wood, spirits, and even unknown and ambiguous deities. In this sense, the Fathers <note>The Church Fathers, early influential Christian theologians</note> call the error of the gentiles "Superstition," not "Religion"; unless the notation of "vain" or "false" is added. For there is a great difference between Religion and Superstition. For not only philosophers (says Cicero) <margin>f Book 1, On the Nature of the Gods.</margin> ^f^ *but also our ancestors separated Religion from superstition; for those who prayed and sacrificed all day long so that their children might be "survivors" (superstites) to them, were called Superstitious*

<sig>A</sig>
<meta>The catchword "Sunt" indicates the first word of the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the scholarly and theological definitions of "superstition," contrasting the views of classical authors like Lucretius and Cicero with Christian perspectives like those of Isidore of Seville and St. Paul. Delrio explores the word's etymology, linking it to both the "fear of things above" and the "delirium of the elderly."</summary>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

*...they were so named, a name which later became more widely used. However, those who diligently reconsidered and, as it were, "re-read" <note>Latin: "relegerent"</note> everything pertaining to the worship of God, were called "religious" from "re-reading" <note>Latin: "relegendo"</note>, just as "elegant" comes from "selecting" <note>Latin: "eligendo"</note>, "diligent" from "valuing" <note>Latin: "diligendo"</note>, and "intelligent" from "understanding" <note>Latin: "intelligendo"</note>. For in all these words, there is the same sense of "reading" or "gathering" <note>Latin: "legendi"</note> as is found in the word "religious." Thus it happened that between "superstitious" and "religious," one became a term of vice, the other of praise.]* I have included these words because

<margin>
g Book 4, chapter 28.
h Book 14 of The City of God, chapter 30.
</margin>
I believe they should be read according to Lactantius^g; by whose authority and that of Saint Augustine^h we must correct Nonius Marcellus, who reports these same things differently. Nonius even claims that the "Superstitious" are so called because they "supersede" <note>Latin: "supersedeant"</note>—that is, neglect—all other things in favor of the worship of the gods. Likewise, he claims the "Religious" are like "the abandoned" <note>Latin: "relinquosos," as found in the manuscripts</note> because, having abandoned all else, they serve only sacrifices—a ridiculous fabrication, and one worthy of Nonius. Lactantius, with his usual scholarly force and abundance of arguments, refutes Cicero’s distinction and concludes: *Religion is the worship of the true; Superstition is the worship of the false.]* This is excellent; however, what he adds is not: <margin>C</margin> *It matters entirely what you worship, not how you worship, or what you pray.]* That was said with too little consideration.

For superstition also arises if, while you ought to worship God the Best and Greatest, you do not worship or pray as you ought. There is only a place for Religion if those who supplicate the one and true God do so in the manner that is proper. For our theologians most excellently define Religion as the virtue by which we offer true and owed worship to the true God; therefore, they frequently say that two extreme vices oppose it. One is found in deficiency, which is called Irreligiousness because it detracts from the honor owed; the other is found in excess—not that it offers a true worship greater than is fair (for who could offer as much as is equal to that infinite goodness and immense majesty?), but it is "excessive" because it is neither true nor good, but corrupted by some superfluous circumstance. Because this is added to the proper matter wrongly, and to the injury of God rather than His honor, they call it a vice found in excess.^i

<margin>
i See Gregory of Valencia, volume 2, dispute 6, question 10, point 1.
</margin>
Since the "good" is the same as the "perfect" and rejects every defect or vice, and nothing deserves to be called "true" unless it is also "good," it is clearly understood that if any evil is found in divine worship, it is not true worship, and therefore cannot be called Religion. Furthermore, since all Superstition indicates by its very name a vice within worship, it follows that no worship which is superstitious should be called true worship; and thus all superstition is of a false worship, as Lactantius had said. But this falsehood arises not from only one source, as he thought, but from two. The first is entirely capital, which he was not ignorant of: namely, if that which is not God is worshipped as God; our scholars call this the *Superstition of false worship*. <margin>D</margin> The other is less destructive, yet often lethal and always harmful, which Firmianus <note>Lactantius</note> seems wrongly to exclude from superstition: if worship is offered to Him to whom it is owed, but not in the manner in which it is owed. Theologians call this the *Superstition of undue worship*, because it is not of the kind that is owed. The former is usually called by the general name Idolatry, because whatever is adored as God when it is not God is called an idol, according to the scripture:^k

<margin>
k 1 Chronicles 16, verse 26.
אליל ELIL.
</margin>
*All the gods of the peoples are idols—that is, vanity and emptiness, and nothing less than what they wish to be taken for.* For that is the force of the Hebrew word *ELIL* <note>original: "אליל", meaning "worthless" or "nothingness"</note>. The latter type retains its own name and has no other specific one that I know of; nor did Saint Augustine^l assign one, contenting himself with describing it when he dealt with imaginary signs of undue worship in these words:

<margin>
l Book 2 of On Christian Doctrine, chapter 23.
</margin>
*...those things which pertain to the care of remedies and other observations; which were not divinely established, as it were, for the love of God and neighbor, but through private designations of temporal things, they scatter the hearts of the wretched.]* Regarding this "undue worship," we shall hold no discussion now; we must discourse only on the "superstition of false worship." And since that comprises two species—*Explicit Idolatry*, when the worship owed to God is clearly and expressly transferred to a creature (as when the Jews worshipped the calf, the Egyptians Anubis, or the Romans Quirinus); and *Tacit* or, as others call it, *Implicit* Idolatry—we shall now set aside that first type...

<summary>The author refutes classical definitions of superstition and religion, arguing that superstition includes both the worship of false gods (idolatry) and the improper worship of the true God. He establishes a theological framework for "undue worship" versus "false worship" to prepare for a discussion on magic.</summary>

...we do, being content to elucidate according to our small measure those things which pertain to the latter part and are more obscure. All prohibited magic is a form of silent idolatry, as we shall demonstrate in its proper place.

# CHAPTER II.

->Concerning Magic, its divisions, and the various names of Magicians.<-

<margin>A</margin>
<margin>a. Francisco de Vitoria, *Relectiones* on Magic, number 8. Angles in *Flores* on Book 1, distinction 7, and others.</margin>
I define Magic, taken in its most universal sense, in the broadest possible terms: *that it is an art or faculty which, by a created and non-supernatural power, effects certain wonderful and unusual things, the reason for which surpasses the senses and common human understanding.* I have used these very broad terms because I see that great men, following Francisco de Vitoria <note>A 16th-century Spanish philosopher and theologian</note> <sup>a</sup>, have excluded artificial and natural magic while establishing stricter boundaries. I have used the word *Art* or *Faculty* to stand for any kind of knowledge and operation—whether it be demonstrative or topical, producing certain science or not, mechanical or liberal, skilled or unskilled, true or deceptive, superstitious or free from vice. I have placed the word *Effecting* to represent every function of the mind, soul, sense, or limbs. By *Power*, I mean either that of the thing applied or of the person applying it; namely, of a human or a demon. I named it *Created and non-supernatural* to exclude true miracles, which are the work of God alone; for this reason, I preferred to say *wonderful things* <note>original: "mira"</note> rather than miracles. Finally, I inserted the mention of *common understanding and sense* because only the wiser men—who are few in every age—can grasp the causes of many magical effects.

<margin>B</margin>
The division of Magic, taken so broadly, must be sought from its final and efficient causes. *From the efficient cause* <note>The "efficient cause" is the agent that brings something about</note> comes the division into *Natural, Artificial, and Diabolical*; because all its effects are to be ascribed either to the nature inherent in things, or to human industry, or to the malice of an evil demon. *From the final cause* <note>The "final cause" is the purpose or goal of an action</note>, you may rightly divide it: first into *good*, if it uses a good intention and lawful means (which applies only to artificial and natural magic); and into *evil*, whose end or the means it uses are wicked. This latter is the specific category of prohibited Magic, which we have called a form of silent <margin>C</margin> idolatry and a species of superstition. This prohibited Magic can be described thus: *A faculty or art by which, through the power of a pact entered into with demons, certain wonderful things are effected that surpass common human understanding.* I said *by the power of a pact*, since all the power of this Magic rests on a silent or express pact, as I will teach in its proper place. Theologians call it *silent idolatry* because Magicians do not usually intend to offer worship to a creature as if it were God, but rather as if to a benefactor and gift-giver from whom they may obtain something. This intention, by reason of its varied objects, gives rise to four species of this silent idolatry or prohibited Magic. For sometimes it happens that only the knowledge or operation of extraordinary and wonderful effects is sought; and then it claims the name of *Special Magic* in the strict sense. Sometimes it happens that one strives to know future things, or even past or present things that are secret and hidden; this attempt is called *Divination*. Sometimes one desires to be instructed and helped, not to do good, but to harm others or take revenge; the name for this crime is *Maleficium* <note>Sorcery or harmful magic</note>. Finally, there are sometimes those who are determined, without harm to others, to benefit themselves or others through this work for utility or pleasure; and then Theologians call it *Trifling or Vain Observance*. Thus, rejected Magic is divided into *Special Magic, Divination, Maleficium, and Vain Observance*; we shall dispute concerning each of these in order.

From this diversity of the species of Magic, a wonderful variety of names has flowed among the Hebrews, Greeks, and Latins to signify those who have been initiated into these Stygian <note>Hellish or underworld</note> mysteries. Let us follow the principal names.

<margin>
Aovoth
Iddeghoni
<sup>b</sup> Leviticus 19:31 & 20:6. Deuteronomy 18:11. 2 Kings 23:24. Isaiah 8:19 & 19:3.
</margin>
Certain Hebrew names seem to pertain to divination alone, such as those two usually joined together, **AOVOTH** and **IDDEGHONI**; of which, unless I am mistaken, the latter has a slightly broader meaning, and therefore in the Sacred Scriptures it is usually placed after the former in passages <sup>b</sup> found everywhere. *Aovoth* comes from the root *Ov* <note>אוב</note>, because the voice is rendered more obscure and as if from a leather bottle...

<summary>The author defines magic in its broadest sense as any art producing wonders beyond common understanding, then categorizes it into natural, artificial, and diabolical types. He specifically identifies prohibited magic as a "silent idolatry" based on a pact with demons, subdivided into special magic, divination, sorcery, and vain observance.</summary>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

<margin>

d As I have taught in several commentaries on Isaiah ch. 8 v. 19.

f See above at letter b.
</margin>
...shrieking $^c$. For **OB** <note>original: "OF"</note> primarily signifies the responding demon itself; secondarily, it signifies the Magician who draws out the response. Just as he is called a *Python* <note>A term for a person possessed by a spirit of divination, named after the Delphic Python</note> and a *ventriloquist*, it refers either to the inhabiting demon itself, responding from the depths of the human, or the human whom the demon inhabits $^d$. Among the Greeks, the word *eurykles* <note>original: "ευρυκλῆς"</note> also signifies both, named after a certain Eurycles who possessed a Python spirit and was the first ventriloquist in Athens, from whom others were called *eurykleis*. This is taught by Suidas, Hesychius, and the Scholiast of Aristophanes $^e$, and could be proven from Aristophanes and Plutarch. **IDDEGHONI** does not pertain to palm-readers <term>Chiromanticos: those who tell fortunes by the lines of the hand</term> and the like, as some believe; rather, it refers to the oracles of demons, whether through **AOVOTH** <note>spirits of the dead</note>, or from the bowels of the earth, or through the summoning of shades <note>necromancy</note>, as is gathered by a comparison of the Sacred Scripture passages where this name is used $^f$; or it refers to a certain kind of divination discussed in the third book.

<margin>
NACHAZ <note>Hebrew: נחש</note>
h Genesis 41:5 & 15.
i Leviticus 19:26; Numbers 23:23 & 24:1; Deuteronomy 18:10; 2 Chronicles 33:6.
</margin>
Other names regard not so much what the demon effects, but what the Magician attempts, and these are more broadly evident. For **NACHAZ**, **KISEM**, and **GHONEM** pertain to all divinations; while **CHABAR** and **LACHAZ** pertain to all incantations; and finally, **CHARTVMMIM** and **MECHASSEPHIM** pertain to all Magic in general, which must now be discussed. **NACHAZ** signifies augurs and all other vain prognosticators who are accustomed to divine the future from certain chance events; such a person Joseph pretended to be for a time through a clever ambiguity of speech $^h$. This divination is prohibited in many places $^i$.

<margin>
KASAM <note>Hebrew: כסמ</note>
k as in v. 21 & v. 10; 1 Samuel 28:8; 1 Kings 3:17; Isaiah 3:2; Jeremiah 27:9; Ezekiel 12:24, 21:21, & 22:28.
</margin>
**KASAM** signifies divining universally, whence **KISEM** is a diviner or soothsayer, and **KESEM** is divination. It is also taken in a bad sense regarding any prohibited conjecture of things, even those not occurring by chance. Mention of it is found everywhere in the Sacred Scriptures $^k$.

<margin>
YONEN <note>Hebrew: עונן</note>
l In the cited places of Jeremiah & Micah 5:11.
m as in Deuteronomy 18:10 & 2 Chronicles 33:6.
</margin>
**YONEN** is applied to any augurs and prognosticators $^l$, and even to interpreters of dreams $^m$. Therefore, the more recent Rabbis wrongly restrict it to those who superstitiously observe days and times to see if they be lucky or unlucky; but this category pertains to all such things.

<margin>
CHABAR <note>Hebrew: חבר</note>
n Job 40:25 & Psalm 57:6.
</margin>
How **CHABAR** $^n$ and **LACHAZ** $^o$ differ I have not yet found; therefore (unless I am mistaken) they signify the same thing, namely an *epaoidon* <note>original: "επαοιδὸν"</note> or enchanter, or (as the Heathens called them) an exorcist; one who performs wonders with profane or evil words or mutterings.

<margin>
LACHAZ <note>Hebrew: לחש</note>
o cited v. 6; Ecclesiastes 10:11.
CHARTVMMIM <note>Hebrew: חרתמם</note>
p as in Genesis 41:8 & 24.
q as in Exodus 7:11 & 22; 8:7 & 18; ch. 9:11.
r as in Genesis above.
s as in Exodus above.
t In Exodus.
u In Genesis.
x Daniel 1:20 & 2:2.
</margin>
Regarding **CHARTVMMIM**, I believe Abenezra, Ben-Natan, and other Rabbis are mistaken, as they seem to refer this to natural or "good" Magic. I ask you, if it signified this, why would it be so seriously prohibited in the Sacred Scriptures? Indeed, I think this name encompasses every kind of Magician who, allied with a demon, divines $^p$ or performs deceptive miracles $^q$; indeed, it is proven to include all manner of chanters and sorcerers. This is because the Septuagint <note>The Greek Old Testament</note> sometimes translates it as "interpreters" <note>original: "ἐξηγητὰς"</note> $^r$; sometimes as "poisoners" or "sorcerers" <note>original: "Φαρμακοὺς"</note> $^s$; and sometimes as "enchanters" <note>original: "ἐπαοιδὸὺς"</note> $^t$. Our Vulgate also sometimes translates it as "conjectors" $^u$, sometimes as "soothsayers" $^x$, and sometimes as "evildoers" $^y$, so that by the authority of these translators we may understand the word to be very broad in meaning.

<margin>
y In Exodus above.
z Exodus 7:11 & 22:18.
aa 2 Chronicles 33:6.
bb Isaiah 47:9 & 12; Jeremiah 27:9; Daniel 2:2; Micah 5:12; Nahum 3:4; Malachi 3:5.
cc 2 Kings 9:22.
</margin>
Finally, the word **MECHASSEPHIM** arises from the root **CHASAPH**, signifying any evildoer using deceptions or any other evil Magical arts. Therefore, our translator interprets it so broadly as "evildoers" $^z$, "evil arts" $^{aa}$, "malefice" $^{bb}$, or "sorceries" $^{cc}$. Nevertheless, there was a certain man, vehemently suspected of this crime and a self-professed patron of witches, who tried to persuade readers that this word—at least in the law of Exodus chapter 22—should be restricted only to "poisoners." His error is easily refuted. First, because the root of the name, **CHASAPH**, does not signify "to kill by poison" (as he thinks), but when it is placed alone, and the sense of the narrative does not demand restriction, it now...

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III.

...broadly, it then contains every kind of magical mockery, as found in Malachi, Nahum, and in the books of the remnants <note>likely referring to the Paralipomena or Chronicles</note>. If, however, it is ever restricted, it happens because the condition of the thing being described demands it. Thus, in Exodus 7, it is restricted to "illusionists" <term>praestigiatores: those who use sleight of hand or demonic deception to trick the eyes</term> properly so called, who were the only ones being discussed. Thus in Jeremiah, it is used for "diviners"; in Daniel, for "interpreters of dreams." Let the adversary produce any single passage where it is used for "poisoners" alone.

<margin>
See 2 Kings 9:22 <note>original: 4 Reg. 9</note>
</margin>
It is well that he brought forward only two passages. One concerning **Jezebel**: as if indeed Jezebel were accustomed to attack the prophets secretly with poisons, and not openly with the sword and slaughter! How much more likely is it that a woman, raging with the insane worship of demons, also drove Ahab and others out of their minds with incantations, magic wheels <note>original: rhombis; a tool used in ancient Greek sorcery</note>, and other love-potions? As Tostatus <note>Alonso Tostado, a prolific 15th-century Spanish theologian</note> rightly judged in that same place, in the latter part.

Certainly, before Jezebel, Samaria was not suspected of magic; afterwards, through this powerful viper <note>original: excetram; a term for a multi-headed snake or a wicked woman</note>, this plague was let in and clung so stubbornly that the name "Samaritan" was used for an "enchanter." Hence the objection cast at the Lord JESUS: *You are a Samaritan, and you have a demon* <margin>John 8:48-49</margin>, as if it were a trait peculiar to that nation to have commerce with a demon.

Therefore the Lord refuted that insult with a single denial: *I do not have a demon*. It is as if he said: I have no familiarity with a "familiar demon" <term>paredro: a spirit that sits beside or serves a magician</term>, or an "oracle-spirit" <term>euricle: likely a reference to Eurycles, a famous ancient ventriloquist/soothsayer</term>, or a "covenanted spirit"; for which reason I am not a Samaritan. And after Christ the Lord, the famous leaders of magic in Samaria and their schools arose: namely, the Simonians and the Menandrians <note>followers of Simon Magus and Menander, early Gnostic figures associated with magic</note>. He brings a second passage from Exodus chapter 22, because the Seventy <note>The Septuagint, the Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> translated it as *Pharmakous* <note>Greek: Φαρμακοὺς</note>; which he contends signifies "poisoners" alone. Being as good a Greek scholar as he is a Christian, he is refuted by the very custom of the Seventy Translators, who it is established use that word more broadly. First, because in Exodus 7:11 they also translate it as *Pharmakous* where it is established they are dealing with illusionists, for they also call the incantation or illusions there *Pharmakias*; and those whom they previously called *Pharmakous*, they also call *epoidoi* <note>Greek: ἐπωιδοὶ; enchanters or those who sing spells</note>. Secondly, they used the term just as broadly in Chronicles: "he practiced sorcery" <note>original: ἐφαρμακεύσατο</note>. Thirdly, because in the words of Jeremiah, they included "diviners" under the name of *Pharmakeion*. Fourthly, because among the interpreters of dreams in Daniel, they list *Pharmakous*. Therefore, those most learned elders of the Hebrews take this word so broadly. That they do so skillfully, I would prove by the testimonies of Hippocrates, Plato, Aristotle, Theocritus, and other ancients, if I thought it worth the effort and if others had not done so before. Just as Josephus does not support our adversaries, so he does not oppose us. Standing for us are Philo, in his book *On Special Laws* near the middle, and most of the Catholic Fathers <margin>St. Jerome on Jeremiah and Daniel; St. Augustine, City of God, Book 21, ch. 6; Hesychius, Book 6 on Leviticus; Rabanus Maurus and others on Exodus 22.</margin>, who referred that law of Moses to all species of magicians. So much for the Hebrew matters.

From the Greeks, there are also their own names which, taken strictly, differ. For *epoidos* signifies an "enchanter," *mantis* a "soothsayer" or "diviner," *pharmakos* a "poisoner," and *goes* <note>Greek: γόης</note> an "illusionist" properly. But frequently the first, third, and fourth are extended to all magic. *Goes* even denotes any kind of impostor. You may see those three joined synonymously by Plato, Synesius, Herodotus, Chrysostom, and Hesychius.

So for the Latins, there is one proper meaning and another adapted by extension. The name of the **Magi** was transferred from the Barbarians <note>Persians/non-Greeks</note> to the Greeks and Latins; initially, it was held in honor among the Persians and Chaldeans <margin>Jerome on Daniel ch. 2; Apuleius in his Apology; Hesychius and Suidas.</margin> as proper to the overseers of sacred rites, the advisors of kings, philosophers, and theologians; it began to turn toward infamy and to be attributed to all evil arts. **Venefici** <note>Venom-makers/Poisoners</note> was the name used previously only for those who harmed with evil drugs, and they are called "poisoners"; later this name was used for "enchanters" and applied to all "Thessalians" <note>Thessaly was famous in antiquity for witchcraft</note>. **Arioli** <note>soothsayers</note>, **Augurs**, **Haruspices**, **Chaldeans**, **Thessalians**, **Genethliaci** <note>astrologers who cast birth charts</note> are often confused. **Lamiae** <note>vampiric spirits or witches</note> are so called because they bring death to children, the name being taken either from that famous and cruel Queen Lamia, about whom Anton...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the etymology and origins of various types of magic and practitioners, following the previous page's focus on the definitions of sorcery.</meta>

Antoninus Liberalis, Diodorus, and others [write of these]; either from succubus demons, of whom Philostratus speaks; or from Libyan wild beasts, of whom Dion the Sophist writes. <margin>See the Adages on "The Towers of Lamia," and our Commentary on the Tragedies of Seneca.</margin> **Striges** <note>original: "Striges"; screech-owls or owl-like hags</note>, named from the ill-omened and nocturnal bird which is believed to bring destruction to infants. To the ancient Romans, these women were also called **Veratrices** <note>Truth-tellers</note>; because they profess a knowledge of divining, just as those dregs of humanity who boast of being Egyptians <note>likely referring to Romani or "Gypsy" travelers</note> are accustomed to do—the refuse of all peoples and nations. The Lombards in their laws call them **Mascas** <note>Masks or witches</note>; perhaps for this reason, that in their secret assemblies they cover themselves with masks; <margin>See the commentary on Medea, verse 732.</margin> <margin>Remigius, Book 1 of Demonolatry, chapter 18.</margin> or because they are of such deformed facial appearance (as most are), that they are more similar to masks or specters than to women. The ancients used to name those **Sortilegi** <note>Lot-casters or fortune-tellers</note> who strove to divine something by a superstitious casting of any kind of lots <margin>Outside the title on Fortune-telling.</margin>: But later Theologians and Jurists confused them with the unspeakable crime of the screech-owl witches. I find these same people called **Sortiarii** <note>Sorcerers</note> and **Sortiariae** <note>Witches</note> as far back as the age of Hincmar, Archbishop of Reims <margin>Chapter last, 33. q. 1.</margin>. These are at once poisoners and liege vassals of the Demon, wherefore they can also be called **Satanic Poisoners**. Their crime is ancient, existing even before the coming of **CHRIST** the Lord. For such were *Horace's Canidia*, and *Lucan's Erichtho*, *Tacitus's Martina*, and the *Thessalian women* of *Lucian* and *Apuleius*. The name "sorcerer" is more recent.

# CHAPTER III.
## *Concerning Natural or Physical Magic.*

<margin>Plutarch's book on Isis and Osiris; Laertius at the beginning of his history; Agathias and others.</margin>
<margin>Apology I.</margin>
<margin>Book 4 of the Recognitions.</margin>
**T**hose who above all other mortals were devoted to Magic were the *Persians*; from whom *Marcion* and *Manes* <note>Founders of the Marcionite and Manichaean heresies</note> borrowed it. They believed in two Gods as authors and Lords of things: one good, *Oromazus* or *Oromagda* <note>Ahura Mazda</note>, whom they judged to be the sun; and the other evil, *Arimanus* or *Pluto* <note>Angra Mainyu</note>. From these deities they derived a twofold Magic: one which was entirely superstitious and handed down the worship of false Gods and many things of this sort; the other which was skilled in the inward natures of things. *Apuleius* ascribes both types to the *Persians*. The former is deceptive and harmful, sufficiently indicating the Devil as its author; he taught it before the flood to those evil giants, by whom *Ham* <note>The son of Noah</note> was imbued with it, and from him his descendants, the *Egyptians*, *Chaldeans*, and *Persians*. For thus I interpret the words of *Saint Clement* concerning the *sinning Angels*; he says: they taught men that demons could obey mortals through certain arts, that is, through magical invocations; and as if from a certain furnace and workshop of malice, they filled the whole world with the smoke of impiety, the light of piety having been withdrawn. For these and several other reasons the flood was brought upon the world, and all who were upon the earth were destroyed, except the family of *Noah*, who survived with his three sons and their wives. From these, one named *Ham* handed down the evilly-discovered discipline of Magic to one of his sons, who was called *Mizraim*, from whom the race of the *Egyptians*, *Babylonians*, and *Persians* is drawn. The nations that then existed called him *Zoroaster*, admiring the first author of the magical art, in whose name also many books are held on this subject, etc.] He adds that this *Zoroaster* was later struck by lightning; he was called "Zoroaster" as if a "living star," and was held for a God. There was not just one *Zoroaster*, but many (for *Goropius* trifles when he thinks there was none). That first one was the *Chaldean* inventor of Magic: *Sixtus of Siena* and others are mistaken who attribute only good magic to him. The second was perhaps the *Bactrian*, who waged war with *Ninus*, of whom *Pliny*, *Justin*, and *Arnobius* speak. The third was he who was called *Erus*, either the son of *Armenius* according to *Clement of Alexandria*, or the son of *Oromazus* according to *Plato*: for I do not think these *Eri* were different people, as *Francesco Patrizi* judged. *Suidas* seems to confuse the first with the second, and does not suggest a fourth. *Arnobius* commemorates four: that first *Chaldean*; the second the *Bactrian*; the third a *Pamphylian* by race, who was also called *Erus*; the fourth an *Armenian* by race, not the son of *Armenius* whom *Clement* mentions, but another, the grandson of *Hosthanes*. *Suidas* perhaps mentions a fifth when he mentions *Zoroaster* the Astronomer...

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III.

<margin>d Book 2, ch. 14. He affirms the same in ch. 16.</margin>
...mentions him, and says he was a Persomedian <note>A person of Persian and Median descent</note>. Pliny also makes mention of Sextus of Proconnesus. All of these were Magi. Likewise, Lactantius,<sup>d</sup> writing about demons, says: <margin>e Book on the Vanity of Idols, ch. 16.</margin> "The entire art and power of the Magi consists of the inspirations of these [demons]," etc. Saint Cyprian asserts the same<sup>e</sup> and adds that Hosthanes was the chief of the Magi. To him, Tertullian adds other famous names in these words: <margin>f Book on the Soul, second to last chapter.</margin> *Hosthanes, Typhon, and Dardanus, and Damigeron, and Nectabis, and Berenice, are now part of public literature, which...* etc. Arnobius agrees with these, if you punctuate the words correctly,<sup>g</sup> as do Eusebius,<sup>h</sup> Saint Augustine, and Marcellinus. <margin>g Book 1 against the Gentiles.</margin> <margin>h Eusebius, Book 10 on the Preparation; Augustine, Book 12 on the City of God; Marcellinus the historian, Book 23.</margin> Suidas mentions Zoromazdis. Pliny mentions Azonaces and makes him the teacher of Zoroaster. He corrupted the name of Noah, for from him Ham and his son Cush were taught "good magic." There were also writings on magic by Julian the Chaldean, a philosopher, in his fourth book on demons, and by the author of the *Theurgic Oracles*, his son, who was called Julian the <term>Theurgist</term> <note>A practitioner of "high magic" intended to invoke the divine</note> for that reason; concerning these, Suidas and Proclus should be consulted. Porphyry cites a certain Pallas, and Symbolum, and others, namely Adelphius, Acylinus, Alexander the Libyan, Philocomus, Demostratus the Lydian, Zostrianus, Nicotheus, Allogenes, and Mesus; the Gnostics used their impious writings. Pico <note>Pico della Mirandola, the famous Renaissance philosopher</note> also testifies that he read books written in Chaldean by the magi Ezra and Melchiar. Of these, only the <term>Chaldean or Magical Oracles</term> <note>original: "χαλδαικὰ seu μαγικὰ λόγια"</note> of Zoroaster survive today, which Francesco Patrizi laboriously collected from the writings of the Platonists—works of greater obscurity than utility. However, God bestowed natural and legitimate <term>Magic</term> <note>original: "Magice"</note> upon Adam along with the other sciences; from him, a learned posterity propagated it through their hands across the world.

<margin>i Psellus, Book on Demons; Proclus, Book on Magic.</margin>
**A** This magic (as Psellus<sup>i</sup> and Proclus observed) is nothing other than a more exact knowledge of the secrets of nature. By observing the course and influence of the heavens and stars, and the <term>sympathies and antipathies</term> <note>The hidden natural attractions and repulsions between objects</note> of individual things, objects are applied to one another at the proper time, place, and manner. In this way, wonderful things are accomplished which seem like illusions or miracles to those ignorant of the causes. For example, when Tobias cured his father's blindness with the gall of a fish,<sup>k</sup> <margin>k Tobit 7.</margin> a power which most, following Galen,<sup>l</sup> attribute to the Callionymus fish; <margin>l Galen, Book 10 on Simple Medicines; Pliny, Aelian, and others.</margin> likewise, when the sound of a drum made of wolf-skin bursts another drum stretched with lamb-skin;<sup>m</sup> <margin>m Oppian, Book 3 on Hunting.</margin> or when a certain Turk in Venice washed his hands in molten lead without harm;<sup>n</sup> and also those illustrious examples narrated by Saint Augustine in Book 21 of *The City of God*, chapters 4, 5, and 6: concerning the flesh of a peacock that does not rot; concerning chaff which, by its coldness, preserves snow, yet by its heat ripens fruit through a contrary power; concerning lime which, having a fiery power within it, is ignited by water but not by oil; concerning the salt of Agrigentum, which melts in fire <margin>n Cardanus, Book 6 on Subtlety; Boisteau, History of Prodigies, ch. 8.</margin> but hardens and crackles in water; concerning the magnet, the fountain of Epirus, and similar things. To these, other Fathers add:<sup>o</sup> <margin>o Tertullian, Book on Repentance, last chapter; Pacianus, Book on Repentance; and St. Jerome on Ecclesiastes.</margin> the dittany herb, with which a stag expels arrows; and celandine, with which swallows give sight to their blind chicks. Pliny confirms<sup>p</sup> that the Psylli people use the scent of Mars to put serpents to sleep and provoke them with impunity; and that the herb of the woodpecker<sup>q</sup> <margin>p Book 7, ch. 2.</margin> <margin>q Book 10, ch. 10.</margin> expels driven wedges—from which the common fable grew that it opens locks. Alexander ab Alexandro confirms<sup>r</sup> the venom of the Tarantula or Calabrian spider; for those bitten by it, there is no other protection for life or remedy than being compelled to dance to a certain musical sound and rhythm until they collapse exhausted. I pass over the innumerable things gathered by Aristotle (if he is the author of the book *On Marvelous Things Heard*) and William of Auvergne in his very learned work *On the Universe*,<sup>s</sup> and among the Belgians, Robert Triezius, author of the book *On the Deceptions of Demons*<sup>t</sup>; <margin>r Book 2, Genial Days.</margin> <margin>s Part 2, principal section 3, ch. 23.</margin> <margin>t Chapter 4.</margin> however, in these two authors, certain things are narrated that are deservedly suspected of a Satanic pact, such as William's claim that the Heliotrope plant makes men invisible, and that incantations are hindered by quicksilver placed between two reeds. Or as Robert teaches, that rue stolen by theft and basil planted with insults grow more abundantly; likewise, that seven stalks of a certain small herb thrown into a banquet provoke all the guests to brawls and the battles of the Lapiths. No less frivolous are the things Lodovico Dolce records concerning the power of gems...

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

<margin>
Siren. book 9 on Fate, ch. 6. Fracastoro, book on Sympathy & Antipathy. Johann Lang, epistle 33.
</margin>
<margin>
book 8 of Antiquities.
</margin>
<margin>
Theophylact on Matthew; Justin, Question 24 to the Orthodox.
</margin>
...that through these gems, whatever a human can imagine can be achieved. *Book on Gems, ch. 1.* Oh, what vain exaggeration! Are gemstones omnipotent? Far more solid and safer examples can be sought from Sirenius, Fracastoro, and Lang. "There is no doubt that King Solomon was most skilled in this Natural Magic." However, the things that Flavius Josephus <note>A 1st-century Jewish historian</note> reported regarding diseases cured by that same King through incantation are either false, or must be referred to that time when he immersed himself in idolatry and every superstition. Some wish to believe—and I could believe it—that the three Magi of the Gospel were imbued with this Natural Magic. But whether they used this alone, and not also Demonic magic before they adored Christ the Lord, is a matter of debate; certainly, after that, they were free from this crime; what they were before matters little. Indeed, Theophylact <note>original: "Theophylactus"</note> judged that they were even imbued with the forbidden kind. What of Apollonius of Tyana? Justin Martyr seems to number him more among the Natural Philosophers <note>original: "Physicis"</note>: but the things which Eusebius wrote so damningly against Hierocles lead me to believe more frequently that he made use of the devil's work.

<margin>
* Miguel Medina, book 2 on Right Faith in God, ch. 7, from the books of Agrippa.
</margin>
Natural Magic is divided into the operative and the divinatory. The latter is occupied with hidden things and the guessing of the future, about which we must discourse at length in the following book: the former produces wonderful effects, which is what we are dealing with now. Many have published books on it, and regarding those who offer a bare catalog * without an antidote, one must see to it that they do not offer poison in the golden cup of Babylon to the unwary. First, I remove from this number, as demonic Magicians: *Robert the Searcher* <note>Robertus Perscrutatorer, a medieval Englishman</note> whose *Ceremonial Magic* and *Book on the Mysteries of Secrets* Agrippa had read and from which he took not a few things; *Picatrix the Spaniard*, *Anselm of Parma*, *Cecco d'Ascoli*, *Peter d'Abano*, and *Cornelius Agrippa*, and *Paracelsus*, and the unnamed author of the pestilential book *Arbatel* <note>original: "ארבעתאל"</note>, who is a marvelous hypocrite; these men are partly atheists, partly heretics. *Al-Kindi*, *Roger Bacon*, and *Geber the Arab* abound in many superstitions, therefore I would think these also should be among the forbidden reading. *Raymond Lull* and *Arnold of Villanova* are themselves also noted for heresy in some matters

<margin>
aa See the Directory of Inquisitors by Eymerich and Mariana, book 14 on Spanish Affairs, ch. 9, and book 15, ch. 4.
</margin>
aa: wherefore no one ought to read their books who is not thoroughly skilled in Theological dogmas. In the same class should be referred *Thomas Bungay’s* *Book on Natural Magic*, and a book by the same title by *George Ripley*, who after death was judged to have been a necromancer. The *Books on Poisons* by *Poncetus and Ardoinus* should not be read except by physicians of a fearful and pious conscience: for others, that reading is unsafe. In *Cardano’s* *On Subtlety* and *On Variety*, a snake often lies hidden in the grass, and they need the purging of the Church’s file <note>The "file" refers to censorship or correction</note>. *Giambattista della Porta*, under the beautiful title of *Natural Magic*, strives to veil many superstitious and illicit magical acts, for example, on the making of the witches' ointment, and similar things. Regarding *Antoine Mizauld’s* book on *Natural Magic*, I have nothing to say because I have not yet read it: I suspect, as he does in his *Centuries of Memorable Things*, that he has not separated superstitious things from Natural ones. The *Book of Wonders* attributed to *Albertus Magnus* is filled with vanity and superstition; but it is a spurious offspring for so great a Doctor. *Cecco d'Ascoli’s Commentary on the Sphere of Sacrobosco* is to be placed among superstitious writings. As for *Pomponazzi’s* short work *On Incantations*, I have certainly wondered that it was tolerated by the Church for so long; now recently, and deservedly, it is condemned in the Roman Index <note>The Index Librorum Prohibitorum, the list of prohibited books</note>, for it is most true what was written by Antonio Mirandulano

<margin>
bb book 6 on the Singular Combat.
</margin>
bb that in this work Pomponazzi showed himself to be neither a good philosopher nor, what is more foul, a good Christian; since he ascribes all miraculous effects to the turnings of the heavens, even to the point that he wishes religions and laws and their givers to depend upon them. Which is utterly impious. Certain people published, under the name of *Gianfrancesco Pico, Count of Mirandola*, *Magical Positions*, in which there are certainly things that even *Bodin* deservedly rebuked as harmful superstition. What of that censor himself? He filled his own *Demonomania* with many errors, nor did those who published it later in Antwerp purge it as was fitting. Many harmful things remain, which sufficiently testify to the author's ambiguous faith and could harm readers.

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III.

For which reason, and by the best right, that book is listed among the forbidden works by the Roman Inquisitors. Concerning the *Theater of Universal Nature* by the same Bodin <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596), a French jurist whose works on demonology and natural philosophy were highly controversial.</note>, if God grants me life, I shall show elsewhere that there is more of Rabbinic delirium in that body of work than solid philosophy; many things also so clash with Theological decrees that he who wishes to speak of them more leniently is forced to call them erroneous and utterly rash. But more on that elsewhere. It is so slippery and dangerous to discourse on these matters unless you always follow God, the Catholic faith, and the censure of the Roman Church as your <term>Cynosure</term> <note>original: "Cynosuram"; literally the "Dog's Tail" or North Star, meaning a guiding principle.</note>. These things have been briefly recounted by me as a warning for the young, not to insult learned men. Now let us discuss certain doubts upon which the decision of the following matters depends.

## QUESTION I.

### *What the aspect and influence of the heavens contribute to Magical effects.*

The chief head of all superstitions rests upon this foundation of the ancient Magi; concerning whom Psellus <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine scholar who wrote extensively on demons and the Chaldean Oracles.</note> explains the <note>original: "λόγια Ζωροάστρου"</note> Oracles of Zoroaster in this way: "They establish Magical principles from the highest powers and from earthly materials. They say that the higher things have sympathy for the lower, and especially for sublunary things." Following these men, more recent writers want whatever they themselves perform through characters, figures, and numbers to be produced by a power communicated to those figures by other higher figures, out of a certain natural sympathy of similar things among themselves, insofar as these figures are fitted to one another with precision. This they confirm by more than one likeness. First, just as an Echo is caused by an opposing wall; second, just as rays collected in a concave mirror inflame an opposing body by reflection; third, just as a lute <note>original: "cithara"</note> with its strings tensioned in the same way as another; if you strike one, it forces the other to sound along with its own ringing: thus they contend that these similarities of images, numbers, and characters have a marvelous efficiency. But these things are proved to be utterly frivolous.

<margin>a *Peregrine Questions*, Book 1, Decade, chapter 9, principal question, page 5, questions 1 and 2.</margin>
First, because those similar images which they devise in the heaven are entirely fictitious, and what philosophers call <term>beings of reason</term> <note>original: "entia rationis"; things that exist only in the mind, not in physical reality.</note>; insofar as a certain likeness of shape and an image of a picture is ascribed to them, and names imposed at will for the sake of distinguishing the stars from one another, as Bartholomew Sibyllanus <gloss>a</gloss> well noted; and it is evident from the images and pictures of the Astronomers. For who does not know that there are no true bears, snakes, lions, bowls, ships, or Pegasuses in the sky; but that we imagine these only for the sake of speaking? Truly, there can be no efficiency in a fictitious figure as such, nor any sympathy or natural love with a true figure: for that which does not exist, what effect can it produce in that which does exist?

Furthermore, those comparisons contribute nothing at all to the matter. In an Echo, all things are real beings: the man speaking, the reflected voice, the reflecting wall; yet there is no likeness of the wall to the man, as they say there is of those images. In those burning mirrors, too, all things are placed as actually existing, nothing is made up: nor is there any likeness of form between the extreme points, the burning sun and the wood which is burned. In lutes, however, an evident agreement is required on the part of the object: the same mode of tension, the harmony of the strings, the proper proximity of the lutes, and a similar rippling of the air which draws out the sound as if with a plectrum.

Finally, who ever revealed the powers and similarities of such figures and characters to those triflers? What testimony of revelation do they bring forward? If they praise the Chaldeans, Egyptians, and Platonists, I will ask again: from whom did those men receive what surely no mortal was able to attain by study or experiment alone?

Besides, the magi confess that certain images are made not according to the likeness of those celestial ones we mentioned, but according to the likeness of the thing which the operator conceives in his mind—

<keywords>Jean Bodin, astrology, sympathy, celestial images, Michael Psellus, Zoroaster, beings of reason, natural magic, refutation</keywords>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

[he] conceived they should be fashioned; for example, if he desires to set two people against each other, he orders images of two men fighting to be carved. If, however, he wishes to procure love, he orders images of two people embracing. If something favorable is sought, it is inscribed on the front surface of the icon; but if something unlucky is desired, it is inscribed on the back or reverse side, according to what you wish to happen. They claim that Nectanebus <note>Nectanebus II was the last native pharaoh of Egypt, often portrayed in medieval legend as a powerful sorcerer.</note> was the inventor of these images, and that Albert <note>Referring to Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century scholar to whom many magical texts were falsely attributed.</note> approved of them in his *Mirror* <note>original: "Speculo"; likely the *Speculum Astronomiae*.</note>. All these things prove themselves to be vanity and <margin>b below, chapter 1, question 1</margin> will later be refuted. Yet, from these ravings, they proceed to sow even greater delusions.

<margin>B</margin>
First, if you wish to bring sickness or health to someone, they say one must first ensure a planet is chosen that corresponds to the person's age: for example, Saturn for an old man, Mercury for a boy, and Jupiter for a man in his prime. Then, one must also choose a planet corresponding to the emotion or quality to be induced, such as Mars for hatred or Venus for love. Finally, one must choose the zodiac sign that presides over the part of the body you wish to help or harm—for instance, Aries for the head or Pisces for the feet. They rely on this reasoning because not only the Hebrews and Greek pagans, but even some of the Catholic Fathers of old and more recent <margin>c Archangelus Mercenarius, Balduinus de Monte Aureo, Paulus Pernumia.</margin> Italian philosophers <note>The author cites specific Renaissance thinkers who debated the "world soul" or the intelligence of stars.</note> attributed an intelligent soul to the celestial bodies.

<margin>C</margin>
But all these claims are as foolish as they are false and dangerous. In the first place, to say that the heavens or the stars are endowed with senses or an intelligent soul was condemned long ago by the Synod of Constantinople and the Articles of Paris. This opinion is full of error and scandal because of the superstition and idolatry attached to it, as Eck and Espence <margin>d Eck in his book on Predestination; Espence in his book on the Animation of the Heavens.</note> <note>Johann Eck and Claude d'Espence were prominent Catholic theologians who defended orthodoxy against both Protestantism and pagan revivals.</note> have most copiously taught. As for the Fathers who seem to have asserted this, they spoke of the "soul of the heaven" figuratively and allegorically, just as Holy Scripture itself does. My colleague Juan Maldonado <margin>e in the first book of Sentences.</note> <note>A famous Jesuit biblical scholar (1533–1583).</note> taught me that all passages in Scripture where this is hinted at must be understood in one of five ways:

*First*, through *Prosopopoeia* <term>Personification</term>, by which the traits of living beings are attributed to the inanimate; such as the sea "seeing and fleeing," or the heavens and earth <margin>f Psalm 113:3; Leviticus 26:34; Deuteronomy 32:1.</margin> "hearing" and "pleasing," etc. *Second*, through *Metonymy*, where the container is put for the contained; as when the "earth" is said to be corrupt, or to be judged by God, or when it is said it will be more <margin>g Genesis 6:11; 1 Chronicles 16:33; Matthew 11:22.</margin> tolerable for a certain "city." *Third*, through *Hyperbole* <term>Exaggeration</term>, as when stones are said to be about to "cry out," or the earth is said to "tremble" at the sight of God, or to "answer" one who questions it. <margin>h Luke 19:40; Psalm 103:7.</margin> *Fourth*, through <margin>i Genesis 1:16; Jeremiah 7:18 & 44:17; Job 15:15 & 38:7; Psalm 32:6; Daniel 4:32.</margin> *Analogy* and *Metaphor*, as when the sun is said to "rule" the day, the moon the night, or the moon is called the "queen of heaven," or when impurity is attributed to the stars, or the stars are said to "praise God," or are called the "powers of the heavens." *Fifth*, not according to truth, but according to the opinion of men, as when <margin>k Zephaniah 1:5 and similar.</margin> we read of the "host of heaven" regarding the stars. By properly applying these rules, all the arguments of Paolo Ricci <note>A Jewish convert to Christianity and philosopher who blended Kabbalah with Christian thought.</note> and similar impious men regarding the animation of the heavens—drawn from Holy Scripture—are dissolved.

<margin>D</margin>
Furthermore, is not that foul analogy of the heavens and stars to specific ages, body parts, and mental affections utterly trifling? Indeed, those who philosophize correctly grant the heavens and stars either no influence over these <margin>l as Pico in book 3 against Astrology; Medina in book 2 on the Right Faith; Buccaferreus in the 2nd book on the Heavens.</note> lower things except for light and motion; or rather, they acknowledge influences that are really distinct from motion and light. Through these, they produce not just heat (for they produce this by motion and light), but also other qualities of the elements which have some power over the temperament of bodily things. But they add that this power must be confined within certain boundaries. First, the heavens can, by themselves, produce inanimate things, mixtures, and elements. Second, the heavens can produce "imperfect" living things <note>Referring to the medieval belief in "spontaneous generation," where insects or small creatures were thought to arise from decaying matter.</note> when they are born from rot, though only by accident; namely, when particular causes have so disposed the matter that it is a fit "seed" for a living thing, then the heaven bestows greater heat upon this temperament by its influence and renders it more perfect. Third, they agree that regarding "perfect" living things <note>Higher animals and humans.</note>, celestial bodies cannot contribute to their generation in any way other than by assisting a particular cause of the same species as the thing being born. In this sense, the "sun and man" are said to beget a man. All of which...

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III. QUESTION I.

These views are more widely accepted among the Peripatetic schools <note>The followers of Aristotle, who emphasized observation and natural philosophy.</note> <margin>m Against Avicenna, Andreas Caesalpinus, and others.</margin> than among certain others.^m Fourthly, regarding the affections of the soul, I would easily grant that since these usually follow the disposition of the body, even the stars—by their influence and through the mediation of the body's temperament—may incline a person somewhat. Nevertheless, the freedom of choice always remains intact for the human being. <margin>n Antonio Mirandola, book 22 on singular contests; Giorgio Veneto, Song 1, book 3 of *The Harmony of the World* from chapter 7; Veracruz in book 1 of *Meteorology*; and Valesius on *Sacred Philosophy*, chapter 31.</margin> Many highly learned writers of this age^n discuss and prove these matters more extensively; I believe they should be consulted by those for whom these remarks are not sufficient. However, nothing in these points confirms that fiction of the astrologers, because the heavens and stars are only common and universal causes, insofar as they preserve lower things from being dissolved by their opposites. Thus, only indirectly and mediately do they bring aid to particular agents for their actions; but they can by no means concur immediately with those same particular causes in acting. From this it also follows that when they exert influence, they cannot influence only one single part of the body without simultaneously influencing the others.

<margin>o Giovanni Francesco Pico [della Mirandola] in the aforementioned work; Pererius, book on *Divine Astrology*.</margin> Therefore, such observations of the "astrologer-magicians" are rightly rejected by skilled physicians, as among others by Battista Condroncho in *On Venomous Diseases*, book 2, chapter 6; and long ago they were condemned by the decrees of the Church, as has been shown by Pico and Benedict Pererius.^o <margin>p In the *Hexameron* <note>A commentary on the six days of Creation.</note>.</margin> Rightly did Saint Basil^p write that it is ridiculous to contradict astrologers, since their sayings are all full of ignorance and impiety. <margin>q 1st part of the *Centiloquium*.</margin> And Saint Bonaventure^q stated that this observation is cursed by God and forbidden by the Church; and that those things procured through the stars are the illusions and deceptions of demons. <margin>r Book *On Idolatry*, before the middle.</margin> This was the reason why Tertullian^r reckoned astrology among the species of Magic; Michael Glycas also, in the first part of his *History*, inveighs against "Mathematicians" <note>In this period, "mathematicians" often referred specifically to astrologers or casters of horoscopes.</note> of this kind.

They object regarding "Critical Days," which physicians observe when reducing blood <note>The practice of bloodletting.</note> and providing potions, on the grounds that on such days certain Planets suitable for repelling that sickness are dominant. But this reason which they bring forward is neither true nor does it serve their purpose. For come now, name a specific planet that dominates on the first critical day. For example, let the day be tomorrow, over which the Moon presides. Suppose there are seven sick people who fell into illness on seven consecutive days; surely then there will be seven consecutive critical days. For the first day will be the seventh critical day of the first patient, the second for the second, and so on for each of the seven others in their order; yet the Moon will preside over all seven continuous days. Nothing could be more absurd. Therefore, a truer cause must be sought from the property of the humor <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) believed to govern health.</note> then following in the body. For some humors are so swift and sharp that they attack the subject every single day; others on the second day; there are those on the third, and those on the fourth (from which the differences in Fevers flow); some slower ones return only on the seventh or eighth day. The days of these attacks provide physicians with a conjecture as to what should be hoped for regarding the patient's health. Hence they are called "decisive" or "critical" days. Therefore, this offers no support to those "heaven-dwellers" <note>A mocking term for astrologers.</note>.

**F** Let the conclusion be from the Article of Paris, XXI: *That images made of metal or wax, or other material, fabricated under certain constellations, or formed with a certain character or figure, or even baptized, exorcised, or consecrated—or rather execrated—according to the aforementioned arts and under certain days, should have miraculous virtues such as are recited in superstitious books of this kind, is an error in faith, in natural philosophy, and in true astronomy.* The conclusion is proven. This is repugnant to the Faith because it is the sin of idolatry to expect effects from a demon or constellations which proceed from God alone. It is also blasphemous and sacrilegious to defile the rites of the sacraments and sacred things with such superstitions. These things are repugnant to physics and astronomy, because the latter does not recognize such influences of the stars, nor does the former attribute such effects to natural causes. For these figures have neither the power to elicit anything from the stars, nor do they induce any new mixture of qualities or any essential form into the subject...

The stars have no relevance to this figure, and the figure itself has no power over things it could not affect before—unless perhaps to stamp wax or another soft material. Even then, it does so not because it is shaped in this or that way, but because it is harder and has sharp corners. Many sober-minded men have seen this, even if they do not profess it clearly. Certain legal scholars assert the same, as do many theologians following St. Thomas Aquinas. <margin>c Chapter 72 of *The Guide for the Doubting* <note>original: "Ductoris dubitant." referring to Maimonides</note>; Martin de Arles' book *On Superstitions*; St. Thomas, *Summa Theologica* 2.2. question 96, article 2; *Against the Gentiles* book 4, chapters 104 and 105, and the commentaries there by Ferrariensis and Silvester under the word "superstition," question 10; Vairus, book 2 *On Enchantment*, chapter 14; Pierre le Loyer, book 1 *On Specters*.</margin> Laurentius Ananias has many things worth reading regarding these images in Book 4 of *The Nature of Demons*, starting from page 168.

### QUESTION II.

->*Can magical effects of this kind, which resemble miracles, arise from a person's natural physical temperament?*<-

<margin>a Ananias, aforementioned book 4; and Sibyllanus, Decade 3 of *Foreign Questions*, question 3, chapter 8.</margin>
Those who have judged this to be the case were not lacking in the past, nor are they lacking today. Avicenna <note>A famous Persian physician and philosopher (980–1037 AD)</note> indeed attributed this to man by reason of the soul. For he believes that all things consisting of matter—celestial and terrestrial, simple and mixed—obey an intellect that is well-disposed and elevated above matter. If this were said regarding moral obedience, it would be insane, because such obedience belongs only to those endowed with reason. If it is said regarding natural obedience, it is hardly philosophical. For between a spiritual and a corporeal creature, or even from one species to another species, there is no natural subjection, except by reason of action and passion <note>The Aristotelian concept of "acting" upon something and "being acted upon."</note>, whereby that which is weaker yields to the stronger. But that "Universal" obedience which the Arab <note>Avicenna</note> seems to suggest is owed to God alone by reason of His omnipotence, and is exhibited by His creatures. Rightly, therefore, do the theologians refute this delirium. ᵃ

<margin>b So say Fulginas, Forlivius, Garbus, and other physicians.</margin>
Others grant this power to a certain special temperament of the body. For they devise a certain "temperament of equality" resulting from an equal mixture of the humors and active qualities according to weight. ᵇ The reckless assert that whoever is endowed with this can perform miracles; and, so you may see that impiety does not stop at one level, they add a blasphemy: that by the force of this temperament, CHRIST the Lord shone forth with so many miracles. Thus say not only the impious Mages, <margin>c Agrippa, Paracelsus, and the *Conciliator* Difference 20, who nevertheless criticized Fulginas on that point. d Aforementioned book 2, chapter 7, from page 66.</margin> ᶜ but also the physician Fulginas.

But that this is all utterly ridiculous is shown by the physician Condronchus in book 2 of *On Diseases from Sorcery*, chapter 4. And Michael Medina ᵈ has taught so accurately that it is impious, that I would rather refer you there than compile the writings of others by adding or changing nothing. It has always seemed to me unphilosophical to devise such a <term>eukrasia</term> <note>A perfect balance of bodily humors</note>, or (as they call it) a "justiciary temperament" of the human complexion; for if it is not impossible, it is certainly superfluous.

From this it follows that no one is born by nature either a physician or an <term>enchanter</term> <note>original: "fascinatorem," one who casts the "evil eye" or bewitches by sight</note> of others. For if this belonged to the species by reason of nature, everyone would be such; if it does not belong to the species, tell me (if you please) what are those individual conditions which <margin>e Book 3 of *On Enchantment*, chapter 6.</margin> would naturally grant this to a specific person? But see Leonardo Vairus on this matter. ᵉ

### QUESTION III.

->*How great and of what kind is the power of the imagination regarding these wondrous effects?*<-

Many have written much concerning the power of the imagination, such as Pico della Mirandola in his book *On Imagination*, Marsilio Ficino in book 13 of *Platonic Theology* and in his commentaries on Priscianus Lydus regarding fantasy and the intellect, and Antonio Mirandola in book 29 of *Singularities*.
