<summary>This page refutes the idea that magical or miraculous powers stem from a person's physical "temperament" or a perfectly balanced soul, arguing instead that such powers are not natural to human biology. The author specifically attacks the blasphemous claim that Christ's miracles were merely the result of a superior physical constitution.</summary>
<keywords>Avicenna, physical temperament, humors, miracles of Christ, eukrasia, enchantment, imagination, Pico della Mirandola, Marsilio Ficino, scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the power of the imagination, specifically focusing on how it affects the body of the person imagining.</meta>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III.

...certain. <note>The author lists several authorities here: Tostatus on Genesis 30; Miguel de Medina on right faith in God; Vairus on fascination; Valesius on sacred philosophy; Pererius on Genesis 30; and the Coimbra commentators on Aristotle's Physics.</note>

On this point all agree: the power of the imagination is very great. Because this power can be considered either in relation to the body of the person imagining, or in relation to the body of another, each must be investigated separately. All agree that regarding the person's own body, the imagination can achieve a great deal. This is taught by both reason and experience. By reason, because the imagination, while it recalls the images of objects perceived by the senses, stirs the <term>appetitive power</term> <note>The faculty of the soul that desires or moves toward what is perceived as good and away from what is perceived as bad.</note> toward fear, shame, anger, or sadness. These affections affect a person such that they are altered by heat or cold—turning pale or blushing, leaping up in excitement, or becoming sluggish and dejected. Therefore, **St. Thomas Aquinas** most excellently taught that the imagination can affect everything in the person's body that has a natural coordination with the imagination. Examples include local movement while sleeping, or alterations through heat and cold and the consequences of such changes. However, it holds no power over other dispositions of one's own body that lack this natural coordination, such as the shape of a hand or foot, or adding a span to one's height, and similar things. <note>See St. Thomas, Summa Theologica, Part 3, Question 13, Article 3; and Against the Gentiles, Book 3, Chapter 103.</note>

Daily experience is seen in those **Sleepwalkers** <note>original: "Noctambulis"</note> who do marvelous things in their sleep; it is well established that these things happen through the imagination while the senses are dormant. The learned **Libavius** has written a book on this subject. I would think this happens chiefly in those whose imagination is more vehement, who are prone to sharper disturbance and more tenacious apprehension. It is enough that they have at some time perceived through some sense the things they imagine—either the whole thing together, or its parts separately, as when someone envisions a **Chimera** <note>A mythical creature composed of parts from different animals.</note> or a **Tragelaph** <note>A "goat-stag," a legendary creature used in philosophy to describe something that can be imagined but does not exist in nature.</note> or similar things. For I do not think it necessary that they perceive it with their senses at the same moment, nor that they perceived it just before, nor even that they perceived it in the exact manner that the imagination represents it.

I will relate an event that took place in **León** <note>A city in Spain.</note> in a certain religious college barely twenty years ago; I know the persons to whom it happened. There was a lay brother who used to spend his days teaching children the basics of the Catechism. These same thoughts would recur to him while sleeping, so that in his dreams he would lead the singing, teach, exhort, and scold the boys just as loudly and fervently as he did during the day; thus he disturbed the sleep of his neighbors. Another lay brother who lived near him had often warned him. One day, he jokingly threatened that if he continued these shouts, he would get up at night, go to his room, and drive away these "fits" with a rope whip. What did our **Gonzalo** (for that was his name) do? He rose around midnight while sleeping and went out just as he had lain down, in his tunic. He entered the room of his fellow brother, carrying scissors in his hand and feeling his way forward, heading straight for the bed of the one who had threatened him. 

See the providence of God: the moon was shining, the night was cloudless, and that brother was awake. He saw him approaching and quickly threw himself from the bed toward the wall, which was somewhat further away. The sleepwalker, reaching the bed, struck the mattress three or four times with the scissors and immediately returned to where he had come from. In the morning, when questioned, he denied remembering it, nor had he ever even slightly intended to do such a thing. He had only thought that if the other man approached him with a whip, he would terrify him and drive him away by brandishing the scissors. Therefore, it is not necessary for the sense perception of an external thing to have immediately preceded the action when the imagination is working. However, it is necessary that there be some sensory basis for these things in nature, and that the person was not deprived of that sense from birth. For this reason, one blind from birth cannot imagine colors, nor one deaf from birth imagine sounds. <margin><sup>a</sup> Aristotle, *On the Soul*, Book 3.</margin> For imagination is a movement produced by the senses in action, according to **Aristotle** in *On the Soul*, Book 2. It is enough...

<summary>This page discusses how the imagination physically affects the body of the person imagining, using the example of a sleepwalking monk in León to show how internal thoughts can trigger complex physical actions without immediate external stimuli.</summary>
<keywords>imagination, sleepwalking, psychology, St. Thomas Aquinas, Aristotle, León, Gonzalo, physical effects of emotion</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the powers of the imagination, specifically whether the human soul can influence external objects or other people through mental force alone.</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

...even as we have at some time perceived with our senses certain likenesses of the things we imagine; for in this way we are able to imagine either that same thing or something similar. Aristotle admits in his *book on Divination* <note>original: "lib. de Diuinatione"</note> that our imagination is often moved by external things—even those very far removed from us in time or place—and that it foretells things to us which later certainly come to pass. Nor does it seem absurd to me that future events, before they happen, sometimes send forth certain signs of their coming which move the air surrounding us; once the air is moved, our senses are moved, and thus the motion of the sense is a kind of preparation for the imagination itself. For the preparation of the imagination, a motion of the sense is always necessary; but for the imagination itself, we require no further motion of the senses. This is undoubtedly the opinion of St. Isidore, St. Jerome, and Thomas More, who will be cited shortly; nor do the arguments of Andrea Cesalpino prove anything more in *book 5 of Peripatetic Questions, final chapter*, where he contends that the imagination can be moved by external things without the sense being moved.

<margin>B</margin>
Regarding an external body, some believe that the power of the imagination extends very far, so much so that it can even bewitch <term>fascinate: from the Latin 'fascinare', meaning to cast a spell or the 'evil eye'</term> or heal those far away, move objects from their place, and draw down lightning and rain from the sky. Such is the view of Avicenna in *book on the soul, section 4, chapter 4*, and others, who nevertheless disagree on explaining the efficient cause. Some wish it to be able to do this through certain rays which they dream up (*Al-Kindi in his book on images*); others through spirits expelled from the body by the force of the imagination (*Paracelsus in his book on images; Pomponazzi in his book on incantations, chapters 3 and 4; Andrea Cattani in his book on the causes of marvelous effects; Sibyllanus in chapter 8, question 3*). Others, along with Avicenna and Gentile da Foligno, attribute it to the mere command of a superior soul. Augier Ferrier attributes this to the power of the imagination in *book 2, method 11*, though he does not indicate whether it is by the soul's command or through the flow of spirits; but I think he agrees with Avicenna's opinion.

Nevertheless, let the conclusion be as follows:
> *The human soul can perform no such miracles upon an external body that is separate and distinct from the person imagining, neither through the imagination nor through any other power, nor by means of any rays, spirits, or mental images.* <note>original: "speciebus"</note>

This conclusion is common to the theologians St. Thomas, Francesco Silvestri, Medina, Pico della Mirandola, and Valerius (already cited), and to the physicians Valles, Condronchi, Bokel, Cesalpino, and others.

It is proven because imagination is an "immanent action" <term>immanent action: a philosophical term for an action that stays within the person performing it, like thinking, rather than affecting the outside world</term>; and therefore the imagining soul can imprint no real quality upon a separate object, which would be necessary for it to act upon it. Furthermore, because such rays do not exist; the spirits cannot have such great power; and mental images only have the power of representation. Finally, there is no natural coordination of one soul to another's soul or body. Therefore, that wonder-working trickster Cæsarius Maltese, who in Antwerp in the year 1599 used to cloak certain sorcerous divinations in this way—as if, by the efficacy of his spirit dominating the spirit of another, the other person was forced to choose what he himself wanted—was lying magnificently.

Pomponazzi contends that these spiritual images can produce something real, namely the thing of which they are images, just as Ideas do in the Divine Mind. But this is ignorantly said. For the Ideas in the Divine Mind are substances, not accidents; they are also of a higher order than these spirits, nor do they innovate or change anything in God. In creatures, however, it has never been seen that an imaginary image produces its like. For example, that the image of a horse should beget a horse; or that the image of heat, which I imagine, should warm another person far away from me who is naked and cold. If the imaginative power were worth anything like that, the alchemists would have obtained mountains of gold long ago. Finally, these spirits, if they exist, must be a clearly weak thing; which, when poured out outside the imaginer (like a fetus outside the mother's womb), would be immediately destroyed by the surrounding air. As for the objections of our adversaries, they are either false, or in those cases an action by physical contact was added to the imagination, which is why they pertain to the following conclusion. From this, furthermore, three things are gathered: first, that a bird-catcher cannot, by sight and imagination alone—

<summary>The author argues against the belief that the human mind can physically affect distant objects or people, refuting thinkers like Paracelsus and Avicenna by asserting that imagination is an internal process only.</summary>
<keywords>imagination, fascination, Aristotle, Avicenna, Paracelsus, Pietro Pomponazzi, alchemy, spirits, Cæsarius Maltese, immanent action</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the power of the imagination, specifically addressing whether the mind can influence external bodies or the physical traits of offspring.</meta>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III.

First, that birds cannot be pulled down from the sky <note>original: "aues coelo detrahere"</note>, despite what Michel de Montaigne asserted in *Book 1 of the Essays, chapter 20* <note>The author cites chapter 2, but Montaigne discusses the force of imagination in chapter 20/21 depending on the edition.</note>. Second, that no one can be bewitched <term>fascinari: the ancient belief in "fascination" or the evil eye</term> by the imagination of another alone, as Francisco Vallés accurately shows in *Book on Sacred Philosophy, chapter 68*. Third, much less can another person be healed in this way, as Aristotle rightly notes in his *Problems* and Gratarolo in the works of Pomponazzi, *chapter 6*.

**The second conclusion:** it is likely enough that, by accident, the imagination can affect a neighboring body that is joined to it by some close connection and contact; provided, however, that the imagination is very intense and the neighboring body is highly disposed toward that influence.

In this way, I believe the contrary opinions can be reconciled. One side absolutely denies that the imaginative power can change external bodies, held by Francisco Suárez in *Metaphysical Disputations 18, section 8, verse 28*, the Coimbra commentators on *Physics Book 7*, and others like Vair. The other side absolutely affirms it, such as Miguel de Medina in *On the Right Faith, Book 2*, Marsilio Ficino in *Platonic Theology, Book 13*, and Benito Pereira <note>A prominent Jesuit commentator</note> mentioned above. To prove this, they usually bring forward wonderful examples of the diversity of children from their parents, such as the rustic trick mentioned by Columella in *Book 8*, and alluded to by Calpurnius Siculus in his *Eclogue 2*:

> Pales herself <note>The Roman goddess of shepherds</note> teaches me the care of the flock: how a black ram, as the husband of a white sheep, may change the offspring into a white lamb; which can neither keep the appearance of the differing parent, but testifies to both with a mixed color.

We read that the Patriarch Jacob first practiced this in *Genesis 30*. And although St. Chrysostom judged this to have transcended the mode of nature, and Isidore of Seville indicates it was "against nature," this should not be taken as if they deny that this variety of colors in offspring is consistent with natural reason. Rather, it is merely outside the ordinary course of nature. For lambs are usually born similar in color to their parents; but here, by the industry of Jacob—which he was nevertheless taught by an Angel, according to Theodoret—the opposite happened. Isidore was not ignorant that this was consistent with natural reason, for he immediately added the reason and confirmed it with examples; as did St. Jerome, St. Augustine, Galen, and Pliny, all of whose words you can find in our author Pereira. One might add a similar example of white offspring from two Ethiopian parents from Heliodorus’s *Ethiopian Story, Book 1*. And another: that in 's-Hertogenbosch <note>original: "Buscoducis"</note> a masked demon lay with a wife, claiming (as lewd drunks do) that he wished to father a demon; the infant born from that union was similar in face to the demon, and as soon as it was born, it began to act playfully and deceitfully, as told by Antonio de Torquemada in the *Garden of Flowers*. But the conclusion is not sufficiently proven by this example and similar ones. For these can be attributed to the imagination of the mother, as St. Isidore attributes them in these words: *“For they say this is the nature of women: that whatever they look upon or think of in their minds at the height of passionate pleasure while they conceive, such is the offspring they procreate. For the soul, during the sexual act, transmits external forms inward, and being saturated by these patterns, it snatches their appearance into its own quality.”* He clearly took this from St. Jerome, who also attributes the whole power to the mother; as does St. Augustine when discussing the Apis bull in *The City of God, Book 18, chapter 5*. But also Thomas More, the Martyr, in a charming poem—of which Pereira cites the whole, but I will select the part useful to us:

<image-desc>A vertical decorative woodcut border strip separates the following two columns of verse.</image-desc>

> Yet the weighty sages teach:
> That whatever mothers meanwhile
> Imagine strongly
> While they labor for children;
> Of that thing, secretly, both certain
> And indelible marks,
> In an inexplicable way,
> Are gathered into the seed itself:
> Which, being received deep within
> And growing together at once,
> The child reflects the image
> Implanted by the mother’s mind.

And so he claims it happened, that she who had borne her husband four legitimate but dissimilar children...

# MAGICAL INVESTIGATIONS

<margin>Note: Commentary on Genesis, chapter 30.</margin>

because, feeling secure in her husband's absence, she did not think of him; she gave birth to a child conceived in adultery who was very similar to this "cuckoo" <note>original: *curucæ*; a reference to a cuckold or a surrogate bird</note>; this was because while conceiving, she feared her husband might intervene like "the wolf in the fable" <note>a Latin idiom meaning 'speak of the devil' or an unexpected arrival</note>. However, the cause of that dissimilarity could also be that while she was conceiving them, the adulteress was focusing her mind and imagination on others. I would therefore think it possible that this happens due to the woman's imagination alone, although I admit that most often the imagination of both parents concurs, as *Francisco Vallés* <note>A 16th-century Spanish physician</note> teaches in his *book on Sacred Philosophy, chapter 11.* Yet, neither parent then acts through their imagination upon the body of the other. For the imagination works only upon the prolific seed, which was part of the person imagining; therefore, it does not actually imprint such a form (for example, skin color or curliness of hair) upon the soul of the imagining seed—of which the seed is not actually capable—but it confers a certain power, carried from the parent and bestowed upon the fetus it forms. Those things which are present in the generator according to the soul begin to exist bodily in the generated body, which were previously in the soul of the generator spiritually. Read the same *Vallés*, whose opinion I have joined with the explanation of *Tostatus* <note>Alonso Tostado, a prolific Spanish scholar</note> on question 10 of the aforementioned 30th chapter of Genesis.

But is the imagination of the male alone sufficient for this? *Pliny* seems to suggest so in *Book 7, chapter 12*, when he mentions the thought of either parent, and perhaps *Marsilio Ficino* above, when he mentions parents indiscriminately, as does *Calpurnius* in the verses praised earlier. However, I would think they did not mean that, but rather require the concurrence of both; whether the male alone is sufficient is problematic, because it is uncertain what and how much the male or female seed contributes to the matter and conformation of the fetus. If the woman does not emit seed, as *Aristotle* argued in *Books 1 and 2 of the Generation of Animals*; or if the father alone grants form to the living being, as it seems to some; or if the seed of both runs together for both matter and form simultaneously, which pleased the most eminent physicians: then it is probable that the father's imagination suffices. If, however, the father contributes only to the form, and the mother contributes equally to the form and alone supplies the matter, it would be more probable that his is not sufficient. Whatever the case, it is clear that imagination at the time of conception is not a sufficiently effective proof of our conclusion.

The imagination that follows after conception is more pressing. For we see that even a child already formed in the womb feels the costs of the mother's imagination, as happens daily among pregnant women. I read of a citizen of Wittenberg born with a corpse-like face because his mother, while carrying him in her womb, had been suddenly terrified by encountering a corpse. In Eisenach, they write that a modest and beautiful matron gave birth to a dormouse; because one of the neighbors had tied a bell to a dormouse so that the others would be driven away by the sound, it ran into the pregnant woman. Unaware of the situation, she was so terrified by the sudden encounter and sight of the dormouse that the fetus in her womb degenerated into the form of the little beast. Under Pope Nicholas III in Rome, a woman in a certain palace gave birth to an infant resembling a bear, because (as the physicians judged) bears were seen depicted in various places there.

Why do I seek examples from elsewhere? My own maternal and paternal lineage provides them. There was one in my mother's family in Antwerp who, while pregnant, kept a pet monkey; her only daughter retained many traits of monkeys, for even as a little girl she would hide, make gestures, and do many similar things. From my father's side, Martín Antonio Delrio <note>The author refers to himself/his family here</note>, a man distinguished by honors for his faithful service to the King, was captured by rebels in Brussels in the full Senate; his pregnant wife, seeing the leaders of the seditious men bursting into the house, was terrified. She later gave birth to a son, whom I took from the baptismal font: that infant always showed his mother's terror in his wide-staring eyes; now as a young man, he remains of a disturbed mind. I flee from these domestic evils. *Fernel* writes in his *book on Human Procreation* that if a peacock is covered with white linens while incubating eggs, it produces entirely white chicks, not of variegated color. I ask you, are eggs not a separate body? Yet you see the effectiveness of the imagination upon them. Furthermore, since imagination kindles heat in the body of the one imagining, why should a body contiguous to it not also be heated?

I take my hand off the tablet <note>An idiom meaning 'I rest my case' or 'I stop writing'</note>: I only advise, or rather, raise a doubt.

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III. QUESTION IV.

*Saint Augustine relates that a certain man, whenever he pleased, by imitating the voices of someone lamenting, could so withdraw himself from his senses and lie so still that he seemed most like a dead man. Not only did he not feel those pinching or pricking him, but sometimes he was even burned by fire applied to him without any sense of pain, except afterward from the wound. Furthermore, Augustine proves that this man did not move his body—not by resisting, but by not feeling—because, as in a corpse, no breath could be found in him. However, the man later reported that he had heard the voices of people, if they spoke loudly, as if from a great distance.* <note>original: "lib. 14. de Civitat. Dei cap. 23." Referring to Augustine's 'The City of God'.</note> *A learned man wishes this fact to be attributed to a powerful imagination, by which the man abstracted himself from the sense of present things. Consider, reader, whether it is not more likely that this man was a magician, and by the help of a demon through a pact, was accustomed to be snatched away into ecstasy in this manner. For an effect of this kind clearly surpasses all power of the imagination, since ecstasy does not depend on the human will, as will be shown in its proper place. Let us proceed to another question.*

# QUESTION IV.

->Whether by touch, sight, voice, breath, a kiss, or the application of a bare linen cloth alone,<-
->wounds and diseases can be healed, and other such wonders<-
->be naturally performed?<-

<margin>A</margin>
**A**s many as have asserted this, place this common foundation for their superstition: that spirits flow forth from the heart through the artery, and break out through the sight of the one looking, or through the mouth of the one speaking, or through the pores of the one touching; and they insinuate themselves into the arteries of the one seen, heard, or touched, and from there seek the heart, penetrating it all the more effectively the more the spirits were emitted by the vehement will of the one seeing, speaking, or touching.

The first conclusion: *this power is not to be attributed to the voice.* This is proven because the voice can work nothing upon the listener except by accident, by reason of the sound or the meaning of the words, altering the listener by the joy, fear, sorrow, etc., which it brings; as Vairus <note>Leonardo Vairus (1540–1603), an author on charms and fascination.</note> has excellently taught.<gloss>a</gloss>

<margin>a See book 2, chapter 11. Also Condronchus, book 2, on venefic diseases, chapter 5.</margin>
It is no objection what they bring forward: that Magicians perform wonderful effects with evil charms—for example, that by whispering certain words into the ear of a bull, the beast is cast down as if dead, and again by others, it is raised to its feet. Also, that the curses of parents hardly ever fall in vain. I say these do not stand in the way, because those things concerning the bull and similar matters are done by a demon by virtue of a pact. Truly, the effect of curses, if sudden, can arise from a vehement alteration of shame, fear, or sadness; but if it be slow and lingering, it is not to be thought that this is performed by the sound of the words, but that God punishes the wickedness and impiety of children by a just judgment. To this I would refer that story of the Westphalian woman who, by curses and devotions, rendered her son immobile; which others have attributed to the operation of a demon.<gloss>b</gloss>

<margin>b Ananias, book 4, on the Nature of Demons.</margin>
The second conclusion: *neither is vision alone, even with a naked imagination, sufficiently effective for this.*

<margin>B</margin>
Vairus (book 2, ch. 9), Ananias (book 4), Condronchus (book 2, ch. 2), and Bokelius (book on philters, ch. 1) prove this regarding <term>fascination</term> <note>The historical belief that one could harm or influence others through the "evil eye" or a powerful gaze.</note>; but there is an equal reasoning regarding health and similar effects.

The authors of the contrary opinion suppose, with the <term>Platonics</term> <note>Followers of Plato who believed vision occurred by rays shooting out from the eye (extramission).</note>, that the eye sees by emitting something. But how is it that those visual rays were not exhausted long ago, or what is this power that always supplies new matter? Far truer and more accepted is the opinion of the <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle who believed vision occurred by light entering the eye (intromission).</note> and the Theologians: that vision occurs by the reception of a <term>species</term> <note>A philosophical term for the "likeness" or "image" of an object that travels to the senses.</note> representing the object; and that the power of seeing is a passive power, which can be altered, but cannot alter. Vision is also an "immanent action"; once it is finished, nothing remains in the object, since nothing was added to it or passed into it. These things are most true, yet many things at first glance seem to contradict them: love from a mutual gaze, bleary-eyedness, the fascination of old hags, the tainting of mirrors—

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

<note>original: "Disquisitionum Magicarum"</note>

...the effectiveness of the sight of the yellow thrush, the wolf, the ostrich, the rooster, and the basilisk; these must now be briefly dismissed.

Sight is merely a certain occasion for the beginning and growth of love. For in the beginning, sight offers the image of the seen form to the imagination: the phantasy, by turning and returning this image, is the cause why a person judges that object to be more worthy of being loved and desired than all other things. Thus one begins to love: soon love is nourished and increases by the presence of the loved thing—not because the object is seen by the external eyes (for those who are absent are often more tormented by love), but because he thinks of it constantly, and values it more than is right, and by imagining it to himself, he provides fuel and torches for the fire. Indeed, if the evil were not internal and depended only on external vision, why is it that when many see the same woman at once and are seen by her, only one man perishes with love while the others despise her?

Inflammation of the eyes <term>Lippitudo: a chronic redness or watering of the eyes</term> and the spots burned into mirrors by the gaze of a menstruating woman do not flow from the vision itself or the organ of the eye; rather, they come either from infected breath breaking out through the mouth and nose, or from the cavities near the eye, where contagious spirits are mixed with the discharge. For in the eye there is an opening containing the pupil, from which vision proceeds through the optic nerve—a truly harmless action. There is also another opening in the brain from which the motor nerve, born from the second pair of nerves, emerges; through this opening the brain pours out tears, eye-inflammation, and other foul humors and spirits. From there, through the outer parts of the eye and the circumference of the orb itself, between the eyelids and the white membrane, these types of fluids drip down.

The fierce look of old women, with its deformed filth and foulness, can terrify infants. This fear then stirs up the humors and causes diseases to break out more quickly, to which the tender little body was already disposed and prepared.

<margin><unclear>Pliny, Book 30, Chapter 11</unclear></margin>
Plutarch (in Book 5 of the *Symposiacs*, Chapter 7) and Heliodorus (in Book 3 of the *Ethiopian History*) write that the yellow thrush <note>original: "Galgalum," likely the Golden Oriole or a similar bird</note>—also called the rock-dweller or the "gaper"—brings health to a sick person and disease to itself if it fixes its eyes on one suffering from jaundice. If this is true, which Vairus rightly doubts, I would attribute it to some hidden similarity and sympathy between the disease and the little bird, not to its vision. The sight is only the occasion for the alteration that completes the matter.

<margin><unclear>Virgil, Eclogue 9</unclear></margin>
Did the wolves take away Moeris's power of speech because they saw him first? Set aside, good man, the trifles of the poets regarding Moeris. Rather, you should praise Saint Ambrose, who writes: *If wolves see a man first, they seem to take away his voice by a certain force of nature; but if the man sees them first, they are said to be driven into a panic.* (Book 7 on Luke, Chapter 10). But take the cause: Thomas of Cantimpré <note>A 13th-century Flemish priest and natural philosopher</note> wants this to happen because the wolf, by sending rays from its eyes into the man, instantly dries up his visual spirits; once these are dried, the arteries are dried, and thus the instrument of the voice is hindered and bound, making the man hoarse (*On Bees*, Book 2, Chapter 57, page 39). Truly, I do not admit this theory of rays. Therefore, I would prefer to say with others that this happens because those who stumble upon a wild beast unexpectedly are more vehemently afraid due to its flashing eyes and formidable appearance. Sudden fear drives the blood to the heart and brings coldness to the other limbs, causing a sudden alteration of the whole body; hence comes the hoarseness and the hindrance of the voice. Nor does the reasoning of hunters seem vain: namely, that the wolf, if it sees a man first, gathers itself for vengeance and thus breathes out a certain harmful vapor upon him so that he cannot cry out; since this vapor is poisonous, it can cause hoarseness. But when the wolf sees that it has already been caught and seen by the man, it fears for itself and, thinking of flight, does not think of emitting this vapor. Julius Scaliger contends that this whole story about the wolf is false.

Neither the ostrich, nor the slow-moving, house-carrying tortoise hatch their eggs by their gaze: for they leave them in the sands and look at them only so that they may keep [them safe]...

<summary>The author argues that the "evil eye" and other visual phenomena, such as the wolf's ability to silence men or the healing power of birds, are caused by natural physical processes like fear, vapors, or biological sympathy rather than magical rays.</summary>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III. QUESTION IV.

...they [the eggs] are hidden lest they be harmed: later, the kind mother earth matures them with her heat, just as happens with lizards.

<margin>d Ananias and book 4.</margin>
The king of beasts <note>the lion</note> truly fears the sight of a **Rooster**, and this is no trick of demons (as a certain man wrote <gloss>d</gloss>): rather, he fears it out of a certain secret antipathy <note>a natural, unexplained aversion</note>. Thus you may see a weasel, with a toad gaping its mouth, cast itself into the mouth to be devoured as if compelled, with a wondrous wailing.

Regarding the gaze of the kinglet or **basilisk**, the story is better known than it is true. If this animal is found, it infects the neighboring air with a violent and poisonous breath, and this kills those who approach. As for what they add—that if it sees itself in pure and clear water, it dies—to put it briefly, I do not believe it. For if those spirits <note>effluvia or vapors</note> went out from its eyes, they would be natural to the one seeing, and therefore would not kill it; if they were not natural, they ought to have caused harm while previously enclosed in the eyes; but to say they are infected and poisoned by the water is ridiculous.

<margin>C</margin>
I will add to these arguments of Vairus <note>Leonardo Vair, an author on fascination</note> one that seems more difficult to me. The people of Spain know a race of men whom they call **ZAHURIS**; we may call them **Lynxes** <note>referring to the lynx-eyed, or those with supernatural sight</note>. When I was staying in Madrid in the year 1575, such a boy was seen there. They say these people see things hidden in the deepest bowels of the earth: veins of water and metals, treasures, and corpses placed under sarcophagi. This is a most widely accepted and celebrated matter: and not only Pindarus, Tzetzes, and other poets, but also philosophers <gloss>e</gloss> have judged it possible. Some of these ascribe this power to the melancholic humor <note>one of the four bodily fluids thought to influence temperament</note> and to the intensity of the spirits born from it. This reasoning would be better if they only *thought* they saw things not seen; but since the truth corresponds with what is seen, the reasoning is empty. What I feel on this I have explained elsewhere <gloss>f</gloss>, nor do I change my opinion: they know the veins of water from the vapors exhaled in those places in the morning and evening. They know the veins of metals from a certain kind of herb accustomed to grow there. As for treasures and corpses (for they say what and of what sort they are), I would think they are shown and indicated by demons. The sharpness of the eyes can cross vast spaces when no dense body is placed in between; but this medium of sarcophagi or earth is so dense, solid, and opaque that it is either entirely devoid of, or little capable of, the illumination and transparency necessary for seeing. Add to this that these people usually restrict this faculty of seeing to certain days, the third and sixth days of the week <note>Tuesday and Friday</note>, which is a sign of a hidden pact <note>a pact with the devil</note>. Furthermore, the redness of the eyes, which is seen to be very great in the Zahuris, would hinder rather than help the sharpness of sight.

<margin>
e Caelius Rhodiginus, book 16 of *Ancient Readings*, chapter 2, and Vairus, said book 2, chapter 4.
f Commentary on *Medea*, verse 231.
</margin>

<margin>D</margin>
**Third Conclusion: Nor is touch alone sufficient by itself for these effects.** ] Regarding fascination <note>the "evil eye" or magical influence</note>, Vairus and Condronchus prove this, but the arguments equally convince <gloss>g</gloss> regarding other marvelous effects.

<margin>g Said book 2, chapter 4; said book 2, chapter 3.</margin>

First, **Touch**, insofar as it is touch, was given to man for the preservation of his own life, and has no other power except that of touching. If indeed it has some power of performing wonders of this sort, that power must be either healthy by its nature or harmful. If healthy, it would first bring health to its own body; if harmful, it would first kill the one in whom it resides. Yet we see none of these things happening. Furthermore, it would act equally upon all whom it touched; it would heal or even hurt those it did not wish to. Finally, there would be no need for other observations and rituals. We see all these things are otherwise.

I know that multiple medleys of experiments are brought forward against our opinion, but they are of little weight.

First, they report that a **cramp-fish** <note>the torpedo ray</note>, when struck by a pike, numbs the hand and the whole body of the one holding it; that dogs fall silent at the shadow of a **hyena**; that a serpent touched by oak leaves dies; that the same is stopped by the feather of an ibis; that a viper is stunned by the strike of a reed or a beech twig; that bats are driven away by the leaves of a plane tree; that an eryngium <note>sea holly</note> taken in the mouth by a goat stops whole flocks; that other feathers mixed with the feathers of an eagle...

<sig>B 4</sig>

<summary>The author disputes the legendary powers of the basilisk's gaze and the Spanish 'Zahuris' (treasure-seers), attributing the latter to natural observation or demonic pacts rather than supernatural sight. He also begins to argue that physical touch alone cannot account for magical or marvelous effects.</summary>
<keywords>Zahuris, basilisk, fascination, antipathy, Leonardo Vair, Madrid, mineral divining, demonic pacts, torpedo fish, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into whether physical contact alone can produce magical or supernatural effects, specifically addressing counter-arguments based on natural wonders and folklore.</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

...consumed by a mere touch: that some are killed by the sight or touch of a sea-hare <note>original: *lepore marino*; a type of sea slug believed in antiquity to be poisonous to the touch</note>: and that if you join lute strings made of wolf-gut with those of lamb-gut, the latter will snap. *I respond* that all these things occur by reason of a natural antipathy <note>A traditional belief that certain substances have an inherent, "occult" (hidden) hostility toward one another</note>, and by a harmful spreading of hidden qualities from one to the other. Nor does the logic hold if you argue from those cases to the contact of one human touching another. For one human is similar in species to another, and natural antipathy has no place within the same species; those other examples differ in their species.

Secondly, they urge that point of Lucan’s regarding the Sucker-fish <note>original: *Echineide* or *remora*; a fish believed by ancients to be able to stop ships by clinging to the hull</note>:
->*Holding back the stern though the gold-clad cables strain*<-
->*In the midst of the sucker-fish waters.*<-

*I respond* that since this has happened only once or twice, if it happened at all, it occurred by chance from some other cause. Why is it that after so many centuries of such frequent navigation, nothing of this sort is heard of? Certainly the species is not extinct; I saw one at the house of Jacques Plateau of Tournai, and others have them elsewhere. That power of stopping ships was likely a fiction; but because Saints Basil and Ambrose mentioned it in their *Hexameron* <note>Commentaries on the six days of Creation</note>, you may resort to some hidden quality of this little creature which has the power of halting motion. Read, if you please, our Coimbran commentators in their second book on *Physics* <note>referring to the influential Jesuit commentaries from the University of Coimbra</note>.

Thirdly, a struck lute string forces another in unison to ring. *Response:* when both are stretched equally and are not too far apart, they receive the same vibration of the rippling air.

Fourthly, iron rubbed with a Magnet can attract other iron. If you tie a raging bull to a fig tree, he will immediately grow tame. *Response:* both can happen through a certain hidden sympathy and a consensus of qualities. Regarding the magnet, our Coimbrans say much in the cited question; consult them. Regarding the bull, I am not a believer, nor one who wishes to make the experiment; however, I know it is referred to a physical cause by Glycas: that the fig is the most full of sap among trees, and therefore emits a warm breath that cuts and penetrates the air, which tames the ferocity of the tied bull and softens the meat of hung birds.

Fifthly, if Alexander the Great had been intimate with the girl raised on monkshood <note>original: *napello*; a highly toxic plant</note>, the contact would have been lethal to him, in the judgment of Aristotle. *Response:* he would have inhaled this plague not by contact alone, but by the contagion of sweat and mingled breath. But contagion has nothing wonderful or unusual about it.

Sixthly, a corpse usually pours out blood in the presence of its killer, because of the preceding contact. *Response:* Various causes are given. Some attribute it to a miracle; some to chance, whereby it happened at some time that a corpse happened to bleed just when the one accused of homicide was present. I have reviewed many opinions on this matter in another place and praised the authors <sup>h</sup>; and yet I do not see what can be more solidly offered than that "antipathy" arising from the violent hatred of the slain for the killer. This hatred impressed a latent and secret quality upon the body, remaining with the corpse; and to this we should refer that passage of Lucretius:

<margin>h Commentary on Seneca’s *Octavia*, verse 127.</margin>
> *The mind seeks that body from which it was wounded by love.*
> *For men generally fall toward their wound, and the blood*
> *Springs out toward that part from whence we are struck.*
> *And if the foe is near at hand, a red horror seizes him.*

*So that there is a comparison between the living lover and the dead enemy. Thus says Lemnius, Langius, Pictorius, and Gemma.*

Seventhly, various types of cures are objected which are wonderfully frequent. Primarily the daily practice of soldiers, who heal even the most atrocious wounds by breath alone, a kiss, or the application of a bare linen cloth; this they call the *Art of St. Anselm*. Likewise that class of *Healers* <note>original: *Salutatorum*</note>, as they are called in Spain, or *Kinsmen of St. Catherine* or *St. Paul*, as they name them in Italy, or *Sons of Good Friday* as the Belgians call them; who

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III. QUESTION IV.

...who all boast that they are endowed with the gift of healing various diseases. They are supported by the authority of Pomponazzi <note>Pietro Pomponazzi (1462–1525), an Italian philosopher who sought naturalistic explanations for "miracles."</note> in his book *On Incantations*, chapter 3. He responds that just as these healing virtues are found in herbs, stones, and animals, there is nothing to prevent similar powers from being found in the entire human species; thus, in one man there might be the virtue of a certain stone, and in another, that of a certain plant. Therefore, what this plant or that stone can do, this or that man will also achieve by the force of nature. ] This matter is more complicated than can be explained in a single proposition.

Wherefore I say first: The gift of conferring health or healing is a supernatural gift, and a "grace freely given" <term>gratia gratis data: a theological term for spiritual gifts given to an individual for the benefit of others, rather than for their own sanctification</term> by God. ] This must be held with certain faith, from the words of St. Paul $^k$;

<margin>k 1 Corinthians 12, verse 9.</margin>
> *To one indeed is given the word of wisdom through the Spirit; to another the word of knowledge, according to the same Spirit; to another faith in the same Spirit; to another the grace of healing in the one Spirit; to another the working of miracles; to another prophecy; to another the discerning of spirits.* ]

Such a gift of healing—though only for curing fevers—is believed to be held in Flanders by those born on Good Friday <note>original: "ipso die Parasceues"</note>, and by those who are born as the seventh male in a legitimate marriage with no female offspring intervening. This cannot be reduced to a natural cause; therefore, the cure is miraculous and, if no superstition is added, should be referred back to this first conclusion. For it is not unlike the truth that God granted this in honor of the day, the holiness of the Mystery, and the honor of marriage. Nor could it happen except by divine power, as is reported of the Kings of England, who formerly blessed certain rings useful for the contraction of nerves, spasms, and the "falling sickness" <note>epilepsy</note>. It is also said they were accustomed to heal the "King's Evil" <term>strumosos/scrophularum: scrofula, a swelling of the lymph nodes in the neck</term> by touch; Polydore Vergil writes in Book 8 of his *History of England*, page 140, that both were granted through the merits of Saint Edward the King. I do not know if we should also refer to this the testimony of Peter of Blois, Archdeacon of Bath, whose works were published last year, partly anew and partly corrected, through the study of our own Johannes Busæus. Peter of Blois writes:

> *I confess indeed (he says) that it is a holy thing to assist the Lord King; for the King is holy and is the Anointed of the Lord, nor has he received the sacrament of royal unction in vain. If the efficacy of this is unknown or doubted, the disappearance of the groin-plague and the cure of scrofula will provide the fullest proof.* ] Thus he wrote in Epistle 150 to the clerics of the Royal Court.

Two years ago, a quite lengthy book appeared by one William Tooker, titled ***Charisma, or the Gift of Healing***, in which he endeavors to teach that Elizabeth, who reigns today in England, is endowed with this gift of healing scrofula. I received the book as a gift from the Illustrious Man Marcus Welser, a Patrician and Councilor of Augsburg, who, in his great erudition and kindness, did not wish me to be without a share of his generosity. In that book, it seemed to me there was much flattery and deceit, and little sincerity or truth; nor does it deserve belief. Primarily, he wishes this gift to have belonged to the Kings of England from the time of Lucius <note>Lucius of Britain, a legendary 2nd-century king</note> onward, yet he brings forward nothing to prove it. Nor can it be so. For after Lucius, there were many Kings who were not Christians, whom he will not, I think, call "anointed." Therefore, the gift ceased among them and was interrupted. And why does he take away from the merits of St. Edward what others attribute to him? Is it to snatch the splendor of an older privilege from the Kings of France? Or to teach (as he impudently asserts on page 84) that the Kings of France received it through some lineage from the Kings of England? And see how he proves it: because almost all the provinces of the Franks were subjected to the jurisdiction of the Kings of England. A fine reason, and one worthy of being clarified by the French—which I do not doubt they will do, unless they disdain to answer this inept meddler. Nor does he prove that the gift was conferred on Edward's successors just because it was on Edward, though Polydore asserts it; regarding which, who would not rightly doubt? For neither in the bull of canonization of St. Edward, nor in his life written by Aelred of Rievaulx, is it held to be more than that St. Edward healed a certain woman with scrofula; not one word is added about a gift for his successors. This is also true of Tagault, whom Tooker himself cites, even writing out the words of both, so that he is caught in a manifest lie. They only proved it regarding Edward. I add from Peter of Blois the case of Henry II, of whom he was speaking; Polydore adds Henry VII of his own time, and Henry—

# DISQUISITIONS ON MAGIC

Perhaps he refers to Henry VIII, but before he became a schismatic and a heretic; for he had written his History before that time. What if I were to tell *Polydore* <note>Polydore Vergil (c. 1470–1555), an Italian priest and historian who wrote a famous history of England.</note> that I believe him, provided that *Henry VIII* himself did not consider him a most lying writer; and if *Camden* <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a renowned English antiquarian.</note> and all those well-skilled in English affairs did not judge the same? So great a matter cannot be proven by a single witness. Furthermore, *Tooker* <note>William Tooker (1558–1621), chaplain to Queen Elizabeth I, who wrote a book defending her power to heal scrofula by touch.</note> contends that this same gift was not simply granted to *Elizabeth*, but in a much more excellent degree, because she is holier than *Edward* <note>Saint Edward the Confessor, the Anglo-Saxon king traditionally credited with starting the practice of "touching for the King's Evil."</note>. Do not be amazed: for in the Preface and chapters 3 and 4 of that booklet, he prefers her more than once to the Apostles, to all the Saints, and even to the Holy of Holies itself. Finally, by what means does he prove this Queenly miracle? First, on *page 90*, through a certain fictitious dialogue of his with certain Papists, whose names, location, or time of the conversation he does not add, lest he be caught in a lie. Why did you not invent *Sanders*, *Campion*, *Allen*, or *Stapleton* <note>Prominent English Catholic exiles and martyrs who opposed Elizabeth's religious policies.</note>? You would have triumphed gloriously over them. Now I respond that there is a certain person who believes nothing about a certain thing from a certain man. Do you want to know who? I, regarding you, and this entire dialogue. The sum of the dialogue is that he convinced them that the bulls of excommunication brought against *Henry VIII* by *Clement VII* and *Paul III*, and against *Elizabeth* by *Pius V*, were impious; because he could show that Queen *Elizabeth’s* <term>charisma of healing</term> <note>original: "charisma sanationis" — a divinely granted power to heal.</note> remained intact. Upon hearing this, he claims they suddenly fell silent as if struck with consternation—I suppose they were admiring such great impudence, or not daring to deny it for fear of being reported. 

But how does he prove it? First, that he heard from a "trustworthy witness" that a certain man of the Roman faith met him on the road near *London* who confessed that he had been freed from <term>scrofula</term> <note>original: "struma" — a swelling of the lymph nodes in the neck, also called the King's Evil.</note> by the Queen's touch. As if indeed that single witness—neither named, nor sworn, nor mindful of the time or other circumstances—were sufficient for conviction. To these he later adds other witnesses cured at other times, whom he names: four in total, two males and as many females, so that there are two pairs. And these are the *many thousands whom he says she has healed, and that she is willing and able to heal an even more infinite number.* He also tries to prove it from the holiness of the ceremonies; which are suspicious to me, because I see that physicians apply plasters beforehand; and these ceremonies are far different from those which *Tooker* is well aware (from *Aelred*) that *Saint Edward* used. A third proof is deduced from the fact that the Queen is accustomed to impart this grace neither to everyone, nor always, but to some people at certain times, and even then reluctantly (*pages 105 and 106*); likewise, that a large portion of the people cured by the benefit of this grace "relapse," as it were, into the former state of the disease (*ibid. page 106*). This is rather an indication that this gift is not from God, whose works and healings are perfect; on the contrary, those things which are done by the help of evil spirits neither heal fully nor deliver perpetually. Nor does what he adds from *Chrysostom* apply to the matter: for that pertains to the beginning of a healing, not to a relapse of the disease, which we are discussing now. He also offers proof because a certain *Jodocus Hondius* published a picture of that cure. Why did he not add a poem? For *to painters and poets, there has always been an equal power of daring anything.* <note>A famous quote from Horace's Ars Poetica.</note> 

He has no more in the way of proof: later in chapter 9, he attempts to resolve the objections of his adversaries; which he proposes in such a way that it appears the Puritans and other mere *Calvinists* clearly disagree, denying and condemning this entire <term>scrofulous trickery</term> <note>original: "scrophulosanam stropham" — a play on words between scrofula and a "strophe" or trick.</note>. If even his Evangelical brothers do not believe it, he seeks in vain for me, a Catholic, to believe him; I who consider him and all those little brothers of his to be heretics. And from his own work, I can effectively persuade Catholics (for I care nothing for *Calvinists* or *Calvino-Papists*) that *Tooker’s* book should not be believed rashly. The arguments are these: Miracles are proper to the Catholic Church, and are given to no one who is outside the Catholic Church (he proves this in chapter 5). But *Elizabeth* is outside the Catholic Church. Therefore, miracles are not given to *Elizabeth*. I prove the minor premise: because the Roman Church alone is Catholic. *Elizabeth*, as a schismatic, is outside the Roman Church. Therefore, *Elizabeth* is outside the Catholic Church. The major premise is proven because the Catholic Church is the same as the Universal Church. The Roman Church alone...

is universal. Therefore, it alone is Catholic. All monuments of history teach that it alone is universal; certainly your English Church, of which Elizabeth is the head, does not go beyond the middle of the island <note>The author argues that the Anglican Church is merely a local, national institution, unlike the global "Catholic" Church.</note>. *Secondly*, miracles cannot be performed to confirm a false faith: (*you say this more than once, and you say it truly.*) But the faith which Elizabeth professes is a false faith. Therefore, miracles cannot be performed to confirm the faith which Elizabeth professes. You will deny the minor premise; I will prove it when you have set before me the articles of the Elizabethan faith. In the meantime, because I am addressing Catholics, for whom it is certain that Elizabeth is a Sacramentarian <note>A term used by Catholics and Lutherans for those who denied the Real Presence of Christ in the Eucharist, specifically followers of Zwingli or Calvin.</note>, and therefore a heretic, the argument is entirely demonstrative. Nor can you retort by saying she would not perform miracles unless her faith were true; but she does perform them, therefore her faith is true. For you take as a proven fact the very thing we are disputing: I, however, rely on premises which are matters of faith for Catholics, that is, most certain. *Thirdly*, you prove in the whole of Chapter 4—citing Saint Irenaeus, Saint Augustine, Saint Gregory of Nyssa, Saint Gregory the Great, and others—concerning the relics of Saint Stephen, Gregory the Miracle-Worker, Hilarion, and other Saints, that the gifts of healing have always shone forth in the Catholic Church down to almost our own times. From this I argue: Theirs was the true Church: we Papists are in the same Church in which they were. Therefore, we are in the true Church: you are in a different one than we are: therefore, you are not in the true Church. From which it follows that our first argument is true and unconquered. You yourself admit that the Church of those Fathers was the true Church; because you admit that through these miracles, the continuous efficacy of those miracles is proven. Do you deny that we are in the same Church as them? I prove it. They recognized the Roman Pontiff as the visible head of the Church, as do we: they believed whatever we believe; purgatory, seven Sacraments, the presence of the Lord's body in the Eucharist, praying for the dead, etc. These things you deny, we profess: nor do we believe anything that they did not believe. You, however, cannot praise even one of those performers of true miracles whom you named, with whom you agree in all things regarding the articles of the Catholic faith, or from whom we disagree in the slightest. Therefore, it is not you, but we, who are in their Church, which is the true one. Therefore, you must further admit either that their miracles were not true miracles, or that those of your Queen—who is of another Church and another faith than they were—are not true miracles.

Finally, if I ask you: why, in the healings performed by Vespasian, Pyrrhus, and other Gentiles or heretics, do you not recognize the hand of God, but of the Devil? You answer in Chapter 5: because works of this kind by infidels are done by the act and deed of impure spirits, and you praise Saint Augustine as the author of this most true opinion. <margin>Book 10 of *The City of God*.</margin> Answer then, since you believe this opinion to be true: is it lawful for you to believe in the miracles which the MOST CHRISTIAN Kings of France might perform in this category for the confirmation of the Roman Apostolic faith? If it is lawful to believe, then you now concede for the proof of a false faith (*for so you profess it to be*) that true miracles can be performed, which you denied, and which is most false. If, however, you say it is not lawful for you; then it will not be lawful for us Catholics to believe that the things Elizabeth does to confirm her faith are done by the help of God, or are true miracles: but we are compelled to say they are either fictions, if the people were not truly sick; or performed by some physical force of plasters or other things applied; or from a silent or express pact with a demon; from which neither the character of heretics nor certain other circumstances are far removed. Look to it, Tooker <note>Referring to William Tooker (1558–1621), chaplain to Elizabeth I, who wrote in defense of the "Royal Touch."</note>, which of all these you would prefer me to believe: for I have not yet decided nor resolved to believe you in the things you assert. Nor do I suggest you care what I believe about this matter: continue rather to stroke your Mistress <note>i.e., flatter the Queen</note>, and write, so that you may bring back a more well-anointed head <note>A metaphor for receiving rewards or ecclesiastical promotion.</note>. Concerning the kings of France—none of whom has yet openly professed heresy—the matter is found not only in the Annals, but also in the public books of physicians, *such as Guy [de Chauliac] in the Great Surgery, treatise 2, doctrine 2, chapter 5; and Jean Tagault, book 1 of the Institutions.*

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

### Page 24

<note>original: "tution. chirurgicar." refers to surgical instructions or institutions</note> and it is attested by certain daily experience; that this favor was formerly granted to them <note>The Kings of France and England</note>, that they might heal scrofula <term>strumas/scrofulas: a swelling of the lymph nodes, often called the "King's Evil"</term> by touch. If that power had been natural, it would not have passed through the hereditary line of the kingdom to the King alone, excluding the other brothers; whose temperament and physical makeup are often more similar to the father than the King himself. Also, the status of the scepter or crown, which this medical faculty accompanies, could naturally bestow no such thing. Regarding the finger of King Pyrrhus, which healed diseases by touch; Antonio Mirandola rightly judged that this should be numbered among incredible lies; or, as I think, among demonic works instead. Nevertheless.

<margin>F</margin>
I say 2. *It must not be denied that sometimes some have been found, and are found, who heal by touch alone.* This conclusion is proven by reading the lives of the saints everywhere, and by daily experience. And these healings are sometimes conferred only for a certain time, as we read that Blessed Petronilla was healed for a short time by Saint Peter, her spiritual father; and what Michael Medina narrates <margin>Book 2, chapter 7</margin> concerning a certain boy from Salamanca: sometimes the disease does not return for the whole of life. Indeed, it happens for a time when the healer has obtained it from God for a specific reason and a greater good than health—for example, lest the person abuse their health, or for the greater merit of patience; otherwise, miraculous cures are usually perfect. But a natural healing happens for a time when the subsequent change for the better proceeds solely from the imaginative power of the sick person, as the same Medina judged concerning that boy. There is in Spain a class of men whom they call *Saludadores* <term>Healers/Blessers</term>, or *Ensalmadores* <term>Chanters/Bone-setters</term>, with this distinction: the latter say they heal by the power of prayers, which they recite with specific words; the former, by the power of saliva and breath, which they breathe into the sick person. Concerning these I say 3. *The Saludadores and Ensalmadores can neither be strictly and universally condemned, nor universally approved.* For it can happen that sometimes some have the gift of healing: so say Victoria and Veracruz <margin>Victoria, Relection on Magic; Veracruz, Book 2, On the Soul, Speculation 2</margin>: and thus what Navarro <margin>Manual, chapter 11, number 36</margin> wrote in these words must be admitted: *Furthermore, those who are commonly called Saludadores (however much they may otherwise be the most depraved of men) can lawfully perform their duty: since that grace, freely given to such men by God alone, is granted for the benefit of others; and he cites the "Margarita Confessorum."* <note>A common 15th-century manual for confessors</note>] But this is stated too generally. For they rarely use it lawfully, as they do use it; nor is it to be believed that this gift is attributed by God to so many wicked men. Therefore, I advise the Vicars and Officials of Bishops, and other ordinaries, that before they permit them to perform this duty of healing, they should diligently examine whether they use natural remedies, or whether they work through grace freely given, or through a pact with the demon: which things are to be known from the circumstances. That they are not to be rejected universally, contrary to what Ciruelo suggests <note>Pedro Ciruelo, a Spanish inquisitor and mathematician</note> in part 3 of *On Superstition*, chapters 3 and 7, is proven. Because the hand of the Lord is never shortened, that He may not bestow this grace on whom He wills. However, I think it certain that this is never bestowed except upon Catholics: for so far, heretics have performed no miracles.

I say 4. *I do not believe that anyone can naturally remove a serious and true disease from another, or heal wounds, or extract iron, by touch alone, or by whistling, or by breath, or by a kiss.* This entire conclusion is against Pomponazzi; and, regarding touch, it opposes Veracruz. I have already set down the foundation of the former; but this is the foundation of the latter: by natural complexion or constellation <note>astrological alignment</note> some are harmful, which is evident in "fascination" <note>the Evil Eye</note> by sight alone. Therefore, from the same causes, some by natural complexion or constellation will be healthy and medicinal. But in so few words he supposes many things that must not be granted. First, what he says regarding constellations, following Albert <note>Albertus Magnus</note>, is not true. If he speaks of the constellation when that person was born: everyone born then would have that power. Who would believe that? If he speaks of the constellation at the time of the touch; everyone who touches then would be able to heal: if of both together; that "Great Year" <note>A massive astrological cycle where all planets return to their original positions</note> must have intervened, which [restores] the same position of the stars—

<meta>This page continues the author's systematic refutation of "salutatores" (healers who claim natural or divine powers through touch or breath), specifically addressing the arguments of the philosopher Pomponazzi.</meta>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER III. QUESTION IV.

<page-num>25</page-num>

...for the influence to be restored when he touches; those influences which existed when he was born—but who lives or has lived for such a long time? Otherwise, he would not be able to perform this on any other day except when that specific alignment of positions, which is required, happens to occur. How uncertain, therefore, would this cure be! Secondly, because he admits that common opinion concerning **fascination** <note>original: "fascinatio" — the belief in the "evil eye" or the power to harm or heal through a look or touch</note>, which I will later show to be entirely foreign to true Philosophy. Thirdly, he errs in his inference. For it does not follow that because a quality of one human is harmful to another, it must therefore be admitted that a quality of one human toward another is healing. For, according to **Aristotle** <margin>Aristotle, *Problems*, section 7.</margin> more things are necessary for health than for sickness. For evil arises from any single defect, but good arises from a complete and integrated cause. Furthermore, contagion is drawn in by breath alone. But in this "cure," they diligently observe certain ways of touching, numbers, and other ceremonies. Many things are added that are full of suspicion and danger.

First, because those **healers** <note>original: "salutatores" — a class of folk healers in Spain and Italy who claimed the power to cure rabies and other ailments</note> say that to exercise this power, they have need of drinking much wine. However, it cannot be that the drinking of wine is beneficial for the cure of contrary diseases. If it benefits those that are "cold," it will not benefit those that are "hot." Likewise, they are accustomed to offer bread bitten by themselves to those bitten by a rabid dog or infected with poison; neither of the causes cited by Veracruz <note>likely referring to Alonso de la Vera Cruz, a prominent colonial philosopher</note> supports this bread. Third, they say that in the presence of another healer who has a greater power of healing, they themselves are unable to heal. This is absurd. For that power, if it were natural, ought rather to be intensified. Fourth, they lie when they say that one healer is naturally recognized by other healers, even if they have never seen him before: which is a sign of some mark impressed upon them by a **demon**. Indeed, in most of them there is the mark of a wheel, as they themselves frequently say, of **St. Catherine** or **St. Quiteria**. Fifth, they are deceived when they think this power belongs to every seventh son, provided a female birth has not interrupted the male sequence. This is ridiculous. Sixth, they often boast that things are revealed to them which were done far away in their absence, like that man who cured the father of **Torquemada** from the bite of a rabid dog. Torquemada himself tells the story. <margin>p. *Garden of Flowers*, colloquy 3.</margin> Seventh, they boast that they can handle coals without harm and enter a burning oven; which things cannot happen naturally unless they protect themselves by washing and anointing with very cold substances, which nevertheless can only resist the activity of fire for a short time. Whence **Vairus** <margin>q. Book 2 of *On Fascination*, ch. 11.</margin> relates that a certain man from this flock, when he had entered an oven and another person, unaware of him, had closed the door, was consumed there by the flames.

<margin>H</margin> I come now to the foundation of **Pomponazzi** <note>Pietro Pomponazzi (1462–1525), a philosopher who sought naturalistic explanations for miracles</note>, and I respond: stones, herbs, and animals, which have received some beneficial power of this kind from nature, possess it from a certain and defined **temperament** <note>the specific balance of qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) in a substance</note> of the whole species. For it follows from this that every **magnet** attracts iron, every **rhubarb** purges bile, every **rupica** <note>likely a reference to a specific medicinal herb or "rock-breaker" plant</note> heals the jaundice. It would be necessary, therefore, if that power were natural to the healers, that they would have received it by reason of the human species, and thus all humans would possess that power. If you attribute this to one person and take it from another by reason of an individual and singular complexion: why is this diversity not also found in the magnet and the *rupica*, by reason of the individual, so that one magnet cannot do what another can? Therefore, the similarity holds ground on neither side. Likewise, that rhubarb tempers bile, it does so by reason of its peculiar temperament. For all individuals of that species, and none of another, heal diseases in exactly the same way. But in humans, there cannot be this similarity and equality of temperament: because the **form** of a human is very different from the form of rhubarb; wherefore also the temperament of the body differs from the temperament of rhubarb. For the form of a human requires far different dispositions of matter than the form of a plant or a juice. In what way, then, could the same natural power belong to a human by reason of temperament as belongs to the *rupica* or rhubarb?

As for those who boast with puffed-out cheeks of the lineage and kinship of **Blessed Paul**, and that they can handle snakes without injury; the imposture is already known to most, that they are accustomed to first protect themselves against the bite with **antidotes**.

<sig>C</sig>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

Regarding that military method of curing wounds <note>likely referring to the "weapon salve" or sympathetic powder, where the weapon was treated instead of the wound</note>; I say boldly that it relies upon a demonic pact, and by its very nature, it is a lethal crime. It is also blasphemous to call it the "Art of Saint Anselm," as it was actually the invention of that Magician, Anselm of Parma. Furthermore, those healed of wounds or diseases in this manner later fall back into the most grievous pains and often more cruel illnesses; <margin>r Ananias, book 4, *On the Nature of Demons*</margin> and for the most part, they obtain the worst possible end to their lives. I could prove this with examples of many people known to me, but I spare the names of the dead.

<margin>s From book 3, *On the Motion of Animals*</margin>
There remains a certain point from Albertus Magnus <note>Albert the Great, a 13th-century polymath often cited in magical texts</note>, who reports that he saw two boys: when one was carried near closed doors, everything situated on his right side would open; while for the other, the same was achieved for doors on his left. Why then should iron not fall out of a wound at the mere touch or presence of someone? Does not Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous botanist</note> write that he knew of a herb in the mountains of Italy which, when stepped on by horses, immediately loosens their iron shoes? Or that a driven wedge is extracted by the "Woodpecker's herb," according to the testimony of Democritus and Theophrastus? Or that by the herb *Aethiopis*, enchanted with a certain rite, all locks are opened if it is applied? He even mentions a thief condemned to hanging in Venice in his own time, for whom all doors were opened in this way. ] *So he says in his Dedicatory Epistle to Dioscorides.* I respond: In the last two cases, open incantation was added; in the first two herbs, there was a hidden power of attracting and loosening implanted by nature—a power which cannot naturally exist in humans. It exists neither by a specific reason (because not all people have it) nor by an individual reason (because individual conditions do not produce such an effect). Therefore, neither Aristotle nor anyone philosophizing correctly is going to believe this. *And it is greatly to be wondered that Albert alone narrates more things of this difficult-to-believe kind that he himself saw than (as Antonio Mirandola says) all other extant writers of so many centuries combined.* ] If such a thing does happen, it must be ascribed to the illusions of a demon. We may sufficiently gather from Plutarch and Pliny that the Psylli and Marsi <note>Ancient tribes famous for snake-charming and resistance to venom</note> relied upon his pact and incantations, and even more clearly from Alcimus Avitus in book 2, in these verses:

> *Who could worthily describe the fraudulent Magic,*
> *Testing hidden powers within a silent breast?*
> *As once the Law-giving prophet <note>Moses</note>, before the proud king <note>Pharaoh</note>,*
> *While showing new miracles of the commanded sign,*
> *Stirred the magicians with envy to attempt such things,*
> *And in their burning zeal, they piled up their own ruins:*
> *Powers which, if they were not vain, they would perhaps*
> *Hasten to take away the monsters, not to add more,*
> *Equal only to the signs in appearance, but not in strength.*
> <margin>Circe, who is also Anguitia <note>A goddess of snakes</note></margin>
> \* *What it cannot remove, it sustains with redoubled anger.*
> *Hence it is that the Marsi can, by a praised song,*
> *Draw forth savage dragons with their silent art*
> *From afar, and often command them to fight with them.*
> *Then, as each one perceives the water-snake heavy for war,*
> *Or recognizes the shut-up anger of the hard asp,*
> *He strikes within the weapons of a secret song,*
> *And immediately, the poisons being loosened by a charming word,*
> *Soon the snake is handled with an unarmed hand without harm,*
> *And only the bite, not the venom, is feared in the serpent:*
> *Sometimes the enchanter perishes, if the cunning snake,*
> *Being deaf, has despised the murmurs of the conjurer.*
> *Because they draw this from their mother and first origin,*
> *That the serpentine fraud and the tongue thus perish,*
> *They return mutual dealings of speaking into a mere appearance.*
