# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV.

# CHAPTER IV.

->Concerning Artificial Magic.<-

<margin>a 1. Tusculan Disputations & 2. On the Nature of the Gods. b Plutarch in the Life of Marcellus & Zonaras vol. 2. c Gellius book 10 of Attic Nights. d Glycas & Manasses in the Annals. e Cassiodorus 1. Various Letters, penultimate; & Poliziano in Panepistemon. Psalm 135 v. 15. f St. Thomas book 3 Against the Gentiles c. 104. Valencia 2. 2. disp. 1. q. 2</margin>
A This branch of magic accomplishes certain wonders through human industry. For now I speak only of the operative kind; I reserve the place for the divinatory kind later. Operative Artificial Magic is itself twofold: Mathematical and Prestigiatory <note>The magic of illusions or sleight-of-hand</note>. I call that "Mathematical" which relies on geometric, arithmetic, or astronomical principles. Examples of this include that Sphere of Archimedes—which according to Claudian was made of glass, and of which Marcus Tullius Cicero speaks <margin>a</margin>—and the burning of ships by means of mirrors during the siege of Syracuse <margin>b</margin>; the flying wooden dove of Archytas <margin>c</margin>; the little golden birds of the Emperor Leo that used to chirp <margin>d</margin>; and the brass birds of Boethius, which both flew and chirped, along with serpents of the same metal that hissed, and similar things recounted by many <margin>e</margin>. This Magic is called <term>thaumaturgic</term> <note>original: θαυματουργικὴ — wonder-working</note> by Heron and Pappus. However, it must be assumed that this can effect nothing that is contrary to the nature of things; rather, it requires the help of natural causes, applied with certain motion and dimensions, as is evident in those mirrors of Archimedes, and in our own hydraulic machines and <term>automata</term> <note>self-moving mechanical devices</note>. Therefore, if any effect is proposed that surpasses the efficacy of natural causes, it must be referred to the miraculous, even if human industry concurred in the making or moving of the instrument. For this reason, I disagree with William of Auvergne, Medina, and others, who believe that the bronze head of Albertus Magnus (of which they tell) spoke articulately by human artifice. For human industry does not reach this far; nor does natural reason allow an inanimate thing to emit a human voice by responding at the will of questioners. For these things require life, and respiration in the speaker, and the most perfect cooperation of the vital organs, and a certain reasoning process in the speaker. Since all these were lacking in that head, if it spoke, it was that being speaking in the head who used to pour out oracles in the statues of idols; he who was none other than the <term>cacodemon</term> <note>an evil spirit or demon</note>. For it is known that idols were devoid of their own voice. <margin>Psalm 135 v. 15.</margin> *The idols of the Gentiles are silver and gold: they have a mouth and will not speak; neither is there spirit in their mouths.* That was the opinion of William, and formerly of Hermes, but it was refuted by Theologians long ago <margin>f</margin>. To this category belongs that ingenious globe, or bronze statue, so constructed that when a cord, which had been stretched from a hidden place, was released, it would shoot arrows at the onlooker; by which means it is read in the *Scottish Annals* of Major and Boece that Kenneth M., King of the Scots, was killed by Fenella. To the same category should be reduced the wonderful skill of certain people who carry immense weights on their foreheads, and rope-walkers standing on spheres (as I have seen), and those who bend their limbs as if they were made of clay or broken, as they please; of whom St. Chrysostom treated in *Homily 19 near the end, to the People of Antioch.* In these matters, bodily disposition and natural agility usually help a great deal, as Andreas Libavius teaches with many examples in *part 2 of the Treatise on Wonders, concerning Sleepwalkers, at the end of the work.*

<margin>g Description of Africa book 8 & book 9. h Daniel 14. i St. Jerome letter 103 to Paulinus. k Herodotus book 3. l Josephus book 18 Antiquities c. 4. Rufinus book 2 Ecclesiastical History c. 25.</margin>
B The other species is playful and deceptive, and can be called *Prestigiatory*: its effects are not of the kind, or what they seem to be. To this belong most of the things believed to be performed by charms by vagabonds, mountebanks, and rope-walkers, when they are actually done by agility of feet or hands; such as the things Pomponazzi recounts of a certain man from Rieti. Sometimes they are also done through brute animals taught by long practice, such as what Joannes Leo relates regarding an ass and a camel <margin>g</margin>. Sometimes they occur through mere imposture and hidden passages entered by some people, as the priests of Bel used to do, who feigned that the food was devoured by a Dragon <margin>h</margin>; or as happened at that famous "Table of the Sun" <margin>i</margin>, which the common crowd of travelers thought supplied food of its own accord, though in truth the food was supplied by the inhabitants of the neighboring city <margin>k</margin>. And it also happened in those fictitious loves and rapes of the idols, of which Josephus and Rufinus write <margin>l</margin>.

C *Thaumaturgy*, like *Natural Magic*, is good and lawful in itself, just as all arts are good in themselves. Both become unlawful by accident: first, when they are directed toward an evil end...

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

...are categorized. Secondly, when a scandal arises because these feats are thought to be performed by the work of demons. For this reason, such things should not be permitted unless the traveling performers <note>original: "circulatores" — itinerant entertainers or "mountebanks" who gathered crowds in circles</note> hold a public and suitable testimonial of their art from Catholics. Thirdly, if any spiritual or temporal harm to body or soul threatens the performer himself or the spectators. For on this account, those are condemned who, without necessity or just cause, expose themselves to the danger of death for the sake of a game <margin>Silvester and Fumus under the word "art." Navarrus chapter 15, number 8.</margin>.

Furthermore, *Natural and Artificial Magic* are two veils with which *Diabolical Magic* is accustomed to hide itself. For it always either fakes the power of nature—as in those things concerning the influence of the stars and the temperament of man which we discussed in the previous chapter—or it fakes craftsmanship, as in characters, images, and such things, which must be debated in the following question.

Now, regarding deceptive illusions <note>original: "præstigiatrice" — referring to the art of prestidigitation or conjuring</note>, in addition to those things which will be discussed later in *Book 2, Question 8*, it was wisely observed by Ulrich Molitor in his *Book on Pythian Women* <note>A famous 15th-century treatise on witches</note> that the medium can be so arranged by the Devil that one thing appears to be another. Many such experiments are performed by jesters; but since Satan is the *master* (he says) *of jesters*, there is no doubt for anyone that the Devil can work these things more subtly. In this, the student is never more learned than the teacher. Such was that case which Nider mentions in the *Formicarius* <note>"The Anthill," a 15th-century work on the dangers of witchcraft</note>, in the *chapter on sorcerers and their deceptions*: *In Cologne,* he says, *a certain maiden was summoned who had done marvelous things in the sight of nobles, which seemed to be done by magic art. For she was said to have torn a certain cloth to pieces and suddenly restored it before the eyes of all; a certain glass thrown by her against a wall and shattered, she repaired in a moment, and similar things.* She escaped the hands of the inquisitor, though excommunicated. Bodin notes that they are accustomed to mix ten acts of magic illusion with one act of subtle or agile craftsmanship, so that they may persuade people that whatever is done by them is done without illusion, by dexterity and art alone. They take care that whatever sorcery they mix in appears to be subtlety and agility. Satan loves to provoke men to laughter, so that while joyful and cheerful they may drink in impiety. Thus these deceptive vagabonds, jesters, wonder-workers, and traveling performers often fascinate the judges themselves, drawing them into a certain foolish joy and admiration, so that everything seems to them merely jesting and not to be punished. There has always been a frequent crop of such people everywhere, and the harvest even today in the courts of certain Princes is more harmful than garlic and hemlock. In France, Triscalinus <margin>Bodin in "On the Demon-mania of Witches"</margin> in the presence of Charles IX (otherwise a praised King), pulled rings one by one from the necklace of a certain nobleman standing far from him, enticing them toward himself while everyone watched, and received them in his hand as they flew, as it seemed; nevertheless, the necklace was soon found whole and unharmed. This man, convicted of many things which could be done neither by art, nor human action, nor nature, confessed that he had performed everything by the help of the devil—which he had previously stubbornly denied. Johannes Trithemius reports from older sources that in the year 876, in the time of Emperor Louis, a certain Sedechias, a Jew by religion and a physician by profession, performed stupendous things before the leading men. He seemed to men to swallow a fully armed knight on horseback; also a wagon loaded with hay with the horses and driver; to cut off heads, hands, and feet, which, placed in a basin before them, he offered to the sight of all dripping with blood, and immediately restored them to the unharmed men in their proper places. Hunts, charges, and jousting matches, and things which men do on the earth, he made to be heard in the air. In the middle of winter, he produced a most pleasant garden in the Emperor's palace, with trees, herbs, flowers, and the singing of little birds suddenly born and heard. If anyone pulled the foot of another, the foot followed, and the man remained unharmed. In Sicily, they put forward Eunus, but that feat of his by which he seemed to vomit flame from his mouth while speaking should be attributed to craftsmanship and not clearly to illusions. For he had a nut filled with sulfur and fire placed in his mouth, which, when moved gently by his breath, poured out flame, as Thomas Fazellus writes in *Decade 2 of Sicilian Affairs, Book 5, Chapter 2*. The same author in Chapter 1, Book 3, Decade 1, reports wonders of a certain Diodorus, whom the common people call Liodorus: this man, imbued with magic art, by a marvelous machination of illusions...

# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION I.

...flourished in the nation of Catania <note>A city in Sicily</note>. *[For he seemed able, by the powerful force of his songs, to turn men into brute animals, and to transform the shapes of almost all things into new metamorphoses; he appeared able to suddenly draw toward himself those separated from him by the longest distances. Furthermore, he provoked the people of Catania with such frequent injuries and dishonored them with insults that, ensnared by the traps of a most vain credulity, they were incited to a zeal for offering him worship. When he was found guilty of a capital crime and was to be handed over to the cross, by the most ready art of his summoned songs, he slipped from the hands of the executioners and ordered himself to be carried through the air from Catania to Byzantium <note>Constantinople</note>—whose empire Sicily acknowledged at that time—and back again from Byzantium to Catania in a short interval of time. By these sorceries he became so admired by the people that, thinking there was a certain divine power in him, they exhibited the worship due to sacred things to him in a sacrilegious error. But at last, unexpectedly captured by the divine virtue of Leo, Bishop of Catania, he was cast into a fiery furnace in the middle of the city before a crowd of people and was consumed by the burning of the fire.]* Thus divine justice prevailed, and he who had perhaps rescued himself from judges moved by less than just zeal could not slip away from the hands of a holy man. I will relate similar deceptions in the beginning of Book 6; and Crespetus <note>Peter Crespet, a 16th-century prior</note> attributes certain things to a certain Cratisthenes, citing Athenaeus and others from Book 3, chapters 43 and 44 of the *Dial of Princes* by Antonio de Guevara, Bishop of Mondoñedo. He rightly adds what I have already warned: that these jugglers, however skillful, employ the labor of demons; therefore, the Kings of France—Saint Louis, Philip Augustus, and Henry III—sanctioned by laws that such people be exiled from the kingdom. (Crespet, *Discourse on the Hatred of Satan*, Book 1, Discourse 12). In our own times, it was reported that Cesare Maltesio was captured in Paris but escaped from prison by cunning; the inquisitor Baxius objected to this among other things during his trial. But when he was pressed and feared damnation, he was rescued by the governor then in power, who ordered the ecclesiastical judges to desist; he then crept into the court and there began to perform many new things. Standing at a distance, he would change playing cards in the hand of another person holding them, so that a different figure appeared on them two or three times. He would draw vessels placed on the other side of the table toward himself merely by moving a small piece of glass. Sometimes he divined the thoughts of others, as when a great multitude of tiny grains of sugar were scattered on a table, he would indicate which grain someone had chosen in their mind; even if someone hesitated in choosing the proposed object, he would describe the hesitation and the resolution. He would boast that he had known long before what the other was going to choose, and many other things. Because of these things, he was summoned to court for a third time by the Most Illustrious Archbishop of Mechelen, Lord Hovius, in the year 1600; although he promised to present himself, he fled to the asylum of all the precursors of the Antichrist. That Prince <note>The governor mentioned previously</note>, who had rescued the deceiver from judgment by authority rather than by right, survived scarcely two years and died in the flower of his age; nor did anything in his government turn out successfully for him after he had undertaken the defense of an evil cause. God never leaves unpunished those Princes who are defenders of His enemies; because He specifically forbade that anyone should allow a sorceress to live, *Exodus 22, verse 18*. This is, as Philo interprets it, that He did not want the punishment to be delayed, but commanded her to be killed on the very day she was convicted.

### QUESTION I.

->Is the power of characters, rings, seals, or images such<-
->as the Magi contend?<-

Ptolemy, Aphrodisaeus, Porphyry, and other Platonists agree with the Magi, as do the most foolish Artephius and Thebit; among more recent authors, Pomponazzi, Ficino, Dolce, and Antonio Mizaldo in his *Century of Memorable Things*, but they do so superstitiously. 
Truly and prudently, Hippocrates, Galen, and all those we shall cite below in Question 3 have denied it. Indeed, Tatian the Assyrian hit the nail on the head; judging that if anything is effected by these things, it happens by an agreement, namely because demons have established these signs for themselves as if they were military tokens <term>tesseras militares: passwords or identification tokens used by soldiers</term> or agreed-upon pacts; so that men...

# DISQUISITIONS ON MAGIC

<margin>
a. Guide for the Doubting, chapter 72. <note>original: "Ductor. dubitant." referring to Maimonides' Guide for the Perplexed.</note>
b. St. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica 2.2. question 96. articles 1 & 4; Against the Gentiles, chapters 104 & 105, and there Ferrariensis; Silvester Prierias on the word "superstition," question 10; Ciruelo on Superstition, page 3; Martin de Arles, Book on Superstitions; Condronchius, Book 3 on Veneficial Diseases, chapter 1; Bokelius, Book on Love Potions, chapter 1; Vairus, Anania, and others.
</margin>
They are accustomed to use certain formulas of words. Moses Maimonides pronounces <margin>a</margin> that those who ascribe such great power of miracles to the mere shape, the mere writing, or finally to the mere lines or to voices born of broken air, are liars and fools; our Physicians, Theologians, and Jurists agree <margin>b</margin>.

<margin>
B
c. Book on Superstition, second to last and last chapters.
d. Book 16, On Variety.
</margin>
It should be noted from Conrad Wimpina <margin>c</margin> that Magicians have three kinds of images or figures. In the first kind, they teach that fumigations and incantations must be applied, along with exorcisms through the names of fifty-four angels, and other things which are treacherous and contain manifest <term>idolatry</term> <note>original Greek: ἰδωλολατϱεῖν - to worship idols</note>. Firstly, because they could not have received these names except from demons (which Cardano admits <margin>d</margin>). Secondly, because to say that such fumigations honor God, or please God, is an error in faith; moreover, to say that using such things is not honoring demons with sacrifice, and therefore a damnable act of <term>idolatry</term>, is also an error. The Theological Faculty of Paris defined both in Articles 10 and 11.

The second kind of images relies on characters and carvings that are barbarous and foreign; among which are nine cauldrons, and the names of the four princes of the world's corners <note>The four cardinal directions</note>, and thrice-seven names plucked from the Mohammedan ravings of the Arabs. They attribute these figures to authors such as Germon the Babylonian, Hermes the Egyptian, and Thorus the Greek; they also count the four rings of Solomon among such things. It is clear that all such things are to be numbered with the first kind of superstitious and accursed practices; they can have no power except from the demon, from whom as teacher and dedicator these wretched men received them. St. Jerome <margin>e</margin>, dealing with amulets and metal plates, taught that these things are in themselves vain and empty. Even Albertus Magnus and Roger Bacon admit this. It is also an error in faith to believe that one demon is King of the East and another of the West, especially by his own merit <margin>f</margin>.

<margin>
e. In the Life of St. Hilarion, near the middle.
f. Parisian Articles, 25.
g. Trallianus, Albertus, Bacon, Mizaldus.
h. Above, chapter 3, question 1.
</margin>
A certain axiom of Ptolemy gave birth to the third species: *the lower faces [of the earth] are subject to the celestial faces*; and from this they inferred that all terrestrial scorpions are ruled by the celestial Scorpio, and all river fish by the celestial Pisces. Many defend this nonsense along with Ptolemy <margin>g</margin>. But this has already been partially refuted above <margin>h</margin>. Now I add: if that correspondence of an imaginary celestial figure to this true lower figure were so powerful, why do they add certain superstitious rites? For example, that if you seek a certain effect, you must write it on the back of the figure; if the opposite, on the front; and that it must be done sometimes with the right hand, sometimes with the left; the seal must now be buried head-down, now head-up, and other things of this sort. Whence I do not fear to conclude, with Gerson and Wimpina, that even this last species is superstitious and condemned. For all these things are founded on a Magic aphorism from an impious book entitled <term>Arbatel</term> <note>original Hebrew: ארבעתאל</note> in the second Septenary; where it is taught that when God imposed names on things or persons, He distributed certain powers and offices to them along with the names. And so, characters and "constellated" names (that is, *Images formed and named under a certain constellation*) have power not by reason of their shape or pronunciation, but by reason of that virtue or office which God ordained to such a name or character. This is entirely a lie fabricated to establish this superstition. God did indeed impose names on certain saints in the Scriptures for the sake of signifying a mystery, and consistent with the office they were to perform; but God instilled no effective power into the name itself. As for all living creatures, Adam gave them names, not God.

<margin>
C
</margin>
The reasoning is the same regarding Magic rings and seals, however much they display the most beautiful names of Raphael, the Maccabees, Solomon, Elisha, Zechariah, Constantine, and certain saints; in which names blasphemy also intervenes, while they wickedly feign that these saints were initiated into Magic. Likewise, because they number the sign of the cross—the sign in which that Great Emperor [Constantine] conquered—among their own demonic seals.

# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION I.

<margin>i Plato, Book 2 of the Republic; Trallianus, Book 10, ch. 1; Philostratus, Book 3 of the Life of Apollonius; Josephus, Book 8 of Antiquities, ch. 2.</margin>
Finally, it must be held as a certainty that whenever some effect follows the use of characters <note>written symbols or magical signs</note>, rings, seals, or images—which some claim occasionally happensⁱ—the entire thing is the work of a demon. By exploiting the belief in these trifles which he himself established, the demon strives either to sow new errors in minds or to fix those already sown more deeply and firmly.

<margin>h This author [Lodovico Dolce] writes from Book 3 on gems, from ch. 6 to the final chapter.</margin>
Furthermore, so that the worthlessness of Hermes <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy and magic</note> and his like may be exposed even by the unlearned, I shall pluck a few examples from the Italian book of Lodovico Dolce. With a harmful example, he published the seals of a fictitious Solomon and Hermes. It would be more fitting in the Catholic Church for such things to be buried in the ashes of silence than to be so splendidly circulated in print. These triflers say, therefore: that he who possesses a gem engraved with Aries, Leo, or Sagittarius will be pleasing and gracious to all; he who has Virgo, Taurus, or Capricorn will be made eager to embrace religion; those with Gemini, Libra, or Aquarius will be faithful friends, observers of the law, and seekers of harmony. Conversely, for those with Cancer, Scorpio, or Pisces, there will be an infusion of love for iniquity, levity, and lying. He with the character of Saturn grows in strength; he with Jupiter is lovable, fortunate, and destined for the highest honors; he with Mars is unconquerable; he with the Sun, wealthy; he with Venus, "un-sinkable" <note>original: *immersabilis*, meaning one who cannot be overwhelmed by misfortune</note> and granted all his prayers; the same for the Moon. Why say more? There is no image of a star to which a peculiar efficacy does not cling. I pass over the stars: the figure of a dragon makes one happy and rich; an astrolabe makes one a prophet; the same (laughable!) for an ass, if engraved on chrysolite. A frog impels enemies toward peace and friends toward hatred; a camel compels demons, etc. If he was joking, he certainly mocks that Prelate—one of the pillars of the Church <note>a Cardinal</note>—to whom he dedicated the work; if he was serious, I have no way to excuse him. For he attributes to the figures of asses and stars those things which belong to God alone: the infusion of prophecy, the love of religion and faith, steadfastness, and the like. Is this not persuading human curiosity to abandon the good arts and ethical studies and plunge itself into the abyss of superstitions? Does it not make a man’s God nothing more than an engraved ring worn on his right hand?

But on the other side, Pomponazzi and Cajetan <note>Pietro Pomponazzi and Cardinal Cajetan, two influential philosophers</note> cause trouble. The former, when we object that pentagons, circles, and other such signs or characters are merely certain qualities of quantity—and that quantity itself is not an active principle—*responds* that while the figure is not the principle of the operation, it nevertheless contributes greatly to it. For in this way, a deformed shape of matter brings sadness to the human soul, while a beautiful one brings joy. This is said by him without reason, because magical characters possess nothing of beauty or deformity worth noting, nor are they fit to produce these passions. When Pomponazzi saw this himself, he finally fled to the "belief" <note>credulity</note> of those using them. Truly, the power of belief is no greater than that of the imagination, concerning which see what was said in the preceding chapter.

<margin>k 2. 2. question 96, article 2.</margin>
Cajetan, as he was of a sharp and piercing intellect, even goes so far as to challenge the common opinion on several points.ᵏ

First, he reasons thus: a figure, although it is not the principle of operation itself, is nevertheless a "co-principle." Therefore, as a figure, it has a peculiar effect. He proves the premise first because in the tools of craftsmen, the shape effects how they work in this or that way; and second, because a broad piece of iron is carried upon the waters, but if you contract it into another form, it will sink. *I respond* that a figure is a co-principle in local motion and in operations performed through this motion—such as the various divisions of a continuous body by a mattock, a hammer, an ax, or a saw—but not in operations performed through "alteration" <note>internal qualitative change</note>. Nor do his proofs demonstrate this; indeed, his last point is perhaps false. For iron can hardly be thinned to such a breadth that it becomes porous and "un-sinkable" in water. But even if it were so, it would be by reason of its quantity (which many say does not differ in reality from the substance itself), not by reason of its figure. For I ask: in what figure would he have the iron be—circular, square, or pentagonal? If he says this one or that one, I shall infer that the result would be the same if the plate were formed in any other shape, provided it were equally thin

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

and a broad shape: which is proof that the shape itself performs no work. If one were to say that in any shape, that specific stretching and drawing out of the iron <note>referring to the preparation of a talisman</note> is sufficient, they must certainly admit that this does not depend on the shape. For the extension of quantity and the shape impressed upon that extended quantity differ no less than the whiteness of a wall differs from the surface of the whitewashed wall itself.

Secondly, he brings forward that foundation of the "third kind of images" <note>a category of talismans believed to draw power from celestial bodies</note>, which has already been exploded <note>rejected/disproven</note>.

Thirdly, he claims the authority of St. Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas</note>, as if the Holy Doctor in *Chapter 104* had conceded that these images possess some power from heaven insofar as they are images and constitute a thing in an "artificial species." *I respond* that since St. Thomas attacks these superstitions in that very chapter, he said this only for the sake of argument on behalf of his opponents; as if to say: "Even if I were to grant you that regarding images, it would still be false regarding characters <note>magical sigils or letters</note>."

Fourthly, he adds that it seems harsh and difficult to condemn all those who use such images. *I respond* that the number of fools is infinite; therefore, it is enough that the matter is in truth evil and superstitious. Let what has been said suffice for now; those who have written professionally on this matter supply more details <margin>1. Langius, Epistle 34; Medina, Chapter 7, folio 72; Ananias, Book 4; Cardanus, Book 16 on Variety and Book 18 on Subtlety; Ciruelus (above) and Baptista Segnus, Book on True Christian Study, Chapter 7.</margin>.

## QUESTION II.

->*Is there any magical power inherent in arithmetic or musical numbers?*<-

The Orthodox parties <note>mainstream Catholic theologians</note> everywhere philosophize much regarding the connection of numbers; yet they only contemplated certain divine mysteries in this numerical proportion and connection; they did not attribute any natural or magical power to the numbers themselves as such. The Pythagoreans and Platonists, however, were foully superstitious in this matter. Let this be the

> *Conclusion: Number, insofar as it is number, contains no power of operation, whether natural or supernatural.*

Leonardo Vairus <note>author of a famous treatise on "fascination" or the evil eye</note> proves this excellently in *Book 2, Chapter 11*, and all other Catholics, to a man, concede it; especially those who contend that number is nothing real distinct from the things being numbered. For how can that which is itself merely imaginary perform any work? But even those who wish for number to be something real added to the things numbered must concede the conclusion. For they admit that number is a quantity: but quantity is not active by itself, except perhaps in local motion, which is not the subject here. Finally, whatever we have said regarding shape holds the same place in numbers. For number is discrete quantity; it cannot have greater efficacy than continuous quantity.

<margin>B</margin>
Why then (someone might say) is the "climacteric year" <note>critical years in a person's life, often multiples of 7 or 9</note> usually fatal to a man, and an eight-month birth rarely viable, if number does not have some weight? Regarding the eight-month birth, this happens because human seed is manifold. One type perfects the fetus in the seventh month, and then the fetus strives to exit after the sixth month: if it finds no great resistance, it comes out strong and robust; but if it finds resistance, it is weakened by that continuous struggle of a month, and in the eighth month, it comes forth into the light quite weak and invalid. Another seed perfects the fetus only in the ninth month, and then the fetus does not strive to exit before this month, and is usually so robust that all contrary impediments either do not resist it, or through the resistance—when the mother carries the womb longer—it is not greatly weakened. See Hippocrates <note>the ancient Greek "Father of Medicine"</note> in his *Book on the Eight-Month Birth*, whom you may follow more safely in this matter than the Academics <note>Platonists</note> who flee superstitiously to the power of numbers; you will find their sayings in the learned work of Fabius Paullinus, *Book 6, On the Week, Chapter 4*. The reason for climacteric years, as with "critical days" in illness, depends chiefly on the ratio and certain natural periods of the humors and temperament that exist in men.

<summary>The author argues that numbers and geometric shapes possess no inherent magical or supernatural power, attributing phenomena like "climacteric years" to biological humors rather than numerology.</summary>
<keywords>numerology, climacteric year, Thomas Aquinas, Hippocrates, natural magic, discrete quantity, superstition, medical astrology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of superstitious beliefs regarding the inherent power of numbers, specifically addressing their misuse in medicine, prayer, and magical traditions.</meta>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION II.

From what has been said, it follows that if the Cinquefoil herb <note>original: *Pentaphylli herba*; a plant with five leaves, often used in folk medicine</note> has any effects, they are to be attributed to the substance of the leaves, not to the number five itself. Furthermore, it is superstitious to believe that one of its leaves cures a one-day fever, three leaves a tertian fever, and four leaves a quartan fever; likewise, that three grains of heliotrope benefit a tertian fever and four a quartan; and other such things which are customarily kept in medicines through amulets <note>original: *ligamenta*; items tied to the body as charms</note> by a bad example. It would be a similar error to judge that pills or medicinal boluses <note>original: *catapotia*</note> are more beneficial in an odd number than an even one, even if they are administered in an equal total quantity. Musical numbers do indeed possess great power, but not *as* numbers; rather, they affect the soul sweetly through the variety of tones and the modulation of sound: more on this matter shortly.

It also follows from what has been said that those who wish for some power to reside in certain Psalms are vain and superstitious: because they are placed at a certain number, they believe them to be effective so that a reader's prosperous undertakings might succeed, or that they might be delivered from grave dangers. For example, Psalm 1, *Blessed is the man* <note>original: *Beatus vir*</note>: because unity is something perfect—as if, forsooth, there were not also perfection in the numbers seven, six, five, and three. They also say that Psalm 10, *In the Lord I put my trust* <note>original: *In Domino confido*</note>, is lucky because the number ten is good; and because the number one hundred is composed of ten times ten, they believe the hundredth Psalm, *I will sing of mercy and judgment* <note>original: *Misericordiam et iudicium*</note>, to be even luckier. They are refuted because the number ten or one hundred, insofar as they are numbers, have no real goodness. For they have neither physical nor ethical goodness, but only an artificial one proceeding from the imagination of the one counting; this obtains no efficacy over the bodies or things being counted. Furthermore, in the Hebrew source, the order of the Psalms is different than in the Latin version; therefore, a Psalm that is lucky according to the Latin version would be unlucky according to the Hebrew source, which is an utterly absurd thing to say—or, one would be lucky for someone speaking Hebrew and another for someone speaking Latin, which is equally ridiculous. Rightly, therefore, does Ciruelo of Daroca <note>Pedro Ciruelo (c. 1470–1548), a Spanish mathematician and theologian who wrote against superstition</note> condemn these numerical psalmists in *Part 3 of "On Superstitions," chapter 6, at the end.*

Moreover, the fraud and deceit of the numerical Magi is also detected in what the Arch-Magus <note>likely referring to a high-ranking practitioner or a specific author like Agrippa</note> handed down from Proclus: that they do not deal with sensible and material number, but with "formal" (as they call it) and rational number. Indeed, the formal reason of number is nothing other than that very thing by which many individuals are "many" and recede from unity; whether this be a pure negation or something positive, it certainly adds nothing effective for acting upon individuals. Doubtless these scoundrels hide something under this term "formal number" which pertains to the operation of a demon by a pact. This conjecture is strongly confirmed because the ancient heretics, who attributed much to numbers, were more than once convicted of the same Magic. <margin>b Book 1, chapter 23.</margin> Irenaeus reveals this regarding the Basilidians,^b after whom others follow.^c <margin>c Tertullian, *On Prescription*, chapter 46; Epiphanius, *Heresies*, 24; and others.</margin> The same is certain regarding Marcus, that most ancient heretic,^d <margin>d Irenaeus, book 1, chapter 12.</margin> and regarding all the Gnostics.^e <margin>e Irenaeus, book 2, chapter 27.</margin>

Therefore, one must read cautiously the things which Peter Gregory <note>Pierre Grégoire (1540–1597), a French jurist and occult philosopher</note> heaps up—but does not sufficiently digest—concerning the efficacy of the odd number being greater than the even in his *Syntax of the Admirable Art*^f: <margin>f book 15, chapter 10.</margin> by attributing that opinion to the orthodox fathers St. Jerome and Cyprian, he does them a great injury. Since he cites none of their passages, he is rightly accused of vanity in this matter; for while the Fathers discourse on the mysteries of the odd number (such as the Unity and Trinity seen in the Holy Trinity) and its excellence, he—not to say anything more severe—inconsiderately related this to some unknown physical power, along with Agrippa the Magician and heretic, whom he joins as a witness to the Fathers. See St. Jerome’s *Apology for the books against Jovinian* near the end, and you will find these things are rightly said by me, and that Jerome cites Origen, Tertullian, and Cyprian, whom Peter Gregory has attacked. One must also be careful not to drink in any vain superstition from the book of Fabio Paolini, *On the Number Seven*: for the many things he related, not so much from his own opinion as from that of the Platonists, are poorly suited to Catholic Theology—such as the fact that they quite openly attributed Magical power to musical numbers. <margin>On the power of music.</margin> For the things the poets invented concerning the power of music, the Platonists transferred into the virtue of Numbers *as* numbers.

He critiques scholars like Peter Gregory and Fabio Paolini for mixing Pythagorean/Platonic number theory with Christian doctrine.</summary>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

<meta>catchword: osten- (showing/demonstrating)</meta>

They had written of the music of Orpheus, that it commanded demons, brute animals, rocks, and trees. For thus says Ovid, regarding the trees:

<margin>D</margin>
> — — — — to which place, after the prophet
> Born of the gods <note>Orpheus, son of Apollo and the Muse Calliope</note> sat down and moved the sounding strings;
> A shade came to the spot: the tree of Chaonia was not absent,
> Nor the grove of the Heliades, nor the winter-oak with its high foliage,
> Nor the soft lindens, nor the beech and the unwedded laurel.
> You also, flexible ivies, came, and together
> The vine-bearing shoots, and the elms clothed with vines, etc.

*Metamorphoses*, book 10. And Seneca in *The Mad Hercules* adds concerning demons, wild beasts, rocks, and rivers:

> The art which had drawn the forests, and waters, and rocks,
> Which had provided delays for the rivers:
> At whose sound the wild beasts had stood still;
> Now soothes the inhabitants of the underworld with unaccustomed voices.

Horace adds the winds:

> From whence the woods followed the tuneful
> — — — — Orpheus blindly;
> By his mother’s art <note>The Muse Calliope</note> delaying the rapid
> Glidings of rivers, and the swift winds.

Virgil in the 4th *Georgic* and 6th *Aeneid*, Ovid, Seneca, Horace, and Propertius have handed down the same regarding the spirits of the dead and demons; and Manilius, having briefly embraced these things, added a fiction regarding the stars:

> But the Lyre, with its horns spread through the sky, is seen
> Among the stars; with which Orpheus once captured
> Everything he had touched with song; and through the spirits of the dead themselves
> He made his way and tamed the laws of the underworld with his poem.
> To this instrument belongs a similar honor, and a power from a similar cause:
> Then drawing forests and rocks, now it leads the stars,
> And carries away the immense orb of the revolving world.

Behold how they extend the force of musical rhythm into the water, air, earth, heaven, stars, and the underworld. But I also find this in Pliny, concerning song: *Many believe that the works of potteries are broken in such a manner; and that serpents are contracted by the song of the Muses, even in the quiet of the night. Book 28, chapter 22.* Although our friend <note>Likely a contemporary scholar or correspondent</note>—who has deserved the best from the world of letters—believes that a reason for this should not be sought, but that it is a certain old wives' opinion of the ancients; nevertheless, because some mention is made in the Holy Scriptures concerning the incantation of serpents, as I shall show in the following book, it seems the reason ought to be diligently investigated.

<margin>E</margin> First of all, I think a distinction must be made between musical modes and magical whispers, or "evil songs" <note>original: *mala carmina*, referring to incantations or hexes</note>: in the latter there is no natural power, but the demon, by a pact, puts serpents to sleep, shatters jars, and does such things; in the former, there is a physical power. But so that we may understand what and how much power is present, again a distinction must be made between those things endowed with sense, and those devoid of sense. For I do not doubt that animals endowed with the sense of hearing can be captured by musical modes, and to that extent I would admit what the aforementioned poets bring forward concerning the wondrous effects of music, and as Martianus Capella says in his book *On Music*, while reciting an *old man's fable*. For I do not believe what Fabius Paullinus wishes to obtain further; but in those things which pertain to animals with ears, I confess they are plausible, as also in the words of Eusebius *In Praise of Constantine*; but those things which pertain to insensible objects, I think are to be rejected and are ridiculous, such as those concerning rocks and forests, etc. And therefore, I do not agree with the argument of Plutarch in his *Symposium* and Solinus in chapter 7 of his *Polyhistor*, by which they argue from Arion’s dolphin to the walls of the Megarians or Thebans <note>Referring to the myth that the walls of Thebes were built by the music of Amphion</note>. A wild beast could be captured by a song, but rocks could not. Nevertheless, that learned man thinks, at least for the sake of disputing (as he says), he should show—

# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION III.

<page-num>35</page-num>

<warning>A dark brown stain/smudge is present on the right-center of the page, though text remains legible.</warning>

...it could be shown that this attraction of forests and rocks was real. But later <margin>page 184.</margin> he protests that he asserts everything he brings forward as proof solely according to the mind of the Platonists <note>Followers of Plato who often integrated mysticism and mathematics into their philosophy.</note>; nor does he assent to them, except insofar as they agree with the sanctions of the Holy Church. This was done prudently and cautiously for himself: but he would have provided even better for others if, after he had so diligently and warmly proposed those ravings of the poets and Platonists for several chapters, he had applied a censure or an antidote to each from the apothecary of Catholic Theology, lest the less learned be deceived by those decorated words. He did not do so: therefore, it will be worth the effort if I attempt to provide what I judge to be by no means foreign to our purpose: albeit briefly and by indicating what the reader must beware of in his arguments.

First, <margin>chapter 5.</margin> he cites the poets and those Pagan writers whom I have already cited; I shall not repeat to what extent they are to be believed. Nor do the words of Nicolaus Vicentinus <note>A 16th-century music theorist known for his work on ancient Greek intervals.</note> the musician pertain to those fables of Amphion and Orpheus; but rather to the true effects which the ancients recorded. Nor does Steuco of Gubbio <note>Agostino Steuco (1497–1548), a scholar and librarian of the Vatican.</note> in the beginning of his commentary on Psalm 67 bring forward anything other than a bare commemoration of the Thracian fable. These remarks shall suffice regarding the authorities cited by him.

<margin>Chapters 6 & 7.</margin> He proposes seven modes in total by which Orpheus could have performed these wonders; before we discuss them, I hope there is no one who will not grant me this: he who proves that something *could* have happened does not immediately prove that it *did* happen. What then? He who promised he would demonstrate it has not even proven it to be probable (unless I am greatly mistaken), except by his final reason, which contributes nothing at all to the power of music. And at the start, he lays the foundation of all his modes, which is of this sort: *the heavens move by harmony, and especially the planets themselves.* What if I deny this according to Aristotle? Will not the entire sketch of the building be erased? I shall act more kindly. Let it be so. What then? This Harmony, as Proclus <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> and other Platonists themselves testify, is not perceptible by the bodily sense, but only by the soul and mind; he who would deny that rocks and trees lack such a mind must surely be a crag or a blockhead himself. Next, he says, *all lower things are subject to the planets, and receive power from the stars.* This, because it is stated too indefinitely and universally, we certainly deny. He proves it by the experiment of flowers and herbs, which change, move, grow, and wither according to the increases of the moon and the turnings of the sun; also of animals which follow the natures of the constellations; and of stones, for which the law is the same, such as the Heliotrope <note>A green stone with red spots, once believed to turn the sun red when placed in water.</note> which imitates the sun, the Selenite <note>A transparent variety of gypsum, named for the moon (Selene).</note> which imitates the moon, the Helioselinum <note>Literally "sun-moon" plant; likely a type of parsley or celery believed to follow both bodies.</note> which imitates both stars, and the magnet which follows the Little Bear <note>original: "cynosurae" - the constellation Ursa Minor, used for navigation.</note>. Yet we say all these things are to be referred to certain hidden qualities <term>occult qualities: properties of matter that are not immediately apparent to the senses, often used in Renaissance science to explain magnetism or medicinal effects</term> implanted in those things. For if this does not depend on the peculiar nature of these stones, animals, and shrubs; if all this arises from that subjection of lower bodies to the stars; why do not all other herbs, animals, and stones suffer the same? But he proceeds, and mentions the wonderful effects of sympathy and antipathy, and contends they are born from the fact that all natural things are marked by those rays of the stars dedicated to them, participating in a peculiar character of their virtue; and that the higher bodies pour their powers into these lower ones. I am forced to repeat some things from <margin>chapter 3, question 1</margin> and lightly taste of them again. I admit that the spheres of the heavens and the stars influence the powers in lower things to some extent; but not just anything indiscriminately into anything, but according to the nature of the substance or essence of each thing, aptly and diversely, and furthermore according to the disposition of qualities and the condition of individual instances. For in this way, it is not the diamond, but the magnet that turns; not toward the closer signs of the East, but toward the North. If the Little Bear influences the magnet because it is lower, why not iron, lead, or other stones? Furthermore, it is of much more excellent efficacy to bestow the power of attracting upon another than to attract that thing itself. For example, the Little Bear would need a more powerful influence to give the magnet the power of alluring iron than to attract the magnet itself. Nor does it follow that because the magnet attracts iron, and the magnet is attracted by the Little Bear, therefore the magnet received the power of attracting iron from the influence of the Little Bear. What can be such a great force of efficacy...

<meta>The catchword "siderum" (of the stars) indicates the first word of the following page.</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the Platonist view that music and celestial harmony can physically move inanimate objects like rocks and trees, arguing instead for "occult qualities" inherent in specific materials rather than a universal celestial influence.</summary>
<keywords>Orpheus, Platonism, celestial harmony, magnetism, occult qualities, astrology, Nicolaus Vicentinus, Agostino Steuco, Aristotle, sympathy and antipathy</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues a theological and philosophical critique of magical claims regarding the influence of stars on earthly objects, specifically addressing the musical and sympathetic powers attributed to celestial bodies.</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

of the stars, as if they could act upon things so different in appearance and kind; winds, rivers, mountains, forests, rocks, demons; could a musical sound effectively make all these things move by some operating command? In what way could the same sound of a single lyre attract such diverse things? In vain would you flee to "sympathies" and "antipathies" <note>Natural attractions or repulsions between objects</note>: for these do not flow down from the stars, nor are they rained down from the celestial spheres, but they follow from the very differences and specific forms of the things themselves. As for those "peculiar characters" of the stars which the Platonists boast about; the Theologians (that is, the Christian philosophers) have long since exploded those ideas as fictitious and vehemently suspected of superstition. Let us therefore bid those ideas farewell, because we are Catholics, and because we detest superstition. The things he adds are of no greater weight. For neither did natural magic, when it wishes *the lowest things to be in the highest, and the highest to lie hidden in the lowest*, intend to introduce such a confused chaos that it wants all the lowest to be in all the highest, and all the highest in all the lowest; but rather certain things in certain others: namely in those whose specific forms are suitable for receiving the influx of the highest things: a condition which is lacking in those insensitive objects.

There is one and only one God; in whom alone all other things exist with a certain excellent and eminent dignity: He alone is the harmony of Himself and of the universe: and only in this sense is it true that *harmony holds the whole together* <note>original: ἁρμονίαν συνέχειν τὸ πᾶν</note>: because, clearly, God has disposed all things in number, weight, and measure. As for what Antoine Mizauld <note>A 16th-century French physician and astrologer</note> further argued in his *book on the harmony of heaven and earth*, it was done by him neither piously nor philosophically. As for what Macrobius asserts, that *everything that lives is captured by music*: if we speak of the music of physical and true sound, this is true only of those creatures which are truly endowed with the sense of hearing: if you take it to mean that metaphorical, imaginary, or speculative music, which is placed in a certain bare quantitative proportion; it has nothing to do with Dionysius, as they say. For such music would lead rocks and forests only in an imaginary and metaphorical sense. That *everything in the world is animated and lives by a certain Soul of the World* <note>The "Anima Mundi," a concept that the universe itself is a living being</note> is a mere fiction of the pagan philosophers, long ago condemned in the schools of the Theologians: since there is no soul of the world separate from the substantial forms of individual things. If rocks are alive, as Philoponus (in book 1 *On the Soul*) and others wished to believe following Thales and Timaeus, and as Paulinus strives to prove with many arguments in chapter 6; they live by their own soul, not a common one; and by a specific soul, such as that by which trees are alive, which is merely vegetative, not sensitive. Nor is anything else proved by the arguments of Hippocrates in his *book on the nature of the child* or Plotinus in the 4th *Ennead*, book 4. Truly, what kind of idea is this, and is it to be tolerated by the Church? That *the stars are endowed with sight and reason*, which is said on *page 108*; the erroneous falsehood of this dogma was most learnedly demonstrated many years ago by Espence in his *book on the animation of the heavens*.

But they say *various stones are moved in various ways by the rays of the stars*, such as the selenite <note>Moonstone</note>, which not only imitates the moon in shape and colors, but also in motion, for it follows it constantly, or rather is carried around with it; or the *magnet*, which has also received the power of handing over and transferring motion to others; or the *pantaura*, a stone found by Apollonius, which itself draws other stones; or the *androdamas*, which draws silver, copper, and iron; or that *runaway stone of Cyzicus*, which the Argonauts had left there to use as an anchor, and which the people of Cyzicus often fastened with lead when it fled from the Prytaneum; or the various *automata* <note>Mechanical devices or "robots"</note> of Boethius and others, which received this motion from the rays of the stars, penetrating into those lower places and bodies, and acting upon them: just as an image is cast *into a glass mirror*, and a wall *reflects a voice*, and *fire acts upon sulfur and tinder*. In my opinion, these examples cannot move me. For these "rays" are established without any necessity, and we have refuted them elsewhere. Those automata, since they are artificial, received their power from human skill through certain instruments supported by mathematical proportions, cords, wheels, etc., and not from the stars; and they were affected and moved from within, not from without: as can be seen in that figure of Saint Francis, and several others, which the most serene Archduke of Austria, Duke of Styria, Carinthia, etc., Ferdinand (son of Charles, grandson of Ferdinand) [received] from his ancestors...

<summary>The author refutes the idea that stars or a "World Soul" animate physical objects like rocks or magnets, arguing instead for natural properties and human mechanical ingenuity (automata).</summary>
<keywords>natural magic, astrology, harmony of the spheres, Anima Mundi, automata, magnetism, superstition, Catholic theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's systematic refutation of Neoplatonic and astrological theories regarding the source of magical or medicinal power in earthly objects. The previous section likely introduced the argument that certain gems or herbs derive power from the stars; here, the author argues that these powers are instead innate to the objects' own "specific forms."</meta>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION III.

[The man] most worthy by his own religious zeal and virtue, possesses it. *Fire* acts upon sulfur and fuel, but not upon other things, because those others lack the dispositions required for sudden inflammation. *Mirrors* reflect images solely by the transparency of a polished surface and a brilliant evenness, finished with a suitable backing. An *Echo* results only from the repercussion of a voice: and all these things occur indiscriminately, regardless of which planet or star is dominant. You may observe this also in the *magnet*, the "man-slayer" stone <note>original: *androdamante*; a legendary variety of magnet or diamond believed to tame passions or resist fire</note>, the *pantaura* <note>a mythical "all-bright" solar stone</note>, and other stones. From this it is clear that this power is implanted in these gems not by a celestial body, but by their own **form and species** (which I am forced to repeat often, because my adversaries insist on the same errors so often), with the heavens bestowing nothing beyond their common and ordinary influence. And so, the *selenites* <note>Moonstone; the author argues its changes follow its own nature rather than a direct "command" from the Moon</note>, on account of some hidden quality flowing from its **specific difference** <note term="specific difference">In Aristotelian logic, the "differentia specifica" is the essential trait that distinguishes one species from another within the same genus.</note>, produces those admirable changes of colors and movement; this is because that quality is joined to lunar qualities by a certain friendly similarity of nature and (if I may speak thus) by a bond of physical love. It is no wonder we cannot fully explain these qualities; for the very sources of them—namely, the specific differences—are hidden from us.

Nevertheless, the specific difference proves they must be posited. For different species require different natural dispositions: and it is discovered by physicians through experience that some things act by their whole selves—that is, by reason of their specific difference or form; while others are found to act only by one particular quality suited to certain effects. Such things are naturally fit to exercise that power at any time, and there is no need to wait until this or that star is dominant.

The vanity of this Platonic fiction shines forth even more clearly if you consider more closely the very method which they extract from these refuted arguments; you have this expressed thus on **page one hundred and ninety-seven**:

-> "Just as from a certain mixture and composition of herbs and vapors, prepared through the medical and astronomical art, a certain common form is forged—like a harmony—adorned with the gifts and, as it were, the dowry of the stars; as in *theriaca* <note term="theriaca">Theriac: a famous ancient "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients, often including viper flesh.</note>, which is thought to have marvelous power against approaching old age and poison, with three virtues consenting equally within it: the first celestial, summoned through the artifice of timely mixing; the second likewise celestial, but naturally implanted in its parts; and the third, finally, elementary. So from tones, first selected according to the rule of the stars, then composed among themselves according to their very model and agreement, a common form of harmony, as it were, is gathered, and in it a celestial power also arises: a celestial power responding to this similarly tempered harmony; which virtue, summoned from heaven, is able to bend minds, soothe them, and drive them to anger or restrain them." <-

In these words, I see a certain heap of false suppositions. 
**I.** Because he wants *theriaca* and other medicines to be composed and mixed through astrological observations, I ask: at what time must that position of the stars be considered by the pharmacist? When the herbs are being gathered, or when they are being mixed, or both? If both, that "Great Platonic Year" <note>A cycle of roughly 25,800 years; the author is being sarcastic about the impossibility of perfect timing.</note> must be waited for, and meanwhile the virtue of the herbs will wither away. If when they are gathered; yet when they are mixed, there will perhaps be a contrary influence. If when they are mixed; this mixing does not happen in one day, but often over many. Then, how can it be that—since there is such diversity, and even sometimes contrary qualities in the herbs to be mixed—one and the same influence of heaven at one time can grant to each thing what each requires, and what suits the whole? 

Likewise, that "common form" is merely a Platonic chimera, and a certain "being of reason" <note>A mental construct with no basis in physical reality.</note>, subsisting or existing nowhere: as is also that first celestial power depending on a certain superstitious observation of days, months, and years. Finally, they want this power to be mixed with the first virtue summoned from heaven (do you not seem, reader, to hear the magical rites of Numa concerning *Jupiter Elicius* <note>Numa Pompilius, legendary King of Rome, was said to have "elicited" Jupiter from the sky via ritual.</note>, and the like?); but they proceed: that for the summoning, it is...

D

<meta>This page continues the critique of Neoplatonic and "natural" magic, specifically focusing on the theories of Marsilio Ficinus and Fabio Paullini regarding the power of music and words to influence the physical world and the heavens.</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

<page-num>38</page-num>

<header>INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC</header>

...knowledge of the correspondence of musical tones with the rules is necessary; for this knowledge, according to the testimony of Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficinus (1433–1499), a central figure of the Italian Renaissance who attempted to harmonize Platonic philosophy with Christianity.</note>, astronomers prescribe three rules. The first is to investigate what powers each star, constellation, and aspect possesses within itself, and what effects it produces of its own accord; what each of these brings and what it takes away; and all these things must be introduced into the sounds and the meanings of words; one must reject those things which take away—and the musician must loathe them—but approve those which bring [influence]. The second is to consider which star chiefly rules over which place, or thing, or person; and to observe what kind of sounds and songs those people commonly use, and from what genus of harmony any thing is constituted by nature (that is, by what proportion and in what manner), so that the musician himself may employ similar ones, together with the meanings which he strives to express to those same stars.

The third rule is to observe the daily positions and aspects of the stars, and under these to explore to what speeches, songs, movements, dances, customs, and acts men are most incited, and what effects individual things are accustomed to; so that he may employ similar things to the best of his ability in songs, and imitate the heavens to please those who are similar and likely to receive a similar influence.] Thus far their views: all of which we have rejected as futile and as certain coverings and wrappings of forbidden magic in *chapter 3, question 1, and chapter 4, question 1*, and, as far as those signifying words are concerned, we shall reject them in the next question. Their first rule rests upon false foundations and requires an observation for which no man’s life—not even the centuries of a crow <note>A classical reference to the long life-span attributed to crows in antiquity.</note>—would suffice; it is also in itself most uncertain. The second is not free from error and heresy, as it supposes that the stars rule over men, a matter we shall discuss (God willing) in the *third book of our disputations against astrological divination.* The third, insofar as it concerns moving things devoid of sense by song or voice, must be judged ridiculous and unworthy of philosophers, as I have taught.

Since, therefore, such are the foundations of the Platonists, it is not obscure to those who see anything how solidly and prudently those seven modes are built upon sand; by which [Paullini] asserts in *chapter 7* that Orpheus was truly and actually able to draw rocks and woods along with living creatures to the melodies of his lyre. But what are these modes?

*The first* is that this could happen by the very power of music eliciting, drawing down, and attracting the influence of the heavens, as I said.] This mode rests on two false hypotheses: namely, that there is a certain common <term>Soul of the World</term> <note>Anima Mundi: the idea that the universe is a living, ensouled being.</note> composed of harmonic numbers pervading individual things, which is delighted by musical concord; likewise, that the heavens and stars are animated by a soul capable of reason, which can be stirred by anger and soothed by sonorous pleasure. All of which, as Paullini himself confesses, are disapproved of by Theologians.

*The second* is that song is endowed with this power, not only by reason of sound, but also from the occult power of words, by which they judge that heavenly power can be drawn down from its supernal seat; and they confirm this by other effects of words full of portents—all of which Paullini, following the prescription of Theologians, prudently does not deny should be referred to the frauds of demons (*pages 204 and 206*).

*The third* is dependent on that astrological observation to which Ptolemy, Albumazar <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886), a famous Persian astrologer.</note>, and similar men attributed much: this too Paullini (*page 207*) numbers among Magic; he ought to have added "illicit and demoniacal," lest anyone take it for natural magic.

*The fourth* is sought from the fact that the proportions of numbers have a power similar to figures: for the efficacy of figures shaped with certain lines and points is wonderful; and because the lyre of Orpheus [was fashioned] after the figure of the celestial Lyre...

<meta>catchword: ...was</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the Neoplatonic theory that music and words can magically influence the world and the stars, specifically refuting the "rules" of Ficino and the claims of Fabio Paullini regarding Orpheus.</summary>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the legendary powers of Orpheus’s music, specifically refuting various naturalistic or philosophical explanations for his ability to move inanimate objects.</meta>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION III.

...had been shaped. But this reasoning—and the delusion of the Platonists regarding the power of geometric shapes—is rightly considered by Paullinus, along with other theologians, to be mere trifles and dreams; he adds that such studies are prohibited by the Councils and the decrees of the Popes. I myself refuted this in Question 1, Chapter 4.

**The fifth method** is derived from a certain extraordinary force of nature, which was granted to Orpheus alone (or perhaps a few others). This was instilled in him through a principle of "sympathy" <note>The Renaissance concept of 'sympathy' suggested a hidden natural bond between different objects in the universe.</note> with all things, via certain individual conditions suited for this purpose. We see that nature has implanted wonderful virtues in countless things, many examples of which Paullinus accumulates *from page 210*; however, he mixes the true with the false, and the proven with the unproven. He mingles things we read in Holy Scripture with things read in the poets, and things performed by divine miracle with those accomplished by natural force. 

For what does the strength of Samson have in common with nature? Or what natural virtue is implanted in his locks or hair? Neither do the Kings of France heal the "King's Evil" <term>struma: a swelling of the neck glands, historically believed to be cured by the touch of a monarch</term> by their touch through any natural power. Neither the speaking ass of Balaam, nor the bell of Vililla, nor the little fish of the monastery of St. Maurice should be numbered among the wonders of nature. Nor is it likely that the power to attract stones and forests was given to Orpheus by nature. For of the examples that stand on physical reason and are cited by him, all exert their force upon the material of only one species, such that they can do nothing of the sort to another. 

A magnet attracts iron, but a smaller mass of gold <note>original: "Magnes ferrum attrahit, auri minorem molem."</note>. The Tentyritae <note>A people of ancient Egypt famous for their ability to hunt crocodiles.</note> flee from crocodiles, not from asps or hippopotamuses. A drum made of wolf-skin has power over a drum of lamb-skin, but not over one of calf-skin. The lion fears the crowing of a rooster, but the fox does not; and so it must be said of the rest. Yet these men believe Orpheus had the power to lead away by his song not only oaks, but all species of trees without exception; and not only trees, but also rocks and marbles, the very cliffs and vast mountains—Ossa, Rhodope, etc.—and also animals, not only the teachable ones like the elephant, the dolphin, and the deer, but especially the most wild and dull-witted beasts of every species.

**The sixth method** teaches an ingenious and natural mixing of the elements. But even if this should sometimes accomplish marvelous things, it is superfluous in the question we are now discussing. For here, no natural mixing of elements intervenes; there is only the naked striking of the air by the sound of the lyre, a thing which cannot attract inanimate objects, nor soothe those lacking the sense of hearing. I find nothing of a natural mixing of elements in song, but only that imaginary mixture of three kinds of forces, which I have shown to be non-existent.

**The seventh** is a certain attractive force, either enchanting or magical. Plotinus, in his 4th book, wishes this to happen either from certain occult qualities of things, or from shapes, or song, or desire, or imagination. We have already rejected the second and third modes. We also reject the fourth, which attributes an effective power to the imagination over things far distant from the one imagining. But I have also refuted the first, showing that no physical "occult quality" can be given that is able to operate simultaneously in the same way upon things of such different species, or even different genera; since it would be necessary for it to act as a cause—not a primary or universal one, but a secondary and particular one. For no singular quality exists in any singular thing (insofar as it is singular) that does not belong to and flow from its individual dispositions; and therefore, it can produce nothing but a particular effect proportioned to itself.

It remains, therefore, that **those things reported concerning the attraction of inanimate objects are either fables or the operation of demons, as Pausanias most truly judged in Book 6.** For I remember reading in Suidas <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia.</note> that Orpheus was a distinguished magician.

<summary>The author systematically rejects naturalistic, elemental, and philosophical explanations for Orpheus's legendary ability to move inanimate objects, concluding that such feats are either myths or the result of demonic magic.</summary>
<keywords>Orpheus, natural magic, occult qualities, sympathy, magnetism, demonology, Plotinus, Pausanias</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the natural and supernatural effects of music and words, specifically addressing the phenomenon of "Tarantism" and the efficacy of incantations.</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

Let us add another wonderful effect of music. They call it the *Tarantula*, an insect named after the city of Taranto in Apulia <note>A region in Southern Italy</note>, where it is common. There are two kinds of it, which Bustamantius <note>Juan Gil González de Bustamante, a Spanish naturalist</note> in his book on the animals of Holy Scripture and others do not seem to distinguish sufficiently. One is of the lizard kind, which can be called a *stellio* <note>a spotted lizard</note>, and which the Spaniards call *salmanquesa* <note>salamander or gecko</note>, of which we are not speaking now. The other is of the spider or *phalangium* kind, unknown in Spain (as far as I know). Regarding this one, I shall relate a peculiar detail from Mattioli’s <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century physician</note> commentary on Dioscorides, Book 2, Chapter 57. It wanders in the fields during summer and crawls out of little holes in the earth, biting harvesters whose legs are not protected by boots. Once bitten, some sing perpetually, others laugh, others weep, others dance, others shout, others sleep, others are afflicted with sleeplessness, others suffer from vomiting, others sweat, others become trembling, others are infested with terrors, and others suffer various other discomforts, becoming like those who are frenzied, delirious, or manic.

*But it is truly a marvel (Mattioli adds) how easily the force of this poison is soothed by music. For, as I myself can attest, upon hearing stringed instruments or the sound of flutes, they immediately cease their languishing at the first encounter and begin to caper and dance in the midst of it all. They continue this as if they were healthy and held by no pain whatsoever. However, if it happens that the flute-players rest even a little or pause, not long after, the victims fall to the ground and return to their former languor—unless they dance and caper to an unceasing sound until the virus of the poison is driven out and dissipated, partly imperceptibly through the pores of the skin and partly through sweat. For this reason, flute-players are hired for a fee and changed in shifts so that the bitten may dance without any interruption of the sound until they are fully cured. Meanwhile, while this is happening, there are those who strengthen them with antidotes, namely theriac <note>a famous ancient polypharmaceutical herbal jam used as an antitoxin</note>, mithridatium <note>a semi-mythical remedy against poison</note>, and others that oppose poisonous diseases.*

Thus he writes; to which it seems should be added that music helps because that poison instills melancholic and delirious affections. Therefore, since music (as the Saint <note>Likely referring to St. Augustine or another Church Father</note> professes) generates joy in the soul, the contrary harm is expelled if it finds the body's humors suited to this. This is confirmed because not just any melodies benefit any bitten person, but specific ones for specific people. From this it arose that some, not considering this—who had encountered those to whom the appropriate melody was not applied—dared to deny this experience, which is otherwise very well known to "the bleary-eyed and the barbers" <note>A Latin idiom meaning "everyone knows this."</note>. I have heard some add that if the tarantula that bit someone should die, even in another place, the one bitten by it is immediately freed from the disease. If this is true, the miracle of this poison is indeed to be wondered at, the cause of which is not clear to me. For what dependence has a poison so remote from its source or origin? Or what is this operation across such a distance?
