# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION III.

<margin>
a Book 28, chapter 2.
b Pico della Mirandola in his *Magic Propositions*, Ficino in Book 2 of *On the Life of Scholars*, Pomponazzi, etc. Also from the Canon Law, see also Grillando *On Fortune-telling* question 8, and Pierre Le Loyer Book 1 of *On Specters* the last chapter, and Book 4, chapter 4.
</margin>
Regarding Paracelsus <note>A famous 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist</note>: Pliny <gloss>a</gloss>, and with him certain more recent authors <gloss>b</gloss> who are too devoted to Plato, hold various views. But all of these rely on different foundations. Some impiously deny that we should care whether the work of demons intervenes in these matters or not (such as Paracelsus), who thereby sufficiently indicates that these things are not accomplished naturally; and to say this is heretical. Others (such as the physicians I cited, and Pliny) cling to experiments alone; they too lightly attribute the things they see happening to the very physical means they observe, thinking nothing of the things they do not see. The Platonists, likewise, are less devoted to natural science but too ready to believe in magical trifles; they easily accepted many superstitious things as natural. Against all of these, we establish this

<margin>B</margin>
**Conclusion: [No words have a natural power to heal wounds or diseases, or to drive away other harms.]**

<margin>Augustus Thuanus in *Prometheus Bound* <note>original: "Parabata vincto"</note> says, "Words heal the diseases of the body," etc., which is certainly erroneous. Unless one wishes to excuse them by saying they speak of words as they rely on a pact; but this would be to attribute power to words improperly, for the power is not from the words, but from a settled pact, and indeed one made through a demon.</margin>
I said, *No words* (denying this of any kind of words) whether uttered by mouth or contained in writing; whether single and simple (as they call them) or complex, and related in some proposition or speech in verse or prose; whether meaningful or signifying nothing; whether in Hebrew or another language; whether spoken with a hiss or a breath, or otherwise, in any way whatsoever, whether the sick person is absent or present.

<margin>
The Law of Jealousy
Numbers 5.
</margin>
I said, *Natural power*, because that adjuration and law of **THE LAW OF JEALOUSY** <note>original: "TORATH KINA," referring to the ritual in Numbers 5:11-31</note>, whatever efficacy it had, it had from above; almost as today, Church prayers, the formulas of the Sacraments and Sacramentals, and exorcisms have a supernatural power from God’s institution or His gracious way of concurring. It is not our purpose to discuss that matter now. For (as must be noted for this entire subject) we are not now dealing with a "natural work" as it is opposed to an "animal work," or a "free work," or a "violent work," but as it is opposed to an "artificial work," or even a "supernatural work." For the perfection of this universe, God first established a certain *order of nature*, while He bestowed upon individual things their own nature and peculiar essence, and gave to each its own operations fitting to that nature; these are called *natural operations*, because they are suitable to the nature of the subject. Next, God added another *supernatural order*, which can be divided into two species. The first is the *order of grace or the miraculous* (for I take these two as the same for now), to which order belong certain operations that surpass the powers of all men and Angels; the principle of these operations is not the nature of an individual thing, but that very grace of God, His absolute will, and omnipotence: these are called *operations of grace and supernatural* in the strict sense of the word, and likewise *miraculous operations*. The other is the *prodigious order*, which in reality does not exceed the limits of the natural order, but is only said to exceed it by reason of the "manner" <note>the method or mechanism</note>, which either all men or most men are ignorant of; and therefore we are accustomed to call it also "supernatural" in a broad sense of the word, but it is more clearly and significantly called the *preternatural* order. To this are referred many marvelous operations performed by good or evil angels through local motion or the sudden application of natural agents. But since in these, the effect does not conflict with the essence of the nature of things, nor does the manner of operating surpass angelic powers, therefore such effects are rather "natural" in a broad sense than "supernatural" or "miraculous" properly speaking; and "preternatural" rather than contrary to nature or violent. However, because they are commonly judged to surpass the order of nature, they are usually counted as such; but properly and expressly they should be called preternatural, or wondrous, or prodigious. Therefore, God established three orders: the natural, the miraculous, and the prodigious; or, if you prefer two, the natural and the supernatural. To these, man added another order, namely that of *Artificial things*; when, by adapting and applying natural things to an end intended by himself, he bestowed different forms upon things which nature would not have given them unless the industry of the human intellect had labored at it. However, human industry can never—

<sig>D 3</sig>

He distinguishes between the natural, supernatural (miraculous), and preternatural (prodigious) orders to explain how effects are produced in the world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limits of natural magic and the efficacy of incantations, specifically addressing whether words possess inherent power to heal or affect the physical world.</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

industry can accomplish anything that is contrary to the nature of things: therefore, this artificial order is added to the natural order, but it does not destroy it; rather, it serves it instead of transcending it. In this and other questions of this treatise, we take the "natural" only in that first sense, so as to exclude those three other orders: the artificial, the prodigious <note>extraordinary or ominous events</note>, and the miraculous.

It must be noted, moreover, that a natural effect is not produced by nature except through motion or change. Health, however, must necessarily be brought about by change. Change does not happen without the power of active qualities. Active qualities require a suitable subject for operation, one that is properly prepared for action.

<margin>
† Book 1, at the beginning.
D. on extraordinary knowledge.
g Hippocrates, Book on the Sacred Disease. Galen, Books 6 and 10 on Simple Medicines.
</margin>

From this foundation, a conclusion rises strongly against all the maneuvers of our adversaries; it was the conclusion of all the Fathers (except perhaps Origen), of St. Thomas, Gerson, and all theologians (as far as I know). It was also the view of Ulpian the Jurist, who denied that incantations should be numbered among medicines. It was the view of the healthier physicians^g and philosophers^h.

<margin>
Fernel, On Hidden Causes, Book 2, chapter on supernatural cures. Benivieni, Book on the Hidden Causes of Disease. Jo. Lange, Epistle 33. Francisco Valles, On Sacred Philosophy, chapter 3. Auger Ferrier, Book 2 of Method, chapter 11. Scaliger, Book 16 on Subtlety, chapter 349. Condronchi, Book 2 on Venefic Diseases <note>diseases caused by poisoning or sorcery</note>, chapter 5. Bolcelius, Book on Love Potions, chapter 1.
</margin>

As we prevail by authority, so we do by reason. For we reason thus: Words have no power—neither artificial, nor natural, nor supernatural—to produce effects of this kind. Regarding artificial power, no one has ever even suspected it. If the power were supernatural, it would belong to grace or a miracle; but this cannot be said, because God has promised no such thing, nor revealed to the Church anything like what the Magi boast of. Nor does God assist magical incantations, nor does He work through Beelzebub in such a way as to communicate His glory to him in the measure He is accustomed to give to the Angels and the Blessed. From this it appears how incautiously that recent interpreter of Homer, Spondanus^i, is wont to repeat that axiom: *“That it belongs to the goodness of God, and pertains to His glory, to have granted such admirable effects to a creature, namely the demon.”* But he was a Calvinist when he wrote those things as a young man; had he been more mature in age, when he repented and returned to the Catholics, he would never have blurted this out. If this power is not supernatural, it remains that it is either prodigious or natural. If it is prodigious, it is not by the ministry of good Angels, because (as I will show later) they do not involve themselves in magical operations; therefore, it is by the ministry of evil angels. This, some adversaries deny, but they cannot escape the force of the argument; others confess it, and are therefore also forced to confess that there is no power inherent in the words themselves. <margin>C</margin>

<margin>
h Theophrastus, Book 9 of the History of Plants. Plutarch in the Life of Pericles. Rabbi Maimonides, Book 1 of the Perplexed, chapter 72. Leonardo Vair, Book 2 on Fascination, chapter 11. Antonio Mirandola, Book 26 on Singular Harmony. Grevin, Book 1 on Poisons.
i In the Odyssey, dealing with Lycanthropes <note>werewolves</note>.
</margin>

Finally, these words have no natural power. For if the words are written, they are something dead and devoid of all energy; if they have any power, they must receive it from the temperament of the ink or the paper on which they are inscribed, and therefore they do not operate by their own proper force. But words uttered by the mouth have only the power to strike the air by contact, just like the sounds of animals and other inanimate things. But sound is no more able to change the sense of touch than color is to change hearing, or sweetness to change sight, because they are not the objects of those senses. Therefore, since it is necessary for the sense of touch to be changed for the healing of the body, they must admit that the mere utterance or writing of words contributes nothing to the healing of diseases.

Pomponazzi <note>Pietro Pomponazzi, a Renaissance philosopher known for seeking naturalistic explanations for the supernatural</note> responds that words change the hearing first and by itself, but then, through the changing of the soul's affections, the touch is also changed "by accident" <note>indirectly</note>. Just as we see happen (he says) with orators through the faculty of Rhetoric, and with skilled musicians through the power of *harmony.* But on the contrary: Orators do not persuade with naked words, but by the weight of their sentiments and the importance of their reasons. If they proposed only naked, barbarous, and meaningless words, or words poorly suited to moving souls, or insane and ridiculous ones—as are the words of enchanters—they would never stir the mind. In music, the very sweetness of the harmony and the melodic modulation distracts the mind from the thought of pain, and by provoking joy, it soothes and tempers—

<summary>Delrio argues that words and incantations possess no inherent natural or supernatural power to heal the body, refuting both magical claims and the naturalistic theories of Pomponazzi. He asserts that physical healing requires physical change, which mere sound or writing cannot provide.</summary>
<keywords>incantations, natural magic, philosophy of language, demonology, Spondanus, Pomponazzi, medical theory, scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of magical practices and the power of words, specifically refuting the naturalistic explanations of Pomponazzi and the mystical claims of the Kabbalists.</meta>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION III.

...stirs the humors: to which, what is there similar in the horrific and shrill hissing and whispering of magi?

**Pomponazzi** <note>Pietro Pomponazzi (1462–1525), an Italian philosopher who sought natural explanations for "miracles."</note> also saw that he had labored in vain here; therefore he fled to the "sacred anchor" <note>A metaphor for a last resort or final refuge.</note>; claiming these things happen because of the vehement faith of the enchanter and the enchanted. In saying this, you betray your own cause, Pomponazzi, and you strip all power from the words themselves. If all that is the work of "credulity," which you call "faith," I ask: what kind of faith is that? You will not say, I think, that it is supernatural? For to call superstition and perfidy by that name would be utterly impious and treacherous. You will say then it is natural, or human. Bravo! You have **Ferrerius** <margin>k in the place cited.</margin> favoring your cause, who demands this confidence even from the bystanders. Ridiculous! As if the imagination, whether of the bystanders or of the enchanter himself, could work anything upon the imagination, body, or soul of the sick person <margin>l This was refuted in the previous chapter.</margin>. The credulity of the sick person himself, trusting that he can be helped by a thing which has no natural power to help, could be so vehement that it induces an alteration of the humors, from which health might follow—as **Galen** <note>The famous Greek physician of antiquity.</note> rightly says, if indeed he is the author of the book *On Incantations*. But in that case, health is to be ascribed to the words only as a certain occasion, not as the cause.

In a similar way, the following are repugnant to sincere and correct philosophy: the opinion of **Origen** <note>Early Christian theologian.</note> in books 1 and 5 *Against Celsus*, that names in one language produce effects which they cannot when translated into another; and the opinion of **Pico** <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, who integrated Kabbalah into Christian philosophy.</note>, that "barbarous" <note>Meaning foreign or non-Greek/Latin, often nonsensical sounding.</note> and meaningless names have more efficacy; also those things which **Gregorius Tolosanus** <margin>m Book 19 of the *Syntaxis*, chapter 8.</margin> handed down, when he adapts things said of actors, orators, and poets to enchanters—namely, that their words are only then ratified and effective when they utter them with an intent and firm mind. For those [enchanters] do not work by persuading and exciting the affections as the others do.

<margin>D</margin> See now the frivolous and impious defenses of Magic, and the vain foundations upon which it builds; which have nevertheless wonderfully pleased the lovers of **Kabbalistic** insanity. They assert, first, that there is a certain connection of sympathy between letters and syllables and the celestial bodies and minds. Yet they do not prove this by any reason, nor even by tradition; nor could it be proved, except by the testimony of those to whom it was revealed by demons. But demons are most deceitful, never willing to speak the truth of their own accord, nor can they be compelled by magi to bring forth the truth—this I assume for now, intending to prove it below.

Secondly, they say those words are of a greater nature in their efficacy which have arisen from a worthier language, and were instituted by a holier authority, and which signify holier things: in which matter **Laurentius Ananias** <margin>n Book 3, *On the Nature of Demons*, folio 99.</margin> is found to have trifled wonderfully. The devil devised this so that he might more easily and willingly make his initiates abuse all the holiest things. But it is refuted, because neither the significance of a holy thing, nor the dignity of a language, nor the integrity of the man instituting it, can imprint any operative physical quality upon words. God also promised no grace or virtue to the formulas of Magi, nor does He bestow it. How could God be contrary to Himself? How could He approve of arts which He Himself forbade? Finally, the **Theological Faculty of Paris** long ago defined that asserting such things is blasphemous and erroneous in articles 9 and 10 *On Magic*.

Thirdly, they dream that proper names are certain "rays" of the things they signify, and that in the names themselves, as it were, a certain life lies hidden. What is worthy of **Anticyra** <note>A place famous in antiquity for hellebore, used to treat madness; he is calling them insane.</note> if not this? For whatever the **Platonists** may say, names are bestowed at the will of men. Names are neither corporeal nor spiritual substances, nor are they accidents inherent in a subject; therefore they cannot have life in themselves: they are merely added to or "clothed" upon the things they signify.

<summary>The author refutes the idea that magical words have inherent power, arguing against Pomponazzi's theory of "imagination" and Kabbalistic claims about the mystical properties of language and letters.</summary>
<keywords>Pomponazzi, imagination, incantations, Kabbalah, Origen, Pico della Mirandola, demons, linguistics, Paris Theology Faculty, superstition</keywords>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

and they name them, purely from the outside: for which reason rays should not be said to emanate from the things themselves. There are also many names that signify nothing; many that signify something other than the nature of the thing: indeed, even those which do signify the nature of the thing (such as those <margin>o Genesis 2:20</margin> thought to be the ones Adam gave to the creatures, o) could receive no influence from the natures of the things: because corporeal things can naturally effect nothing upon that which lacks a body, as is the case with names.

<margin>E</margin> <margin>p Psalm 147:4 <note>Original cites Psalm 146, following the Vulgate numbering.</note></margin> <margin>q Luke 10:20 <note>Original cites verse 19.</note></margin> They object to us that God himself bestowed names upon things. They prove this because it is written, *He calls them all by their names* p, and elsewhere q; *rejoice, because your names are written in heaven.*] I respond that God indeed knows the proper natures of all things, even of individual angels and stars (of which the prophet treats in that passage), and thus, when He pleases, He can call them by their proper names—that is, names agreeable to their nature: but those names are unknown to mortals, nor have they been revealed. Furthermore, the present tense is placed here for the future, *He shall call*; as is evident from the Hebrew word, which is in the future tense. Therefore, the first passage helps these hair-splitters <note>Latin: argutatores; those who use overly subtle or fallacious reasoning.</note> not at all: and the second even less. For to have the names of the disciples written in heaven is nothing other than to be recorded in the register of the friends of God, which elsewhere is called *being inscribed in the book of life*; whether you take this according to God's eternal predestination, which cannot be changed, or according to present righteousness, which is subject to change; certainly, impious men dare to transfer this with too little reverence to the given names by which the disciples were addressed by men. Why should they greatly rejoice over a naming they have in common with other men, and even with brutes and nettles: whose names were no less known to God, who knows all things?

From this, however, they infer that meaningful words signify primarily through celestial influence, and secondarily only through human imposition: and because in meaningful names both concur, while in non-meaningful ones only one does, they claim meaningful names have more power. This is first of all denied by others, who attribute more to non-meaningful names. Indeed, I should like to ask all of them: is it necessary for any influence of the celestial orbs to flow through transmitted qualities into a subject so remote, which is itself fit and adapted for receiving those influences? Secondly, are incorporeal things, such as names, a suitable subject for receiving corporeal qualities? Just as the former cannot be denied, so the latter cannot be affirmed. Therefore, that first celestial influence contributes nothing to the energy <note>term: energy (energia); here used in the Aristotelian sense of "active power" or "efficacy."</note>. As for human imposition, since it is a naked and merely arbitrary naming, what it could possibly influence, not even Argus could see, who they say was the most sharp-sighted.

<margin>F</margin> The fourth delirium is that the power of complex words (namely propositions and orations) is more effective. First, because truth (says the arch-magician <note>Likely referring to Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa.</note>) contained in the proposition itself is added here; the power of which truth is greatest when the formula or "song of power" contains a commemoration of the operation of the star or the deity <note>Latin: numinis; a divine power or spirit presiding over a place or thing.</note> which they pray to. O Satan, Satan! How perpetually similar you are to yourself! You proceed with this weapon to seek divine honors. Is this not sufficiently indicated by the examples your initiate seeks from the Hymns of Orpheus and the writings of Apuleius, which are full of idolatry: both because those authorities of the poets are filled with vanity, and because he ascribes the sense and intellect of the song to the stars: and because by this "deity" he understands evil spirits <note>term: cacodaemones; literally "bad demons," used here to distinguish from neutral or good spirits.</note>, which Magic distributes among the stars and the species of individual things. He also tries to prove it because he who utters an entire texture of speech intends his mind more toward the words than he who utters one or two words: wherefore a more vehement force of imagination concurs, and a more copious spirit passes into the enchanted thing. These things are vain, and not worth a trifle <note>Latin: titiuillitio; a word for something of no value, often associated with the demon Titivillus.</note>. For the imagination has no power outside the one imagining upon separate things;

<meta>catchword: any;</meta>

He specifically attacks the use of pagan hymns and the idea that human imagination can project power into external objects.</summary>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION III.

...any [power]; nor does any spirit go forth from the speaker to touch the enchanted object, except for the breath. However, the power of the breath is null regarding health; though it has some power toward disease and harm through contagion, as we have taught.

<margin>G</margin>
From these points it becomes manifest that the corollaries they infer from such "beautiful" axioms are also false. First, their assertion that those healing in this manner must breathe upon the sick person and, as it were, inhale the words into them. Second, that any incantation of this kind, if uttered or written outside the usual order or backwards, produces unusual effects. Third, that those things which the Magician wishes to obtain must be inscribed upon the object he uses as an instrument. For example, if he uses a tablet, or parchment, or the leaves of a herb, he must write upon the leaf, membrane, or tablet the effect for which he acts. Why should I believe this? Is it because words uttered by themselves are not sufficiently effective? Or because the demon is otherwise deaf and cannot hear them; or that he understands written things better than heard ones? Or is this industry needed to bolster a lapse of memory? None of these is the cause; rather, that crafty one <note>The Devil</note> loves to ensnare the wretched through a multiplication of vain observations, like various sections of a net, and thus through many sins. <margin><sup>a</sup> Book 1 of *On Enchantment*, ch. 5.</margin> I have read the examples that Leonardo Vairus collected, <sup>a</sup> but in all of these, a pact entered into with the demon was the cause of the effects. See below, Book 6, Summary Admonition V.

---

# QUESTION IV.

->Concerning amulets and charms worn around the neck.<-

<margin>A</margin>
The custom of the Roman gentiles is well known; they were accustomed to hang shameful objects from the necks of small boys—*Prefiscini* <note>Amulets intended to ward off the "evil eye" or fascination</note>, as they called them—lest they be enchanted (whence the name *Fascinus* <note>A phallic charm</note> is numbered among obscene things). They also wore other amulets of amber or other material against diseases. In the same way, the Greeks had their *periapta*, *periammata*, and *apotropaia* <note>Greek terms for charms, knots, and "turners-away" of evil</note>, which were, as they thought, averters of evils. So also the Jews thought their *phylacteries* <note>Small leather boxes containing Hebrew texts</note> profited them, and that they were guarded and preserved from evils through them, wrongly interpreting the divine precept<sup>a</sup>. <margin><sup>a</sup> Exodus 13:16. Deuteronomy 6:8. Matthew 23:5.</margin> <margin><sup>b</sup> Book 3 of *Demonolatry*, ch. 3.</margin> Not only the heretic Weyer, but also Remigius of Lorraine <sup>b</sup> (who appears Catholic from his writings), fastens this same error upon Catholics; hence they rashly condemn the use of all charms based on a poor understanding of the Church Fathers.

<margin>B</margin>
I say first: *Amulets of this kind, which hang from the neck, possess no natural power by reason of the words, characters, figures, or constellations: but only by reason of the material, if there is any natural power of antipathy or sympathy within it.* This is the conclusion of all Catholics, and even of the gentiles whom we cited in the preceding question. It is proven because the faculty of a <term>physical ligature</term> <note>A "binding" or medicinal charm tied to the body</note> must depend on the nature of the thing itself, which is only applicable by reason of the matter or subject. Therefore, if any effect is produced whose cause cannot be the natural power of the subject, that effect will be a prodigy, and the amulet superstitious—such as many collected by Costa Ben Lucas, a Portuguese Jew<sup>c</sup>. <margin><sup>c</sup> Book on *Ligatures*.</margin> To this we must refer the attention they pay to shape; such as the charms hung from the necks of Spanish boys made of jet, shaped like a hand in derision, with the thumb inserted between the forefingers. They call this a *Higa* <note>The "fig" gesture, used as a protective charm</note>.

*I say secondly: When hope is not placed in the words themselves, the manner of writing, the number of crosses, the figure, or similar things: it is pious and holy, for the sake of reverence, to carry the relics of Saints, wax images of the Lamb of God <note>Agnus Dei</note>, the Gospel of St. John, a Psalm of David, and similar testimonies of Holy Scripture hanging from the neck: but the effect which arises from this will be supernatural, to be ascribed to the beneficence of God.*

<summary>This page refutes the magical efficacy of spoken or written charms and discusses the history of amulets, distinguishing between superstitious pagan/Jewish practices and the pious use of Christian relics.</summary>
<keywords>amulets, charms, fascination, phylacteries, Agnus Dei, Higa, superstition, Leonardo Vairus, Remigius, Weyer, Costa Ben Lucas</keywords>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

[...should be observed. *However, to use other ligatures <note>original: "ligaturis"; physical charms or knots tied for healing or protection</note> which are neither commended by the practice of the Catholic Church nor by the art of upright physicians is entirely forbidden.*]

Regarding relics, the distinguished Doctor of Theology, Ciruelo of Daroca <note>Pedro Ciruelo (c. 1470–1548), a Spanish mathematician and theologian who wrote a famous treatise against superstition</note>, in his book *On Superstitions* (book 4, part 3), seems to have spoken without sufficient caution when, supported by weak reasons, he does not hesitate to disapprove of wearing sacred relics around the neck. He differs from the heretics, for he himself wishes the relics of the Saints to be honorably preserved and venerated in churches; but he does not want them to be worn hanging from the necks of private individuals, thus rashly rebuking the ancient custom of very holy men. Did not the Blessed Helena place one of the nails of Christ in the bridle of a horse and another in the helmet of Constantine? Did not the Blessed Anthony exultantly carry that palm-leaf garment of Saint Paul the Anchorite, and Anthony’s own worn-out cloak made of skins? Gregory of Nyssa says similar things regarding the dust of the Martyr Theodore, as does Gregory the Great regarding the chain of Blessed Peter. But one must not dwell on a well-known matter.

<margin>
d Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica 2. 2. question 96. article 4.
Maldonado on John, chapter 1.
</margin>
The first part of the conclusion is proved by the ancient and salutary custom of the Catholic Church, as well as by the opinion of Saint Thomas, Maldonado,<sup>d</sup> Navarro, Silvester, and others. The reason is that our ancestors always venerated the relics of the Saints in this way: those Lambs <note>original: "Agni"; likely referring to Agnus Dei wax discs</note> are blessed for this purpose every year by the Supreme Pontiff alone. Just as it is permitted to utter the testimonies of Holy Scripture with the mouth, it is also permitted to carry them written down around the neck. Bishop Leontius relates that Saint Simeon the Holy Fool wrote an amulet <note>original: "amuletum"</note> on a small tablet for a sorceress; when she wore it, she was no longer able to divine or make magical amulets (*Surius, July 1*). Nor did any of the Holy Fathers ever 
<margin>e Homily on Psalm 45.</margin>
disapprove of this. For Saint Basil<sup>e</sup> condemns "periammata" <note>original: "περιάμματα"; Greek for amulets or charms worn around the body</note> marked with characters (namely, magical ones). An uncertain author on Matthew, whose work exists among the works of Chrysostom<sup>f</sup>, 
<margin>f Homily 43 on Matthew.</margin>
inveighs against two wicked kinds of men: first, those who, by hanging such things from their necks, wished to appear holier themselves, like the Pharisees of old; second, those who out of the same pride bestowed bits of their own hair or 
<margin>g On Matthew, Homily 73.</margin>
clothing for others to hang from their necks. Saint Chrysostom<sup>g</sup> himself rebukes those simple women, either because they did this with a Pharisaic intention rather than a right one, or because, through an error of opinion, they expected miraculous help not from the grace of God, but from the very material or form of the writing—as do those today who say the Gospel must be written on virgin parchment with red letters or lines, and so forth. Truly, Chrysostom rebukes it with the same mind as Saint 
<margin>h On the said chapter 23 of Matthew.</margin>
Jerome,<sup>h</sup> who indicates clearly enough that he rebukes that Pharisaic superstition which expects protection from the little images <note>original: "pictatiolis"</note> themselves, and not from above, from the Keeper of Israel, the Lord of Hosts. Likewise, he rebukes them because while they wore these things outwardly, they neglected the inward worship and observance which is most important. Finally, in the life of Saint Anthony, we read that he dispelled a certain infestation of demons when he had written certain prayers on a small piece of parchment and ordered them to be fixed in his chamber before an image of the Virgin Mother of God (*Surius, volume 3, folio 43*).

The second part of the conclusion is proved by the canons, which thunder anathema against those who use phylacteries (that is, "preservatives"); and if clerics should make them, the canons command that they be cast out of the churches. *Canon 1 & 4, question 5, case 26.* How these canons are to be understood, and what kind of phylacteries they speak of, must be sought from another canon, namely the last of the same question, where among other things Blessed Isidore writes in this manner: *To these belong the ligatures of accursed remedies which the art of physicians does not commend, whether in incantations, or in the hanging and binding of characters; in all of which is the art of demons, arising from a certain pestilential society of men and 
<margin>i Tractate 7 on John.</margin>
evil angels.*] Saint Augustine<sup>i</sup> confirms this with these words: *Malignant spirits fashion certain shadows of honor for themselves, so that they may thus deceive those who follow Christ. To such an extent, my brothers, that those very ones who seduce through ligatures, through incantations, through the machinations of the enemy, mix the name of Christ into their incantations, because they can no longer seduce Christians [otherwise].*

<summary>This page defends the pious wearing of holy relics and scriptural texts against critics like Ciruelo, distinguishing these "sacred amulets" from forbidden magical charms (phylacteries) and superstitious ligatures condemned by the Church Fathers.</summary>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER IV. QUESTION III.

[...Christians, so that they give poison while adding a bit of honey.] <note>original: "ut dent venenum addant mellis aliquantum." A metaphor for how demonic magic is disguised with holy words.</note> **Chrysostom** in his 25th homily to the People of Antioch: *What indeed would someone say of those who use incantations and ligatures <note>term: ligatures (ligaturæ). Physical charms or knots tied around the body, believed to have magical healing or protective powers.</note>, and of those who bind golden coins of Alexander the Macedonian to their heads or feet? Tell me, is this our hope, that after the cross and death of the Lord, we should have hope of salvation through the image of a pagan king?* etc. Such are those wicked formulas of prayers which some, through falsehood, ascribe to **Blessed Leo** and other saints against wounds and the dangers of fire, water, and other things; and (alas!) many today wear them constantly. The Devil always eventually deceives these people; when they are in the worst state of mortal sin, he allows them to be killed, deserting those he had previously defended a few times. I could name someone well known to me to whom this happened. The French know that in previous years, when the swarms of German heretics broke into France with **Baron Dohna**, and were vigorously defeated by the **Duke of Guise**, scraps of writing of this kind were found hanging from the necks of almost all the slain by those who despoiled the corpses. **Lucian** mocks this kind of superstition <margin>^k in the Philosophical Dialogue</margin>, and **Spartianus** testifies that **Antoninus Caracalla** decided to restrain it with punishments. And **Tatian the Assyrian** <margin>^l Oration against the Greeks</margin> refers all these things to the deceits of demons and pacts entered into with them through Magi. To this I also refer what **Balsamon** reports in his Commentary on the 6th Synod in Trullo regarding "bear-leaders": *Those who are called bear-keepers are said to lead bears; they indeed hang dyed cloths on the head and the whole body of the animal, and shearing hairs from it, they give them along with the dyes as if they were remedies and amulets, as if they could protect against diseases and the evil eye.* <note>original: "fascinantes oculos."</note> To this also belong all formulas in which unknown names of Angels are mixed—that is, others besides **Michael, Gabriel, and Raphael**. This will perhaps seem strange to someone, because **Trithemius** and the Rabbis also devise other names for those they say are the movers of the celestial spheres, and likewise that they decree fates and administer fatal events, and that men learn certain marvelous things from them; and similar impieties with which that book **Arbatel** <note>original: ארבעתאל. A famous 16th-century grimoire of "spiritual magic."</note> teems. But I rely on the authority and sanction of the **Roman Council**, which was assembled at Rome under **Pope Zachary** to condemn the errors of **Aldebert** and **Clement**, heretics disturbing Germany. There, among other things, a prayer of Aldebert was read in which these words were contained:

> [I supplicate you, Angel Uriel, Angel Raguel, Angel Tubuel, Angel Michael, Angel Adimis, Angel Tubuas, Angel Sabaoth, Angel Simihel.]

When these were read, **Pope Zachary** asked the Bishops to bring forward their opinion on them; they pronounced that Aldebert must be condemned as a heretic. The cause is indeed added in the Decree of the council itself in these words: *For the eight names of Angels which Aldebert invoked in his prayer are not the names of Angels (except for Michael), but rather the names of demons whom he invoked to provide him aid. We, however, as we are taught by your holy Apostleship and as divine authority hands down, acknowledge no more than the names of three Angels: that is, Michael, Gabriel, and Raphael. Pope Zachary said: It has been most excellently provided by your holiness that all his writings be burned with fire; but it is appropriate that they be preserved in our archives for his rejection and for perpetual confusion. Now indeed, because all things are finished, it has been deliberated according to your opinion, of whom mention was made above. The entire council said: Aldebert, whose acts and wicked commentaries have been read to us; and who judged himself to be called an Apostle; and who gave his hair and fingernails to the people as relics of holiness; and who under the pretext of Angels invokes demons to his aid: let him be alienated from every priestly office, etc.*] <margin>^m see in Surius, volume 3, month of June, in the life of St. Boniface, page 494.</margin> Therefore, according to the opinion of this council, all *periammata* <note>term: periammata. Greek term for amulets or charms worn around the neck.</note>, *periapta* of ligatures, and similar things are to be condemned where names other than the three Angels named by Holy Scripture are read. There is named indeed

And someone might say that only the decision of Pope Zachary (namely the declaration of heresy and the burning of the writings) was approved, but that he did not necessarily look to the reasoning provided by those Fathers; for although the decision of councils is a matter of faith, the reasons brought forward for the decision are not, as is well known.

### CHAPTER V.

->The goldsmith's art, which they call Alchemy: to which species of magic should it be referred?<-

This question cannot be sufficiently answered unless it is first established how far its powers extend. Therefore, four things must be discussed. First, does this art achieve its end? Second, should it be counted among the liberal arts? Third, to which species of Magic does it belong? Fourth, is it licit or illicit?

---

### QUESTION I.

->Is true gold produced by this art?<-

This question encompasses two things: what can be done by the aid of nature, and what is actually done? The reasoning for these is not the same. Before we decide the question, certain things regarding the name and origin must be set forth.

---

### SECTION I.

->What is Alchemy and when was it invented?<-

<margin>
<sup>a</sup> Nicolaus [Rigaltius] commentary on Manilius.
</margin>
Learned men maintain the name is Arabic; and that this is indicated by the *Al* prefixed to the name, as in *Almanac*, and *Almagest*, and similar words.<sup>a</sup> It would therefore be a "mixed-barbarian" word, from the Arabic *Al*, and the Greek <term>cheo</term> or <term>cheuo</term> <note>original: χύω / χύνω</note>, which is the same as *to pour* or *to smelt*. Others think it is a purely Greek word, among whom is Johannes Chrysippus Fanianus, 
<margin>
<sup>b</sup> Fanianus in the Dialogue on Chrysopeia [Gold-making].
</margin>
who derives it <term>from "chymeia" or "hals"</term> <note>original: παρὰ τὴν χυμείαν ἢ ἅλα</note> from salt and smelting.<sup>b</sup> But the Greek writers oppose this, as they mostly write it <term>chemeia</term> <note>original: χημείαν</note>;<sup>c</sup> not <term>chymeia</term>. But what does <term>chemeia</term> 
<margin>
<sup>c</sup> Cedrenus in the Annals; Suidas, soon to be cited.
</margin>

<margin>
<sup>d</sup> Book on Osiris and Isis.
</margin>
namely because the Egyptians, the first of mortals, received it from Mizraim the son of Ham <note>The biblical progenitor of Egypt</note>, the Arabs learned it from the Egyptians and added their article *Al*, and the Europeans received both the name and the art from the Arabs, as they did almost all of medicine and Greek philosophy. Those authors are too generous toward the Arabs. For the name was known to the Romans before any philosophers or learned men were known among the Arabs, and at a time when Arabia was still Christian. You may find it in Julius Firmicus, a Latin<sup>e</sup> writer who lived in the time of Constantine the Great. Among the Egyptians, this art seems to have been in use even in the time of Diocletian, if we believe Suidas<sup>f</sup>; and perhaps the Romans received it from them. # BOOK I. CHAPTER V. QUESTION I.

<margin>g Psalm 78:51</margin>
<margin>h Psalm 105:23</margin>
...said to be from Ham, for so we read in the Psalms; *In the tabernacles of Ham*, g. meaning the Egyptians; and afterwards h; *the land of Ham*, Egypt itself is called. This name, therefore, the priests preserved as a testimony of their antiquity; though in common speech Egypt was called Mizraim: whence even today the Turks and Arabs still call it *Masra*. Therefore, if the Arabs had wished to name this art after Egypt and add their prefix *Al*, they would have called it *Almasram*. What then? I suspect, as with many Arabic words, that it is actually Hebrew, and solidly so. Nor does it signify anything other than the art of casting or melting. For to the Hebrews, *Alichim* i, signifies paths or flows, from *Alich*, which means to make to walk or to flow k. From this comes the art of melting or casting metals: by dropping a small letter from *Alichim*, it becomes *Alchemy* <note>original: "Alchimia"</note> by a very easy formation.
<margin>הליכים i Job 29:6</margin>
<margin>הלך k Job 24:10</margin>

**B** For what else is Alchemy but pyrotechnics, or the art of fire, for the resolving and purifying of metals? Just as it is a certain species of *Chymia* <note>Chemistry/Alchemy in a broad sense</note>: so also gold-making, or <term>Chrysopoeia</term> <note>from Greek: the making of gold</note>, is a certain species of Alchemy, which is entirely occupied in extracting or separating, and in gathering or coagulating gold from other metals: whence some not incorrectly call it the <term>Spagyric</term> art, <term>from the Greek words 'to draw out' and 'to gather'</term> <note>original: "παρὰ τὸ σπᾶν καὶ ἀγείρειν"</note>, from separating and gathering. To this art someone referred that verse of Manilius l,
<margin>l Book 4 of the Astronomica</margin>

->*And the material is doubled by the hand with certain art:*<-

And because it was said concerning Alchemical matters, he thought—by error—that it should be removed as spurious (which is a piece of critical audacity), judging against the faith of the ancient manuscripts. Both were wrong. For the verse is indeed by Manilius, and is to be understood not as referring to gold-makers, but to gold-beaters, who by hammering gold into the thinnest plates or leaves, draw it out and dilate it, and thus "double" the surface so dilated. In Alchemy, they certainly do not double the material by hand so much as by fire.

I have not determined to treat the whole of Chemistry; which art, insofar as it assists medicine, I certainly praise and venerate; as a most excellent offspring of physiology; the inventor of potable gold <note>liquid gold used as a medicine</note>, a thing no less useful for healing than for nourishing, and as far as possible, for prolonging life; for it draws out the most subtle spirits from metals, gems, and plants; the more subtle they are, the purer; and the purer, the more effective the remedies they provide; it purifies metals, separates them, perfects them, melts stones, draws out water, and quickens fire; and in that quickened and almost perennial fire, it gives a specimen of its art and efficacy in extending human life. To me, indeed, there is no doubt that the liquid of Maximus Olibius, by the benefit of which a lamp was kept burning for many centuries, was Chemical; concerning which Bernardus Scardeonus m writes that in Padua some years ago, an earthenware urn was dug up inscribed with these verses:
<margin>m in the History of Padua</margin>

> *Thieves, touch not this gift sacred to Pluto,*
> *That which lies hidden here is unknown to the world.*
> *For Maximus Olibius enclosed the elements,*
> *Digested with heavy labor, under this modest vessel.*
> *Let abundance be present as a guardian with her fertile horn,*
> *Lest the price of such a great liquid should perish.*

As a pagan man, he dedicated this specimen of his art to Pluto, the God of Riches, crediting him for the wealth. Inside that larger urn, smaller items were found; in this, two skillfully worked flasks, one of silver, the other of gold: both full of a certain very pure liquid, by which the lamp was thought to have been preserved burning for many centuries.

I have decided to speak only of <term>Chrysopoeia</term>; which either multiplies gold from a few sprinkled filings of gold powder, or produces true gold from non-gold: for that which produces the same amount or less gold from melted gold, but purifies—

## [Book 2, Page 50]

<margin><unclear>id. lib. 2. fol. ...</unclear></margin>
<margin>D</margin>
...more purified; it does not deserve the name of the gold-making art. Therefore, some people make the origin of <term>Chrysopoeia</term> <note>The art of making gold; from the Greek 'chrysos' (gold) and 'poiein' (to make).</note> very ancient for us, thrusting forward a certain notable book under the illustrious title of Adam. Others peddle books by Moses, and his sister Mary <note>Often identified in alchemical tradition as 'Mary the Prophetess' or 'Maria Prophetissima'.</note>, and Solomon, and Hermes Trismegistus, and Aristotle, and certain Pythagoreans. I would consider all these to be regarded as impostures and the dreams of idle men. For what: because the first principles of the sciences came from Adam, did he therefore also write about this art—he, of whom it is not established that he wrote about anything? Or, because Moses excelled in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, did he therefore, and his sister (I am surprised Aaron was not added, and Bezalel), apply their minds to cultivating alchemy? Or, because the Cabbalists talk nonsense about Ham stealing books on natural Magic from his father Noah in the Ark, which he then gave to his son Mizraim; were there also chemical books among those? But we know that Ham was the propagator not of natural philosophy, but of Demonic Magic. <margin>See Book 1, Chapter 3.</margin> Not a few books of Hermes Trismegistus survive; I ask you, what even slight color of this art is in them? I suppose those who have distorted them with Phaethon-like daring <note>A reference to Phaethon's disastrous attempt to drive the chariot of the Sun; implying reckless overreaching.</note> will dig it out. Those things which the Holy Scriptures recorded concerning the Tower of Babel and the Promised Land, and what Solomon wrote in *Ecclesiastes* concerning the harlot, depicting her character while inspired by the Holy Spirit—these people have twisted toward the mysteries of Chemistry. They have dared to adjudge to Solomon's alchemy those riches which the History of Kings expressly attributes to economic prudence and the navigation to Ophir. Regarding these people (to deal with them most mildly), I shall say nothing else than what we usually say to the foolish: that a sound mind is to be wished for them. Aimar Ranconet and Cardano err with far less danger, who transferred that prophecy of the Sibyl:

->I have nine letters. I am four syllables. Consider me. etc.<-
<note>original Greek: ἔννεα γράμματ' ἐχώ . τετρασύλλαβός εἰμι . νοέιμε</note>

to Arsenic, the material of the "admirable stone" <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note>; as if there were no other word of four syllables and nine letters. Likewise, those who contend that this is the cup of Pandora mentioned by Hesiod: but it must be admitted that there are more Epimetheuses <note>The "after-thinker" who acted impulsively.</note> than Prometheuses <note>The "fore-thinker" who acted with wisdom.</note> in this flock, with only hope remaining.
<margin>Sisyphus, as if 'wise-god'. <note>A pun on the Greek 'oiosophos'.</note></margin> No less delightful are those who extol the Sisyphian stone, and imagine that he earned the name because of the discovery of this stone, as if he were divinely wise; and that because of his indiscriminate sharing of the benefit with the common people, he was thrust down to the underworld and condemned to the lifting of a stone that always rolls back. These are men of excessive greed. Others are more liberal, who, along with Suidas, turn the Golden Fleece of the Colchians into this. For they say that the "golden-fleeced skin" of Jason was actually books written on ram skins containing the doctrine of the alchemical art of gold-making. Others add that this was that "golden thigh" <note>original Greek: χρυσᾶν μηρὸν</note>, or (as others correct it) the "golden stream" <note>original Greek: χρυσᾶν ῥοῦν</note>, shown by Pythagoras at the Olympic games, which Origen nevertheless called ivory in *Book 6 against Celsus*. They claim this is that "wonderful and especially sacred work of the earth" in Hippocrates, from which living things, plants, food, medicines, fortune, and Plutus <note>The god of wealth.</note> himself flow forth. <margin>Hippocrates in the letter concerning Crateuas.</margin> From this it appears how ancient an origin these people fetch for this art. <margin>E</margin> Indeed, I think it is ancient: but I find no certain testimonies before the age of Caligula. For what has been said is mere conjecture; nor is it more certain what some prove from the 4th Book of Esdras, Chapter 8, Verse 2: (*"But how will you ask the earth, and it shall tell you: that it will give much more earth from which pottery is made, but little dust from which gold is made."*) For this can clearly be referred not to the art of smelting, but to the natural production of metal from the dust of the earth. If, however, anyone should contend that it pertains to the transmuting matter of gold, let him know that he relies on no infallible testimony, because that book is apocryphal; nor...

# BOOK I. CHAPTER V. QUESTION I

<margin>From Book 33 of the *Natural History*, chapter 4.</margin>
...nor can the antiquity of the art be proven in a clear and open place. Pliny teaches that it began under Caligula, <gloss>because</gloss> he sought a method of smelting gold from orpiment <note>original: *auripigmento*; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral once thought to contain gold</note>, but the attempt soon ceased. This mineral is dug up in Syria; it is the color of gold, brittle, and resembles mirror-stone <note>mica or selenite</note>. Caligula, who was no less greedy for gold than for blood, ordered a great quantity of it to be melted down. Indeed, excellent gold did flow from it, but of such little weight that he felt a loss <note>i.e., the cost of processing exceeded the value of the gold recovered</note>; nor was it attempted by anyone thereafter. Following this, there was a long silence among writers. They remained silent until the time of Diocletian. For Suidas is our authority that, when the Egyptians were stirring up a revolution during his reign, this fire was smothered at its very beginning by the slaughter of many illustrious men. At that time, the ancient writings concerning the smelting of silver and gold were sought out there, and all were committed to the flames by order of the tyrant, lest the Egyptians, enriched by that art and relying on their wealth, should rebel against the Romans in 
<margin>Suidas, under the entry for "Diocletian":</margin>
the future. Some years later lived Zosimus the Greek writer, whose books on the *Sacred Art* and *Chrysopoeia* <note>The art of making gold</note> are preserved in manuscript in the Library of the Most Christian King at Fontainebleau; where also the book of Blemmidas on the same *Chrysopoeia* is said to exist. This is that Nicephorus Blemmidas who, under the Emperor Ducas, published many excellent monuments of philosophy. I know of no Greek older than Zosimus; he precedes all the Arabs who are circulated in this age: the fictitious Morienus (whom foolish people imagine to be a Roman hermit writing in Arabic) and the true Geber, or Jabir the Arab—who was not the inventor of the art, as Fallopius and Erastus believe, but its chief cultivator and embellisher. This is what I had to say regarding its origin.

---

## SECTION II.
### On the efficacy of the alchemical art in making gold.

It is marvelous how the rope of contention is pulled in opposite directions here. The practitioners of alchemy defend the affirmative so strongly that they contend they can cherish and draw forth a more perfect gold from their furnaces than nature is accustomed to; on the other hand, the haters of the Chemists <note>Early term for alchemists</note> consider all such things to be deceptions, mockeries, and the mere empty boasts of braggarts. For my part, I think that between these extremes, as is often the case, the middle path is trodden most safely. Let us see briefly what can be argued for each side.

I find three opinions. The *first* is that true gold cannot be produced in this way. The *second* is that gold can indeed be produced that is, as it were, true, but which nevertheless may not be sold for the same price as true gold, nor may it be administered in medicines. The *third* is that a more perfect and pure gold can be made this way than that which is brought forth in the bowels of the earth. The first opinion is held by Tostado in his commentary on *Exodus, chapter 7, question 10*; by Giles of Rome in *Quodlibet 3, question 8* (who also cites Avicenna on his behalf, but wrongly, for Avicenna truly held the opposite view and wrote expressly about this art); it was also denied by Averroes in *Book 1 on Generation*; and by Pietro d'Abano in *Difference 209*. But even the Chemist Paracelsus himself denied it in his *Book on Philosophy*.

<margin>
a. 22. q. 77. art. 2. reply 1.
b. On the 2nd book of Sentences, dist. 7, q. 3, art. 1.
c. On the same article 2.
</margin>
The second opinion (as I believe) was truly that of St. Thomas [Aquinas]. For although, in places where he did not wish to decide the question, he spoke only hypothetically; <sup>a</sup> nevertheless, where he spoke absolutely and decisively, he openly and clearly held this view: that gold can be produced as far as external qualities are concerned, but that it does not reach the intrinsic perfection of gold <sup>b</sup>; this also pleased Cajetan <sup>c</sup> and the rest of the Thomists to be cited later.

The third opinion is that of all the Chemists, and furthermore of Timon in *Book 3 of the Meteorology, last question*; of Antonio Mirandola in *On Singular Certainty, book 10*; and of Marcello Palingenio...

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC
## Page 52

<margin>See Book 1 of *Singularities*.</margin>
The views of Cardano, Lacinius, and others are accurately defended by Andreas Libavius in his *Syntax*, Book 1 of *Singularities*. <note>Andreas Libavius (1555–1616) was a German physician and chemist who sought to distinguish chemistry from the more mystical aspects of alchemy.</note>

<margin>
d. Disputations on Magic.
e. On the *Secunda Secundae*, disputation 6, question 13, point 2.
</margin>
To these we could add a fourth opinion, that of the Pyrrhonians and the Academic Skeptics <note>Ancient schools of philosophy that advocated for the suspension of judgment.</note>; those who confess that the matter is not sufficiently clear to them. I believe this is actually the more common view among people of this age. Among our own writers, Benedictus Pererius and Gregory de Valencia defend it; for they doubt the goodness and perfection of alchemical gold. They suspect that true and perfect gold has never been produced by anyone; and Valencia also indicates that by human study and art, it either cannot be done, or can scarcely be done. What shall we say? "Plato is a friend, Socrates is a friend, but truth is a greater friend." <note>original: "amicus Plato, amicus Socrates, sed magis amica veritas." A famous maxim suggesting that objective truth must take precedence over the authority of great teachers.</note>

<margin>A</margin>
### Axiom 1.
*Those who agree that true gold cannot be made through alchemy do not prove their case with any suitable arguments.* To prove this conclusion, it is sufficient to transfer the burden of proof to the opponent. Because this is a negative conclusion, the burden lies on the one asserting that it is impossible to show why it is impossible; this is because he contends he can prove it with suitable arguments, which we deny. Nevertheless, the conclusion can be proven thus: such a metamorphosis is not repugnant to nature, neither on the part of the matter or subject, nor on the part of the method. Not on the part of the matter: for matter is apt to be altered and corrupted, and also apt to be led toward a more excellent form; it is capable of having a new form introduced. Not on the part of the method: because the method is through the natural decoction <note>A process of heating or boiling to extract essence.</note> of fire, which very closely imitates the natural decoction of natural heat. What, then, is missing? If any of these points are lacking, let it be shown.

They attempt to show it with the following arguments, which we shall dilute one by one by applying a solution. First, they argue from the uncertainty of the art itself and the disagreement of the practitioners; because the chemists <note>The text uses "chymici," referring to alchemists/early chemists.</note> agree among themselves neither in their way of speaking, nor in their way of operating, nor regarding the matter or the name of their "stone." They agree that this transformation is to be attributed to the benefit of the stone; but they do not agree on what name to call this stone. It would be a long task to heap up all the names from Mercurulinus, Trevisan, Fanianus, Vallensis, the *Rosary*, and others, which have plainly different meanings. From a few, the rest can be judged. They call it <term>gold-seed</term> <note>original: "χρυσόσπερμον" (chrysospermon)</note>, living water, water of life, dry water, the wood of life, human blood, virgin’s milk, the Mercury of the philosophers, the dragon, the crow, laton, elixir, the medicine for all diseases, that which he who drinks does not die, and similar names—partly ridiculous, partly conflicting, and partly irreligious. They devise the most diverse methods of operating and fight among themselves over them; Tauladanus contends with Brachescho, Trevisan attacks Villanova, others attack others, and they call each other fools and ignoramuses. But perhaps they at least agree on the material of the stone? Not at all. There are those who demand iron dross, some who demand salt, alum, magnesia, calamine, vitriol, or arsenic; there are those who thrust forward toads, hair, eggshells, menstrual flow, or human blood. I respond that the same thing is signified by these many different names; but the writers used them for the sake of hiding the science, which they did not wish to be understood by everyone. Surely the Egyptians were permitted to use their symbols, poets were permitted to hide the principles of wisdom through metaphors and fables, and Aristotle was permitted to envelop himself in the obscurity of his sayings, as the cuttlefish does with ink; the Sacred Scriptures themselves speak in parables. Why do you find fault if these men also played with different names? If anything was done less aptly, successfully, or piously, forgive it. Look at the substance, disregard the words. The ineptitude or infancy of the teacher could never extinguish the truth of the matter or the efficacy of the art. Furthermore, what wonder is it that this art also has its clashes of dissenters, which no part of philosophy, nor medicine, nor jurisprudence, nor Scholastic Theology is known to lack? That disagreement regarding the material is either one of words and names,

# BOOK I. CHAPTER V. QUESTION I.

...or indeed it was so in the past, when the art was less cultivated and more unknown; the experts agree on sulfur and mercury <note>original: "sulphur & hydrargirum"</note>.

**B** They later prove that the nature of metals is unknown to them [the alchemists]. First, because they believe that metals live and are nourished, as Cardano <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), an Italian polymath who argued metals were alive.</note> holds; or that they possess a soul of a higher order, though a very hidden one, as Francesco Giorgio <note>Francesco Giorgio (1460–1540), a Franciscan friar and Hermetic philosopher.</note> suggests in *The Harmony of the World*. Second, because they think stones are the "refuse of the stars" (as Paracelsus <note>The famous Swiss physician and alchemist who founded iatrochemistry.</note> claims), while metals are the fat and marrow of stones; and therefore, that metals are the more excellent excrements of the heavens. Third, because they maintain there is only one perfect species of metal, namely gold; the others being merely raw and incomplete gold—about which more will be said shortly. Fourth, they do not have a clear understanding of the efficient cause of their gold. They attribute the efficiency to color alone, yet the substance of gold cannot be perfected by color alone. For it is necessary that the sun first cook and transform the substance of water by its heat; then, earthly cold and dryness cause the water to grow together. Fifth, they differ greatly regarding the matter of gold. Gilgil <note>Likely a reference to an Arabic alchemical source.</note> wants it to be ash born from the earth and mixed with water; Barceschus <note>Another obscure alchemical authority.</note> says vitriol; most say sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argentum uiuum"</note>; and there is no lack of those who add arsenic. In investigating this matter, they seem like the Umbri <note>An ancient Italian people, here used proverbially for those following a scent blindly.</note>, following the lead of a keen nose; certain indeed to be led to the "deer's nursery" <note>A metaphor for a hidden or difficult-to-find place.</note>, but deceptive in discerning the essence of things.

Furthermore, how can mixed things be resolved back into that mixture from which they were most recently generated? Must they not first be resolved into simple bodies? Therefore, if—as is their fundamental principle—metals are resolved into sulfur and mercury, then surely these two cannot be the proper [original] matter of metals. This argument involves many questions which, if I wished to discuss them precisely, would be the work not of one section, but of an entire book. It might be better to settle it in a single word: even if we grant them all these points, nothing else follows except that the alchemists who hold such opinions are not true philosophers, nor do they understand the causes of their operations, nor do they work scientifically, but are merely bare "pyrotechnic" statue-makers and mechanics, rather than scholars <note>original: "philologos"</note>. However, it in no way follows—which was the point to be proved—that gold cannot be made by them. For a blacksmith does not fail to make a sword simply because he does not know the nature of iron, or what rotation and vibration of the sword is suited for battle. Nor does a chemist draw out a less perfect rose water if he employs the other requirements of the art, even if he is ignorant of the essence of the rose, the power of fire, and the principles of distillation. What then? If someone who is ignorant of the nature of metal uses the required separation and freezing, and skillfully employs all other things; will the "cooker of fires" <note>A derogatory or technical term for a common smelter.</note> achieve less toward its blowing and composition than toward another's, adding nothing more to the operation and effect beyond the knowledge of causes? He who knows the difference between one thing and another is already a participant in the intelligence of fire. But suppose someone who knows the nature of metals perfectly: this argument will certainly not hinder him regarding the production of gold. Therefore, it can be produced by someone. These points should suffice; however, I will say what I think about each of them.

<margin>
f Book 1, *Natural and Moral Magic*
g Pliny, Book 36, chapters 18 & 19.
Leon Battista Alberti, Book 2, *On the Art of Building*, ch. 9.
h Scaliger, *Exercise 1*. Falloppio, Book 1, *On Metals*.
</margin>

That metals live and are nourished, as if having a vegetative soul: this is not entirely absurd, but I confess what Aristotle judged is truer <note>margin: f</note>, that neither stones nor fossils of this kind live or are nourished. They grow, however, by the addition of matter, and decrease by its subtraction. When Pliny <note>margin: g</note> and others say they are conceived, give birth, live, grow old, and die, they are speaking only metaphorically. But Scaliger and Falloppio <note>margin: h; Gabriele Falloppio (1523–1562), the famous anatomist who also wrote on minerals.</note> say more on this. I am willing to bargain generously with them. Let it be that metals do not live: what then? Surely the transformation of an inanimate thing into another inanimate thing will be easier than that of a living thing into another living thing. I do not dwell on those expressions of the Paracelsians, which are certainly not quite right or convenient, but improper and figurative. They speak unclearly; they think wrongly. I do not defend them; I speak of the reality, not the words. This one thing I think is most true, and cannot be denied: that there is more of a fatty moisture, and if you will, more oily substance...

<summary>The author critiques alchemical theories on the biological nature of metals while arguing that a lack of theoretical knowledge does not necessarily prevent the practical success of creating gold. He compares the alchemist to a blacksmith or a distiller who can produce results without understanding the underlying metaphysics.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, transmutation of metals, sulfur-mercury theory, Paracelsus, Aristotle, Girolamo Cardano, metallurgy, efficient cause, vegetative soul of minerals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and chemical investigation into the nature of metals, specifically focusing on the "efficient cause" of gold and the shared origins of all metallic substances. It follows the previous discussion on the properties of minerals.</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

There is more moisture in metals than in stones and gems. For this reason, metals are ductile and malleable, properties denied to stones because of their parched and dry nature. Whether there is only one species of metal, we shall see shortly. Concerning the efficient cause of gold, it must be considered. I believe far more is required for a mixed body to first exist and coagulate from the earth and—so to speak—from almost bare elements, than is required for another mixture to be fashioned from a mixture already coagulated and existing, differing only in its degree of perfection. The substance of gold, made from the dust of the earth and the liquid of water in the veins of caverns, cannot be perfected by heat alone; coldness and dryness are also required. However, once the metal has already coalesced into a metallic substance, fire alone will then be able to supply what is necessary to perfect it and change it into the qualities of another metal. This is because those qualities are accidental <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidental" means a property that can change without changing the essential nature of the thing.</note> and do not exceed the power of fiery activity.

This can be declared by an example. For natural realgar <note>original: "sandaracha"; a red arsenic sulfide mineral</note> cannot be produced without that cold; but once lead has been produced in the bowels of the earth,

<margin>
i Galen, book 9 on the *Properties of Simple Medicines*. Pliny, book 34 at the end. Vitruvius, book 7.
k Pliny, book 35, ch. 6. Aetius, book 2. Amatus on Dioscorides, book 5, narration 63.
l See Pliny, book 33, ch. 4 and Vannoccio [Biringuccio], book 1 of *Pyrotechnics*, ch. 8.
m book 26, ch. 27.
n Aristotle, book 4 of *Meteorology*. Vannoccio, said book 1, ch. 7. Albertus Magnus, book 2 on *Stones*, tract 3.
o See Girolamo Maggi, book 2 of *Miscellanies*, ch. 8.
</margin>

if you mix vinegar with it, you will make white lead <note>original: "cerussam"</note><sup>i</sup>. However, for realgar to come forth from white lead, there is no longer a need for cooling; rather, scorching alone suffices so that artificial realgar or "Syrian red" <note>original: "sandix Syricum"</note> is born<sup>k</sup>. Brass <note>original: "Orihalcum"</note> is also made without cold from copper, glass powder, and the tincture of a calamine stone; it requires only boiling and straining through fire<sup>l</sup>, even though the mined and natural kind (if such a thing exists) would also demand that cold. What of the fact that Pliny<sup>m</sup> also judged that stones are melted into copper and iron is produced by fire alone? What of the fact that by boiling alone, iron becomes what the Greeks call *stomoma*<sup>n</sup> <note>original: "στόμωμα"; tempered steel</note> or what the Hebrews call *Chalamisch* <note>original: "Chalamisch"; likely referring to flint or hard stone/steel</note>, which we today call *chalybs* or steel <note>original: "aciarium"</note>? The ancient Latins called it the "kernel of iron" because they lacked a specific name for it, for to them *chalybs* was the same as iron in general<sup>o</sup>. In these matters, there is no need for any other cold than when the fire has finished everything, so that the material may then be allowed to return to its natural coldness, or be cooled and hardened more quickly by being thrown into water.

Furthermore, as for the disagreement they raise concerning the matter of gold, it makes a noise but does not blunt the argument. Tell me, what do they want it to be? Whatever they say, the chemical philosophers will contend that all other metals also consist of this, if the transition to gold is to be easy. But the more learned agree on one opinion, which I also follow, and thus I think Aristotle can be reconciled with these authors. We are not dealing now with the most remote matter, which is "prime matter" <term>materia prima: the fundamental, formless substance of the universe</term>, which no one doubts is the same for all; but we are dealing with "secondary matter," which is already imbued with certain forms. This, again, is divided into that which is somewhat remote and that which is more proximate. For here there is no immediate transition from the last to the first.

Let us take gold, for example. This gold will have a more remote matter: on one hand, a certain moist exhalation (which is called "liquid matter" by some, or "intrinsically oily water," and by others "incorporated aqueous oiliness" or "liquid moisture"); on the other hand, it will have a thick and viscous portion of earth. Thus, that fatty and airy water, mixed with this earth, is embodied into the first and (if I may call it so) elementary matter of gold. This matter is not yet the specific matter of gold, but is the common matter of all metals, and even of stones. For if it has more dryness and lacks moisture, it becomes a stone; if it has more moist fatness, it turns into a metal. And because of the abundance of this moisture—shiny, pure, and solid—

<margin>
p Plato in *Timaeus* and there Proclus and Calcidius. Aristotle, book 3 of *Meteorology* at the end. Theophrastus.
</margin>

metals are so bright and polished. This is the opinion of Plato, Aristotle, and their followers<sup>p</sup>. Nor, in my judgment, is the opinion of those Chemists who both know and embrace physical principles—that is, those who are not mere empirics but are lovers of learning—repugnant to this view. For such men easily assent to this remote principle of all metals. Indeed, it seems to me it cannot be denied. For I ask, whether quicksilver and sulfur are so immediately...

# BOOK I. CHAPTER V. QUESTION II.

<margin>Book on Stones <note>likely referring to Aristotle or Theophrastus</note></margin>
...born from <term>prime matter</term> <note>the fundamental, formless substance from which all things are made</note>? If you say yes, I ask: by what preceding disposition was this form introduced rather than another? If you deny that they emerged immediately, I ask: what preceded them? For that matter could not have lacked all form. Therefore, this must not be denied: but we must go further, and after this common matter which is so remote, we must seek a more immediate matter. All dispute concerns this. I would therefore judge that this remote matter does not jump suddenly from one extreme to the other, skipping the middle steps—advancing, as they say, by a leap—but first certain more imperfect things are brought forth, and thus nature proceeds toward the more perfect. From that remote matter, first **sulfur** and **quicksilver** or <term>hydrargyrum</term> <note>original: "ὑδράργυρον"; Greek for liquid silver/mercury</note> are born. The latter primarily coalesces from that oily and airy moisture (which is why it is so mobile); while from the fatness or viscosity of the earth, sulfur is primarily born.

<margin>Janus Lacinus the Minorite, Book 1, Chemical Art, ch. 13.</margin>
<margin>Albertus, Book 3 on Metals, tract 1, ch. 2. Contarini, Book 3 of Philosophy, ch. 6.</margin>
The **Chymists** <note>early chemists or alchemists</note> maintain that this sulfur later acts as the male in the generation of metal, and the quicksilver acts as the female. This matters little; I would rather believe that each acts and is acted upon by the other through a mutual reaction. Nor does the objection of certain people convince me otherwise: namely, that gold is not found in most mines of sulfur or quicksilver, nor is sulfur or quicksilver found in most gold mines. For it is enough that traces of these are found in some, as <term>pyrotechnicians</term> <note>those who work with fire/heat, here meaning metallurgists or alchemists</note> have often experienced.

<margin>Georgius Agricola on Metals.</margin>
And just as metals are born from these as if from matter, so the chymists maintain that metals are resolved back into these again. They prove this resolution by smell and taste, claiming to find the qualities of sulfur and quicksilver in the resolved matter. But since we are dealing here only with the accidental differences by which these inanimate things are distinguished by the senses alone, the keen sense of smell in dogs is raised as an objection in vain. For it is not said that these are resolved into formal and perfect sulfur and mercury, but into a certain matter which, retaining most of the qualities of sulfur and mercury, sufficiently indicates that those forms preceded these fossils <note>in the 16th-century sense: anything dug from the earth</note>. Just as when a man is killed and the corpse is almost dissolved by corruption, the stench gives evidence that the body was once made of flesh; or, more similarly, when you dissolve salt or sugar into some dish, the flavor indicates that the substance of those things was the matter of this seasoning; and the scent of distilled water shows clearly enough which herbs were distilled.

<margin>Avicenna's letter to Hasen, Geber, Lullus, and others; see Lacinus in the cited Book 1.</margin>
Furthermore, it is not necessary for all things to be resolved into their most immediate matter; for a body is not resolved back into seed. Instead, it is enough to be resolved into a more remote or elementary matter. Therefore, regarding the final part of the argument, we say: metals are immediately resolved into those principles of theirs when they are altered, and through that alteration they cease to appear as such metals and become something liquid or dry, retaining many qualities of those principles. When this happens, they are then in a way resolved again into simple or elementary bodies—namely, into a moist liquid and a fatty earth—just as gradually, through various alterations of pyrotechnics, those primary immediate principles coalesced into this or that metal.

Thirdly, they argue that the foundations of the Chymists, if inspected more deeply, are either false or entirely doubtful; they propose and attack the following points, to which I will add solutions individually and in order.

<margin>Book on the Marvelous Power of Art and Nature.</margin>
First, they bring forward that saying of **Roger Bacon**:
> *Although nature is powerful and wonderful, yet art, using nature as an instrument, is more powerful than natural virtue.*

This, then, seems to be false. Firstly, because human art can do nothing that exceeds the powers of nature (which is nothing other than the ordinary disposition of God in His creatures). Therefore, if any effect is produced that surpasses the nature of things, it is not human, but either divine or monstrous. Secondly, because every mechanical art...
