<meta>This page continues a scholastic debate regarding the limits of art (alchemy) versus nature, specifically addressing the theories of Roger Bacon and the definitions of metals.</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

Art rests upon nature as its foundation and imitates it; yet, by its own efforts, it cannot equal the perfections of nature’s works. 
<margin>Book 3 of the *Institutes of Oratory*</margin> 
*For most truly did Fabius <note>The Roman rhetorician Quintilian</note> write: imitation, which is the proper domain of art, never expresses nature with such success that there is not always some distance between them; truth itself leaves hypocrisy <note>In this context, "hypocrisy" refers to a "mimic" or "imitation"</note> following in its footsteps many leagues behind.* Anyone who denies this should be commanded to compare the lilies of the field with true ones, the flight of Daedalus with that of birds, or to escape the hands of an enemy upon the horse of Praxiteles or Phidias <note>Famous Greek sculptors; the author implies a statue cannot run away</note>. 

*Response:* The comparison of nature and art can be established in two ways. First, precisely and stripped down, by comparing one alone with the other alone, and matching the power of each separately and mutually. If Bacon <note>Roger Bacon, 13th-century philosopher and scientist</note> intended this so that he might attribute to his furnaces those things denied to nature, I confess he was most worthy of all censure, and the contrary is solidly proven by argument. But in the second way, these can be compared not by cutting one off from the other, but extensively; for example, human craftsmanship combined with nature itself. In this comparison, craftsmanship presupposes the force of nature. It is evident from many experiences that art, supported and aided by nature, can then achieve more than nature can alone. For in this way, natural operation is helped, accelerated, and perfected through human industry and art. Roger’s words indicate that he intended only this, and it is most true.

Secondly, they criticize what is said by the Chemists <note>Alchemists</note>: that there is only one perfect metal, or a single species of metal (gold), and that the other metals are merely the beginnings of gold. Therefore, they claim the remaining metals are as easily reduced to gold—their natural perfection—as a sick man is restored to health, or an egg to a chick. They seem to themselves to not merely shake this idea, but to undermine it with a triple battering ram. It once seemed so to me as well; but now, having weighed the matter more maturely, I see that those very arguments I then brought forward can be disarmed and weakened by a suitable explanation—just as those under siege are accustomed to break the force of a battering ram with wool sacks. 

These three battering rams were covered by a triple shield. The first is taken from the definition of metal, which Geber, Petrus Bonus, and many chemists admit: *metal is a fossilized body, hard, preserving its nature, and expandable by a hammer.* But this entire definition applies equally to silver and lead as it does to gold; therefore, they are just as much metals as gold is. 

*Response:* The essential differences of things (except for man) are unknown to us. Therefore, we are forced to form definitions from accidental differences, which can often be common to many things that probably seem to differ in species. For instance, if you define a dog, you certainly define it by the property of barking; yet the hyena imitates this, though it is not a dog. Thus, this whole collection—"a dog is an irrational animal, four-footed, swift, capable of barking"—will also apply to the hyena. For it too will be an irrational animal, four-footed, swift, and capable of barking. Yet no one would infer that the name of "dog" fits the hyena, or that a hyena is equally a dog; rather, one should infer that something is found in the hyena which is more perfect in the dog. So I say that the accidental conditions of this kind of definition found in individual metals do not prove that each of these to which they apply are distinct species of metal. For this is not an agreement of genus in substance, but only, as it were, an agreement of genus in accidents. For specific differences must be drawn from the essence of the thing. Therefore, for the adversaries to prove generic identity from a definition, it is necessary that they first prove—or prove simultaneously and consequently—the essential difference of the alleged species, or something somehow equivalent to it. For example: Man and horse are species under the genus of animal. One cannot prove that simply because the definition of animal (which is nonetheless essential) applies to both (for it applies to Peter and Paul, and yet Peter and Paul do not differ in species), but it must be proven besides because man is possessed of reason and a horse is not. So also, that an eagle and a lion differ in species is not to be proven only by the fact that a generic definition applies to them, but...

<meta>The text breaks off mid-sentence; the catchword indicates the next page begins with "but"</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the relationship between alchemy (art) and nature, defending Roger Bacon's view that art can perfect nature. It also begins a technical refutation of the argument that all metals are distinct species, suggesting instead they may be developmental stages of gold.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and alchemical debate regarding the nature of metals, specifically whether base metals are distinct species or merely "immature" versions of gold. It follows the previous discussion on the definitions of substances.</meta>

# BOOK I. CHAPTER V. QUESTION II.

...but also because the essence of each is different, since one is volatile <note>original: *volatile*, referring to birds or substances that evaporate</note> and the other is a beast or a walking creature. Thus, it is not because the definition of gold applies to silver that these two are distinguished by species; rather, it follows only that both are metals: silver indeed imperfectly, because it can be perfected; gold most perfectly, because it cannot be further perfected in the nature of a metal. The definition of "man" applies equally to an infant and a grown man, just as "animal" applies to both a calf and an ox; yet the infant can still be perfected and can participate more perfectly in the parts of that definition. Nevertheless, the infant and the man are not different species of human. The same must be concluded regarding other metals and gold, for nature established that other nature for this purpose: so that they might progress toward golden perfection.

But here new difficulties arise, and new obstacles emerge. For someone might say that from this it would follow that nature more often wanders from its goal than achieves its end—a reproach that would be directed at God. Furthermore, the "aberrations" of nature are not usually predicated <note>A logical term meaning to state or assert something about a subject</note> of their subordinates in the manner of species. This is because nature does not multiply individuals of imperfect things, as we see in the case of monsters <note>In early modern science, "monsters" referred to any biological birth that deviated from the natural norm</note> which are punished with the loss of fertility. Yet these metals are rightly predicated of their individuals, such as "silver" of this or that piece. **I respond.** Nature does not wander from its goal, because its end is to adorn this world with a variety of things, and it intends for the more perfect things to be rarer. However, it happens through the greed of men, who dig up metals before they reach their due perfection, that there is less gold than other metals. Just as because many calves are killed by butchers, fewer oxen exist; if these were spared, they would become oxen; if those [metals] were left from being dug up, they would gradually turn into gold <note>original: *aurescerent*, literally "to become golden"</note>. Truly, the method of naming is sometimes by species or genus, and sometimes merely accidental. The accidental naming of monsters can take place just as well as that of silver, gold, etc. For just as you rightly say, "this vessel is silver," so you also rightly say, "the Gorgon is a monster," or "the Sphinx is a monster." And just as you might say the essence of this vessel is tin, or its matter is tin, nothing prevents you from truly saying, "this pygmy is monstrous," or "the substance of this pygmy is monstrous." But these points regarding naming matter little to the case. Because in truth, not everything that is still imperfect is for that reason "monstrous"—especially if it is fit to receive its perfection. A chick in an egg is not monstrous, a calf is not monstrous, an infant is not monstrous; because this one tends toward a man, that one toward an ox, and the other toward a rooster; they are even potentially what they will be in actuality. The Chemists <note>original: *Chemici*, referring here to Alchemists</note> say the same regarding metals other than gold. Only the monsters of perfect living beings are condemned to sterility, not those of insects or inanimate things. And thus this machine of counter-argument collapses.

<margin>Richard the Englishman in the *Correctorium*, and Giovanni Bracesco against Tauladanus.</margin>

Secondly, the adversaries contend that the "proximate matter" <note>The immediate physical substance required for a specific form to exist</note> of all metals cannot be the same, but that there must be a different proximate matter for each species. This is even admitted by some Chemists, and it is proved thus: because forms that differ in species cannot give form to the same species of proximate matter. This is evident regarding the human soul and the equine soul, neither of which is fit to give form to the body of the other. Otherwise, it would seem that the old madness could be established: *all things from all things*, or that anything can be born from anything. Thus, if the identity of "remote matter" <note>The underlying, universal substance of all things</note> were sufficient, one Alchemist could transform another into that [famous] Apuleius, or the long-eared Lucian <note>References to classical stories of men transformed into donkeys</note>. **I respond.** The souls of perfect animals require organic bodies, disposed perfectly in certain ways. The souls of imperfect animals also—because they are drawn out from matter—require their own specific, though less perfect, composition of bodily organs. This composition or (as I might say) **organization** belongs to the essence of that individual. But I judge that inanimate things require only that common matter...

<summary>The author argues that base metals are not distinct species but are "immature" gold, comparing them to infants or calves that have not yet reached their full potential. He refutes the idea that this makes nature "imperfect," blaming human greed for mining metals before they can naturally mature into gold.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, transmutation of metals, Aristotelian physics, proximate matter, Richardus Anglicus, Giovanni Bracesco, biological analogies, perfection of nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of metals and the possibility of alchemical transmutation, specifically addressing the arguments for and against the idea that all metals share a single underlying species.</meta>

# OF MAGICAL INVESTIGATIONS

and especially that it consists of the same homogeneous parts and is more perfectly disposed toward the form of lead, tin, silver, or gold: because these forms do not result from the structure of the composition or organs, but from the greater or lesser participation in the quality of the "primes" <note>The "primes" refers to the primary qualities or elements—heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—that govern the temperament of matter.</note>. If they have more of an earthy mixture, they become iron, copper, or lead; if they have more of an airy liquid, they become tin, silver, or gold. Nature indeed always strives to produce gold; but when the decoction of the mineral is unable to reach that point, it is content with the generation of other metals according to the measure of its power and the disposition of the nearby matter. If there is not quite enough subtle and red sulfur present, but there is enough subtle and white mercury <note>original: "hydrargyri". In alchemy, mercury and sulfur are the two fundamental principles of all metals.</note>, silver will be made: but if the power of the sulfur exceeds this, and that oily liquid is thoroughly purified in every part, gold will be produced.

<margin>G</margin>
The third of their "rams" <note>A metaphorical term for a battering-ram or a forceful argument used to knock down an opponent's position.</note> was this: that nature never rests of its own accord, but moves and labors until it has obtained its perfection. Yet these metals appear to everyone—even when the efficient cause is present—to rest in their veins, with no concern for being transformed into gold. For even if they remain there for a hundred years unimpeded, they will never become more "golden," but will remain in their own state as iron, lead, or silver. If they lie quiet in this way, they have therefore already reached their perfection. The Chemists <note>The term "Chemici" here refers specifically to Alchemists.</note> *respond*, and respond well, that we are unable to perceive this change: but as to why we cannot, they give an incredible reason: that a period of a thousand years is required for the metamorphosis of another metal into gold. Who revealed this definite time to them? Who would believe that for the perfection of other metals, a course of twenty or thirty years is sufficient? A longer delay is therefore required in forming gold, but one of so many centuries is not to be admitted. Just as we do not see the growth of herbs and trees while they are growing, but perceive that they have grown: so this alteration and change of metals is hidden; it can be seen not while it is happening, but when it has been done. That we do not see it is caused by impatience and greed, as men prefer to take a smaller present gain and dig out the metal as it is, rather than to redeem hope at a price and wait for that so distant transmutation. But why do Fanianus and others boast that they can reach this in three years with their small furnaces and bellows, if nature cannot do it unless several five-year periods have passed? I think the cause is that in the veins of the earth more liquid moisture flows in, and the heat also acts gently and gradually: here in the "gold-maker" <note>original: "chrysollo", likely referring to the alchemical crucible or vessel</note>, no liquid flows in: therefore whatever resists and holds back the course of the efficient cause is more easily consumed here: the fire is also continuous and unimpeded, and presses as much as it can. Furthermore, craftsmen sometimes mix in some mature and perfect gold, so that art, helping nature, may be the cause of converting the rest more swiftly, as Trevisan notes in his *Book on the Transmutation of Metals, before the middle.*

<margin>Albertus Magnus, Book 3 on Metals, Tract 1, Chapter 7. Geber, The Rosary. Vannoccio Biringuccio, and others.</margin>
From these things it appears that the opinion of Callisthenes, Albertus Magnus, and the Chemists is quite probable: that there is only one perfect species of metal. But I shall continue to be generous and courteous; let it be so: suppose metals differ in species, what then? They say that a change from species to species, where one is not subordinate to the other, is impossible for nature. We shall soon show that it is not impossible. Nor does that point about subordination matter. For in truth, the change of many things is shown which are not so subordinated: and that subordination can be understood in the same way regarding lead and gold, tin and silver, as they understand those things which we shall bring forward in the third section.

The fourth argument is: this art cannot transform true gems into other true gems. Therefore, it cannot transform metals into others. *Response:* gems, if there are any that are meltable, can perhaps be reduced to a greater perfection: however, they cannot be changed into others of a different species, because fire cannot induce just any color.

It addresses the counter-argument regarding the long timescales of natural transmutation versus the accelerated processes of the alchemist's furnace.</summary>

# BOOK II. CHAPTER V. QUESTION I. SECTION II.

color: nor is there that kinship between gem and gem as there is between metal and metal. Indeed, most gems—or rather all of them—because they are of the genus of stones, are also infusible <note>original: "illiquabiles"; meaning they cannot be melted into a liquid state like metals</note>: because they do not possess an unctuous moisture as metals do, but rather a watery one, which is dissipated by fire. <margin>* So says Albertus Magnus in Book 5 of the *Metaphysics*.</margin>

Lastly, they object that no sufficient cause is provided to bring this about: for they claim the heavens naturally produce these metals in veins through heat and cold; not insofar as that heat or cold is merely a quality, but insofar as it is the organ and instrument of the heavens, by whose power it acts. Here, however, they claim neither cold is present in the Chemical operation, nor does heat work as the instrument of a principal agent, because no principal agent is present here. *I respond*: we have already spoken concerning cold. Regarding heat, I say that it acts as the instrument of fire, and is of the same species as celestial or elementary heat. Moreover, the principal agent is the "seed of gold" <note>The "semen auri" was a common alchemical concept suggesting that metals possess a biological-like potential to grow into perfection</note> implanted in the metals themselves: which seed is nourished by heat and matures into gold. Indeed, no other proof of this is required than that of experience, to which the alchemists are accustomed to appeal. For we are used to proving *a posteriori* <note>from the effect back to the cause</note> that the power of a seed was present in a thing when we see something generated from it and led from potentiality into act. Finally, our conclusion merely required us to show that this Gold-making <term>Chrysopoeia: the alchemical art of making gold</term> is not contrary to the nature of things, and that the arguments of the adversaries do not prove otherwise. This, I believe, has been sufficiently shown by us. So much for the first axiom.

### SECTION III.
->*Concerning the same efficacy.*<-

<margin>A</margin> LET the 2nd axiom BE: *it is far more probable that the alchemical art can achieve its end, and transmute other things into gold by fire, than that it cannot.*

I say it is *more probable*, because in truth it is not proven apodictically <note>with absolute logical certainty</note>, but only topically <note>based on persuasive or probable arguments</note>. Nevertheless, the reasons are such that they are almost morally convicting.

The first argument by which the conclusion is proved depends on what was said above; because the opposing negative arguments are not conclusive, nor even very pressing.

Second, because the natural causes of many things are hidden from us, and this may be among their number: many things also happen naturally which someone—due to ignorance of the causes—might either deny happened, or contend they were not done naturally, if he did not know the fact for certain.

<margin>a Book 3 on the *Trinity*</margin> Third, because that industry of the Magi, by which they changed snakes into rods and vice versa, was a natural change according to St. Augustine ^a; yet that is more wonderful than this matter which we are discussing.

Fourth, because the heat of fire has the power of transforming metals through various alterations: melting, separation, gathering, and others of this sort; just as the innate heat <note>the "calor nativus" believed to drive human digestion</note> can variously shape food before it increases and provides growth to the nourished body itself. Nor is that change of food (say, an apple) into flesh less to be wondered at than the change of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> into gold. Finally, if you consider those individual alterations which the Gold-makers require—even those who require the most—you will find none that exceeds the power of fire acting upon disposed matter. For I ask, which of them does? And if fire is equal to each individual step, why not to all of them successively, since its power is not blunted when always supplied with new fuel?

<margin>B</margin> <margin>b Exercise 23. c Questions on Alchemy, Book on Magic.</margin> Fifth, this ought to persuade a well-disposed intellect, both because of various similar experiences and the assertion of many people claiming they have achieved it. I will prove this latter point in the following section; the former is proved by the disputes which Scaliger ^b and Benedictus Pererius ^c have reported at length. First, art can generate wasps,

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

<margin>
d. chapter 4.
d. book on metallurgy <note>original: "de re metallica" by Georgius Agricola</note>.
e. Schal. f.
f. book 33, chapter 6.
g. book 2, To Glaucon, chapter 7.
</margin>
beetles and hornets from carcasses and from the dung of animals; indeed, even scorpions from the herb Basil, when properly placed and arranged in certain spots. But these living things are more excellent than metals; therefore, nature should also be able to produce metals. You might say that in these cases, man by his art only applies matter to matter, while nature produces the rest through putrefaction <note>The historical belief that life could arise spontaneously from decaying matter</note> and the heat of the sun. Truly, in our case as well, man only applies his hand, while the heat of the fire effects the rest in the matter and draws out those things which lay hidden in its potential. 2. Pliny is the authority that gold is produced from orpiment <note>term: auripigmentum (arsenic trisulfide), a yellow mineral once thought to be "immature" gold</note> by art, as we have said. If you say that orpiment is a sterile and poor vein of gold—that is, imperfect gold, as Fanianus says—this can also be denied, as it is stated without proof. For it seems to differ more from gold than copper does; for copper is to be numbered among metals, whereas that [orpiment] is rather among colors and dyes. 3. From eggs, by the heat of the furnace in Cairo, chicks are accustomed to be drawn out, no differently than by the warmth of the mother. Why then should the furnace here not take the place of the womb of the earth? If you say that an egg is ordained by nature toward a chick and is a chick in potential, the alchemists contend that lead is the same in respect to gold, and even to silver; and for that reason, these metals are usually found near one another. Indeed, Pliny says that the vein of lead, which is usually neighboring and contiguous to the vein of silver, is called *galena*. But even an egg differs more from a chick than metals do among themselves. 4. Naturally, we see many small worms changed into flying things, such as flies; and yet a creeping thing and a flying thing differ by more than just species. 5. Just as nature produces salt, bitumen, and ink, so also the industry of craftsmen exhibits those things to us. If you say these differ in species from those, I will urge the opposite: since they have the same qualities (which experience teaches), they differ no more than the chicks produced by the furnaces of Cairo differ from the chicks warmed by the mother's breast. 6. Pliny is the authority that wheat and winter wheat are changed into one another. Will you say they differed only by accidents <note>In Aristotelian logic, "accidents" are non-essential qualities like color or size</note> and were of the same species? This seems to be the opinion of Galen, but it is not approved by many. Yet let it be so; if this is true, what of the next point? 7. For according to Theophrastus, darnel <note>A type of weed</note> was also changed into wheat. Will they say this darnel was wild wheat? By what authority? For we know what a difference there is between wheat and darnel. 8. It is well enough known that *chalcitis* is changed into *misy* <note>Ancient terms for various copper and iron sulfates</note>; it is even more likely that they differ in species than that they are the same. 9. That wood turns to stone if it falls into certain springs is known to those in the Ardennes; and who would deny it is both wood and a trunk? Very well, the adversaries do not deny it. But they will perhaps say this change is for the worse—namely, of living wood into stone devoid of life—and that this is a kind of death for the wood, as things degenerate more easily than they are improved. I urge that they can also be improved. For in Scotland, from the rotten fragments of ships and from the fruit of trees falling into the sea (a matter most commonly known, and which will be proven in the following book), ducks are born <note>A reference to the "Barnacle Goose" myth, where geese were thought to grow from driftwood or barnacles</note>. If water exhibits such an admirable effect of transmutation to us, why do we spitefully detract from the efficacy of fire, whose power in acting is far greater? Herodotus even writes that a plane tree turned into an olive; and others that corpses turned into statues of salt, of which I shall say more at the end of Book 2, Question 18.

<margin>
h. This is the opinion of the Scot, Lychetus, on 2.d.7. or 8. I will prove it in the following book, question 14.
</margin>
Finally, it is most well known that demons can at least produce—through the application of natural agents—not only inanimate things, but also imperfect animals, whichever are born from putrefaction. Much more easily, therefore, can one common metal be changed into another by the same application, because the difference between metals is far less than that between the inanimate and the animate. But the Devil can produce no effect of this kind which exceeds the powers of nature, as is established; otherwise, he could perform true miracles. Therefore, an effect of this kind does not exceed the powers of nature. Thus, it can happen that either by the teaching of the Devil, or by industry and study, man may finally reach this knowledge.

# SECTION IV.

## Is it established by any narrative that gold has ever been made by this art?

<margin>
<sup>a</sup> Extravagant *Spondent* regarding the crime of forgery. <note>Refers to the decree *Spondent quas non exhibent* by Pope John XXII (1317) against alchemists.</note>
</margin>

The adversaries hurl no weapon stronger than that of experience. Why, they ask, has no effect followed until now? Why have all lost their oil and labor? Do we not conclude morally from this that it should be numbered among the impossible things? 2. Because whatever experiences are brought forward rely on the testimonies of the chemists themselves, who are least to be believed: both because they are in their own cause, and because their beggary and poverty take away their credibility; and also because, fascinated by hope and a vehement grasp of the imagination, they think that is gold which is not, as Pope John XXII says.<sup>a</sup> 3. Because the operations can be either fraudulent and deceptive, as was the Alchemy of Bragadini the Venetian; who, having been executed in Bavaria in the year 1591, himself confessed his imposture in front of everyone; that he had melted his gold from scrapings and powders of gold which he had mixed with charcoal dust. Therefore, one must be careful lest the reader be deceived by Villamont himself, who, having been deceived, stubbornly defends the gold-making of Antonio Bragadini as true and sincere: for when he wrote those things, time had not yet clearly revealed the fraud of this Cypriot. *See what Villamont says of him in Book 3, Chapter 28 of his Travels.* Or the operations can be prodigious, through the work of a demon. 4. Because no one has ever mastered this art; which is evident from the fact that no one has ever said they achieved its ultimate effect: nor could anyone teach it to others by words or writings. With these arguments they fight.

->*Let the conclusion be: That some have in fact made true gold by this art seems to be highly credible.*<-

<margin>
<sup>b</sup> Book 11, ch. 13.
<sup>c</sup> Jo. Andreas in additions to the *Speculator* under the title of the crime of forgery. Oldradus, counsel 74 on fortunetelling. Also Baldus and Jo. Plateanus.
<sup>d</sup> Pena, Commentary 36 on part 2 of Eymeric’s *Directory*, question 11.
<sup>e</sup> Volume 20, book 3.
<sup>f</sup> R. C. in the *Nomenclature of Distinguished Writers*.
</margin>

I prove this because there are so many narratives of different people concerning this matter that it would seem the mark of an impudent mind and a hardened brow to believe no one. I shall set down what I have at hand. Caelius Rhodiginus is the authority<sup>b</sup> that in his time in France, a certain man from the lowest dregs of the people, wonderfully shrewd, devised a way by which, through the power of "gold-dissolving water" <note>original: *chrysulfæ aquæ*, likely aqua regia</note>, he separated gold from any metal: and he adds that such craftsmen are called *Chrysoplyntae* <note>Gold-washers</note>, and the workshop indeed a *Chrysoplysium* <note>Gold-wash-house</note>. Concerning Arnold of Villanova, very grave legal experts report<sup>c</sup> that he produced gold rods by the chemical art, which he submitted to the examination of everyone in the court of the Pope, and therefore they conclude that chemistry is a true science. They object that Arnold was strongly suspected of familiarity with demons<sup>d</sup>: and therefore either a demon truly did this, or at least substituted true gold. I respond that these are malicious conjectures. If Arnold was suspected, how was he not seized in the Roman court, where he was a constant guest, as a sorcerer and punished? Moreover, it is a poor conjecture that a demon substituted true gold. Others say he "submitted to examination," that is, he offered it, but did not actually perform the trial; I, however, believe from the fact that it is not written by those authors that false gold was detected, that experience more probably supported Arnold. Indeed, this is confirmed by what the author of the *Theater of Human Life* handed down<sup>e</sup> concerning his debate with Raymond Lully; that this Raymond the Balearic, a most sharp-witted man, demonstrated to Villanova by many reasons the <term>impotence</term> <note>original: *ἀδυναμίαν* (adynamian) - inability or impossibility</note> of this art; then Arnold, weary of disputing, said, "rather, I shall convince you tomorrow." When that day dawned, he showed him the metamorphosis with eye-witnessed faith: moved by which, Lully himself embraced this art and wrote many short works about it, nearly 250 years ago. Concerning this Lully, a certain physician of our time (not an alchemist) writes<sup>f</sup>: *I find by inquiring that among the English, he truly performed what he professes in his books: and in the Tower of London, by order of the King...*

<summary>The author argues for the historical reality of alchemy by citing accounts of Arnold of Villanova and Raymond Lully, while dismissing the fraud of the impostor Bragadini as an exception rather than the rule.</summary>

...that he had produced the most excellent gold; and a type of coin was shown to me, which they still call the "Noble of Raymond" <note>original: "Nobile Raymundi"; referring to Raymond Lull, a famous 13th-century polymath often associated with alchemy</note>, made of gold that is truly pure and refined, and of the highest standard.] The same author of the *Treasury* also relates this concerning Bernard of Treviso; that although he had initially squandered great wealth in vain upon this "Chrysolyntic" <note>The art of gold-washing or gold-making</note> craft, he finally achieved it through more diligent study, so that within two years he brought the art into practice and his hope into reality. <margin>g In the work *On Subtlety*, book on metals.</margin> Girolamo Cardano <note>A famous Italian Renaissance physician and mathematician</note> testifies that Antonio, a pharmacist of Treviso, in the presence of Doge Andrea Gritti and the leading Venetian patricians, changed quicksilver into gold, and that certain traces of that work still exist.

Julius Scaliger, in his contradictory exercise, mocks rather than refutes this, saying: if this had been true gold, the Venetians would have compelled him to devote this labor to the Republic or to reveal the secret of the craft. For who indicated to Scaliger that the man from Treviso did not reveal it to them? Indeed, I would believe that the Venetians either despised the hope of an uncertain outcome or ascribed the effect to illusions. You, Reader, lest you feel the same, accept another testimony of the same matter, so that this experiment may stand on the faith of Cardano and Aragosius <note>Guglielmo Gratarolo, a physician and alchemist</note>. This William Aragosius, a man highly skilled in philosophy and medicine, was living in Padua for the sake of his studies; once, in the year 1550, he made an excursion to Venice, and as a mark of respect visited Hector Ausonius, famous for his renown in mathematics and medicine. There, when among other things mention was made of the alchemy of gold-making, and William was undermining it with solid arguments (as it then seemed to him): *Ausonius called a meeting for the following day, and brought there three Venetian patricians from the most noble family of the Cornelii; he took care to exhibit for inspection and handling rings made from that gold which the man from Treviso had made in the sight of the fathers—that is, quickly, without a great delay intervening, in a moment. Afterwards, Aragosius himself, traveling to Treviso, learned the history of the deed. That man related that he had received a powder as a gift of honor from a certain Frenchman, very similar in appearance to Aragosius, who had been hospitably received in his home for several months, but on the condition that he use it as a secret. He, however, moved not so much by private gain as by zeal for public loyalty toward the Venetian Republic, had sailed to Venice without the Frenchman's knowledge and revealed the matter to the fathers: he showed a specimen of the art, and also privately in the house of the Cornelii more than once. Meanwhile, however, that Frenchman departed without saying goodbye to anyone, nor was he able to learn anything further about him.*] You see the reason why the Venetians did not press <margin>k *Treasury of Human Life*. I omit what Fernel testifies about himself in the book *On Hidden Causes*.</margin> the man from Treviso, and how empty is that suspicion of that great man [Scaliger], who was often the unfair "scourge of Cardano" <note>original: "Cardano-mastygis"; Scaliger was famous for his harsh critiques of Cardano</note>. Perhaps by the same art, that man mentioned in Cedrenus <note>A Byzantine historian</note> had fashioned the golden bridle which he offered to the Emperor Anastasius. After the first edition of this work, I came across a book by Andreas Libavius, a physician, who in part 1 of his *Singularia*, Chapter 11 on metallurgy, reported several testimonies. But it seems that man was a trickster rather than a chemist.

Now let us respond to the objections. To the first: I have commemorated many whom not only chemists, but also physicians and most serious legal scholars testify have exhibited the thing itself as done—Arnold, Lull, the man from Treviso. And today I could name serious men whom I know, placed in high dignity and God-fearing, who profess the same of themselves. I could never bring myself to believe that these men lie, or use impostures, or illusions, or demonic arts; and those who could suspect such a thing would be doing an injustice to most honorable men. To the second objection, I say that the Pope <note>Likely referring to Pope John XXII's decree against alchemists</note> speaks of the common crowd, or rather that dregs of chemists who hunt for profit and rush headlong through greed for money; not of those who seek from it only an honorable knowledge of the nature of things. Nor is it true that none but beggars or the poor apply themselves to this study, since many Princes do not shun it. Nor does all poverty bar one from testimony; but only that which baseness or a vicious life dishonors. Phocion was poor; yet I would have believed him more without an oath than I would have believed Paris with an oath. Testimony in one's own cause is rejected, but here the testimo-

# BOOK I. CHAPTER V. QUESTIONS II. AND III.

testimony was given by writers who were not Alchemists. They had indeed received this from the alchemists; but the alchemists proved their words by deeds. Nor truly should "domestic" testimony <note>Testimony from within one's own group or profession.</note> be rejected, just as other types are not rejected elsewhere when they are unsuitable, provided that testimony cannot be obtained from elsewhere <margin>h Peter Gerard, Singularia 100. Hippolytus, Singularia 71.</margin> $^h$. And I ask you, from where could the beginning of history be obtained here, if not from the words and reports of the craftsmen themselves? To the third point: that such people <note>referring to fraudulent alchemists</note> can sometimes exist, and have existed; yet it does not follow from this that there were none who were not such. To the fourth: many have handed down this art in words and writings, and today they hold it and teach others. If any errors happen to occur in practice, it is no wonder, because it is easy to err in those things which depend upon the operation of fire. Nor is it the part of any craftsman to always promise a certain effect in those matters which depend upon the activity of an external natural agent. Nor is there any other art in which someone of sound mind would assert that he has attained the ultimate peak of perfection. There always remains something to be learned: nor is this peculiar to this art, as is objected.

---

## QUESTION II.
### Whether Gold-making <term>Chrysopoeia: The art of making gold</term> is to be held among the number of the noble arts, or should be counted among the mechanical ones?

<margin>A</margin> The decision is easy: *nothing in it is mechanical except the operation itself, and that application of fire. But the Theory or speculation of the art itself, lying hidden in the most remote hiding places of physiology <note>In this context, "physiology" refers to the general study of nature and natural philosophy.</note>, is by its nature just as noble and just as scientific as physics (of which it is no ignoble portion) or medicine, which it serves and assists. On that account, it is to be numbered among the higher and secret disciplines <note>original: "acroamaticis & epopticis" — referring to advanced lectures for initiated students and the highest level of mystery-knowledge.</note>.* If, however, you consider the Alchemists themselves, some of them are mere mechanics, blowers <term>flaturarii: those who work the bellows</term>, and ash-scrapers <term>ciniflones: a derogatory term for those who work with hair-curling irons or furnace ashes</term>; but some claim for themselves by their own right the honorable and especially laudable name of philosophers. For although every Alchemist uses his intellect and his hand at the same time, yet that is common to every mechanical craftsman, and the hand claims the chief part of the work in those who—ignorant of the principles and causes of the art, and not applying a certain use in preparing things according to suitable and scientific precepts—consume time and coal; therefore, they are to be called sooty and merely mechanical. But those who know the causes and precepts of the art in extracting oils, waters, gums, and gold, and relying on these, promise themselves an effect; these, although they join the hand to the intellect and the operation to speculation, are not to be called mechanical, but philosophers. For that operation and the help of the hands are accidental to the science, as is sufficiently manifest in the physician and the surgeon.

## QUESTION III.
### To which species of magic does it belong?

<margin>A</margin> It is equally easy to answer this question. *If the alchemical effect is not real, because the gold is false or only apparent, it belongs to prestigiatory magic <note>The magic of illusions and stage tricks.</note>. If the effect is real, then it happens either by the help of a demon, and belongs to demonic magic—I call it a "real effect" when someone obtains true gold; this, however, can happen in two ways: either by true transmutation, or by substitution, when a demon does this. This act can also be reduced to the prestigiatory kind, except when it is, as I said, demonic. But if a man extracts true gold by his own fire-art <term>pyrotechnia: the art of using fire, here meaning chemistry/alchemy</term> without the help of a demon, then alchemy belongs, according to itself and properly, to Natural Magic; and accidentally to the artificial kind.* For those <margin>a Aragosius and Zwinger.</margin> $^a$ who refer it to the supernatural on account of the influences of the heavens are, in my judgment, mistaken; for nothing is seen here, as has been shown, which exceeds the powers of nature. Examples of natural alchemy, therefore...

<margin>B</margin> <sig>F 2</sig> <meta>catchword: now not-</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

<margin>b See above, question 1, last section.</margin>
I have already proposed several points $^b$. One author on demonic madness <note>original: *dæmonomaniæ*</note> relates a story $^c$; 
<margin>c Book 3, chapter 3.</margin>
that he heard from William Constantine, a famous alchemist, that his associates—when the bellows had been blowing for a long time and no hope or appearance of the result was shown—sought advice from the Devil. They asked whether they were working correctly and would reach the desired end, or if they had committed some error that kept the sought-after metal away. The Devil (as is his custom) gave a deceptive answer; as brief as it was ambiguous: *“Work, work.”* <note>original: *Laborate, laborate*</note> They seized upon this as a favorable promise and were wonderfully encouraged; therefore, they continued with the blowing and the maintenance of the furnaces. They did this so strenuously that they reduced everything to nothing; nevertheless, they were prepared to continue, to boil the whole matter to the very bottom, and to squander <note>A pun in the original Latin: *dilapidare* (to squander) vs *lapide* (the stone).</note> all their goods for the sake of the philosopher's stone. This would have continued had not Constantine, taking pity on their madness, suggested that it is the demon's habit to deceive through the ambiguity of meaning: when he commanded them to "work," he meant they should desist from their fruitless effort and transfer themselves to some honest trade <note>original: *opificium*</note> in which a more certain reward for their labor might be seen. So he says. 

But others tell the story a little differently, and they write in good faith that they received it from the mouth of Natalis, who was Constantine's associate: these workers, greedy for gold, summoned a familiar spirit <note>original: *paredrum*</note> in the form of a nymph, and with a sacred chant demanded from it the preparation of the stone. The demon commanded that they should compose a mixture <note>original: *malagma*</note> from six "anisated" metals (as they call them)—that is, in equal proportion—mixed with a certain mass of quicksilver <note>mercury</note>. When they had placed this on the fire, behold, that minister of fraud, the cunning Mercury, scattered the metals here and there like hail with thunder, and their hopes along with them; while the evil spirit <note>original: *cacodæmone*</note> laughed at those he had mocked. Driven away from their "gold-glue" <note>original: *chrysollo*</note> but not from their madness, they went to the little master again, first complaining of the injury; then they became suppliants and proceeded to beseech him that now at least, in better faith, he might teach them what was to be done. Finally, he returned nothing else but: *“Work strenuously.”*

->Two stories occur regarding deceptive effects: the first is a diabolical illusion; the second pertains to deceptions and swindles through human cleverness.<-

Cedrenus <note>A Byzantine historian</note> tells the first as follows: *“A certain man from the number of those who profess the chemical art, skilled at dazzling the eyes of men with imposture, offered to silversmiths and others the hands and feet of statues, and other golden items, saying he had found a treasure: and thus he reduced many who were deceived to poverty. Caught by his widespread fame and brought before the Emperor Anastasius, he offered a horse's bridle cast from solid gold and set with pearls. But the Emperor, having accepted the bridle, said: 'Though you have deceived everyone, you shall certainly not deceive me'; and he immediately banished the man to a certain castle, where he finally perished.”* Truly, all these golden things were nothing but mockeries of the eyes made through magic. 

Jeremias Mederus describes the latter story in his *Miscellany*. Two chemists entered into a partnership confirmed by a pact: one professed to hold the most certain art of gold-making <note>original: *Chrysopoeiæ*</note>, while the other acted as a root-cutter <note>herbalist</note> or a wandering quack <note>original: *agyrtam empiricum*</note>, and among other things sold gold filings stained with a black color as "powder of lunaria." Ernest, Margrave of Baden, was wonderfully devoted to pyrotechnics <note>here meaning alchemy/chemistry</note>. Therefore, the first man approached and circumvented him, promising mountains of gold and the halls of wealthy Croesus. Everything is prepared, and now that man says, "Everything else is fine; only one thing is needed, which they call *Resch* (using a Hebrew word); the powders for this are easy to find at any apothecary or root-cutter's." The Margrave sends a boy: conveniently, at the entrance of the palace, that other partner had displayed his wares; and as is the custom of this kind of people, he proclaimed with a Stentorian voice that he lacked nothing. The Prince's boy approaches him. He produces the *Resch* <note>The Hebrew letter 'R', used here as a nonsensical alchemical term</note> from a smoky little box, weighs it out for a solid price, and as if it were something common and trodden underfoot, he adds extra weight. The boy brings it back to his Master. The impostor sprinkles that powder onto quicksilver and produces true gold—but because the test is made from gold melted into the powder, true gold is indeed found. The matter was wonderfully pleasing to the Margrave, and he was himself generous toward the master...

# QUESTION IV.

## Whether alchemy is licit or illicit?

<margin>
<sup>a</sup> *under the word Alchemy.*
</margin>
**A**ngelus de Clavasio<sup>a</sup> <note>A 15th-century Franciscan jurist known for his work on cases of conscience.</note> judged it to be prohibited, but he proves this by no specific law; however, Father Valencia inclines toward that view solely by reasoning, suggesting that at least by reason of circumstances it is illicit and pernicious; Father Pererius judged the same: both in the places cited previously.

<margin>
<sup>b</sup> *In the places of Oldradus, Johannes Andreae, the Abbot, and Baldus cited above in question 1, section 4. Albericus de Rosate under the word alchemy; Decisions of Guido Papa, 388. Fabian's treatise on buying and selling, question 5. Zanetini, chapter 1, on accusations. Thomas Arsoncinus, in a certain legal opinion on this matter.*
</margin>
However, the common opinion of the interpreters of the law<sup>b</sup> is that it is licit and prohibited by no law; indeed, it is even accepted among the metallic arts by civil law. We must proceed step by step, for a slip is easy here on a slippery path.

*First Conclusion: By no law in the public courts does this art seem prohibited, such that it is not permitted to practice it, based on the defense of positive law.*

Regarding civil and common Roman law (for I speak now of common law, not of the particular statutes of places or the sanctions of kingdoms), the matter is clear: no such constitution is found. Regarding Canon Law, two canons seem to stand in the way, namely the chapter *Episcopi* (26, question 5) and the chapter *Spondent*, in the *Extravagantes Communes*, book 5, on the crime of forgery. But, if you consider them well, neither canon proves the point. For the said chapter *Episcopi* only condemns those who believe the transformation of persons into various species of wild beasts can occur: which the added reasoning also teaches; for it says, *Satan transforms himself into various species of beasts.*] There is therefore no reason why the canon should be extended to any transformation of one species into another. And even if it were so extended, the alchemists would say they do not believe this metamorphosis is done by a demon, but by nature: nor that it is a change of a true and perfect species into another perfect species, but of the imperfect and incomplete into its own perfection. The other chapter, *Spondent*, binds more strongly, for it expressly prohibits alchemy and appoints the penalty of infamy and imprisonment; it even deprives clerics of their possessed benefices and renders them ineligible to receive others. Arsoncinus responds that the Pope was dealing only with those who sophistically <note>In this context, "sophistically" means using trickery or creating a false appearance of gold rather than a true chemical change.</note> feign transformation: yet the words of the Canon clearly enough indicate that John XXII <note>Pope John XXII (reigned 1316–1334) issued the famous decree *Spondent pariter* against alchemists.</note> believed no true gold was made by anyone, and therefore held them all for impostors; for the decree reads word-for-word thus:
> *The poor alchemists promise riches which they do not produce; likewise, those who consider themselves wise fall into the pit which they themselves have made. For without doubt the professors of this art of alchemy deceive one another, since, being conscious of their own ignorance, they admire those who have said anything of this sort above them; and when the sought-after truth does not support them, they appoint a day, exhaust their resources, and disguise their falsehood with words, so that finally they may feign that to be true gold or silver by a sophistic transformation which does not exist in the nature of things: and sometimes their condemned and damnable boldness proceeds so far that* <margin> *So it is read in the Plantin and Roman editions, but in a corrupted and mutilated way.* </margin> *they strike the characters of public money from base metals for trusting eyes, and by no other means than the alchemical fire of the furnace do they elude the ignorant common people. Wishing therefore for these things to be exiled for all time, we ordain by this edictal constitution: that whoever shall have made gold or silver of this kind, or shall have ordered it to be made after the fact, or shall have knowingly served those making it (while it was being done), or shall have knowingly used silver or gold by selling it or giving it in payment: true...*

F 3

<margin>
The reading should be "by deeds," with a simple change. But the rest remains faulty.
</margin>
...being convicted of such a crime, they shall be compelled to pay into the public fund for distribution to the poor as much gold or silver, by way of penalty, as the alchemical metal amounts to—concerning which it has been legally established that they offended in any of the aforementioned ways. Furthermore, those who manufacture alchemical gold or silver, or who knowingly use it as stated above, shall be stained with the mark of perpetual infamy. But if the resources of the offenders are not sufficient to pay the aforesaid monetary penalty, the discretion of a prudent judge may commute this penalty into another (such as imprisonment, or another according to the quality of the business, the status of the persons, and other attending circumstances). Those indeed, etc. <note>The author is quoting the papal bull *Spondent pariter* by Pope John XXII (1317), which criminalized alchemy.</note> He adds a penalty for those who abuse alchemical material for the debasement of coinage, concerning which there is another constitution of the same Pontiff, in the chapter *Prodiens*, under the title of the crime of forgery. To this Canon, a twofold response occurs to me: one, that it was not received into common use, but rather abolished by non-use. For afterward, this art flourished even in the city [of Rome] itself; especially, barely a hundred years later, under Leo X. For to him, Aurelius Augurellus, an excellent poet, dedicated his book on *Chrysopoeia* <note>The art of making gold; from the Greek *chrysos* (gold) and *poiein* (to make).</note>: who among other things boasted too much concerning his philosophical stone:

->If a tiny portion of it were cast through the waves<-
->Of the sea, and if that sea were then quicksilver,<-
->The whole immense ocean could be turned into gold.<-

There is also a book circulated by Pico della Mirandola on this matter, titled *On the Manufacture of Gold*: nor have books of this kind in Italy been of forbidden reading since that time. The second response is that the Canon proceeds from a hypothesis (that all alchemical gold is false) and therefore condemns only those who strike false coin; and that for the sake of profit; namely, so that they may use it in contracts and payments (which the text clearly indicates). Therefore, those who produce true gold and devote themselves to the art merely for the sake of the mind, not wishing to use this art in commerce or payments, are not condemned by this chapter. Therefore, it is not the art, which is rightly instituted and exercised, that would be condemned, but the abuse of the art. Let it be:

<margin>B</margin>
### **Second conclusion;** *In the court of conscience, this art is not permitted indiscriminately, but with selection: indeed, for most, as it is ruinous, so it must be judged illicit.*

The arguments of Angelus and the authors of the first opinion prove this conclusion best, but they have no force against the preceding one. It must also be noted that whatever is illicit does not proceed from the nature of the art (which is not evil in itself, indeed, insofar as it is an art and imitates the sciences, it is good in itself, like other speculative knowledge), but from the vice of circumstances, which are very often found in this operation. If these are absent, I do not see what can be reproved: but because they are rarely all absent, it seldom happens that vice and sin are not present by reason of some circumstance. Therefore, I said it must be judged ruinous and illicit for most.

But what are these circumstances? The principal ones and the heads of the others are four: the end, the manner, the time, and the person.

<margin>
C
So St. Thomas in the 2nd book of Sentences, dist. 7, q. 3, art. 1 & 2; and the Summa Theologica 22, q. 77, art. 2, reply 1. And there Cajetan, Silvester, and Fumus in their Summaries. Fabianus, q. 8. Albericus under the word "purchase."
</margin>
**If the end is profit and greed, it is illicit**, because profit from this art is illicit; for it is not permitted to use this gold for commerce or contracts, as John XXII decreed. Understand this if someone wished to use it as true gold. For the Doctors <note>The "Doctors" refers to established legal and theological scholars.</note> writing on this matter commonly contend this is illicit: and rightly so, for that gold either never has all the properties of true gold, or it happens so rarely that it must be judged as not happening. Therefore, such a likely cause for doubt existing, it is not permitted to bring it into commerce as true gold. However, that it can be sold as alchemical gold, and according to the estimation of expert goldsmiths, none of those Doctors deny, nor should it be denied. It is also not permitted to do this so that one may use it in medicines: for on account of the reason already mentioned, it must be judged neither to have the healing powers of true gold, nor to be free from noxious qualities contracted from the charcoal fire and poisonous quicksilver <note>Mercury.</note>. Much less is it permitted to use it for the mixing of coins...

# BOOK I. CHAPTER V. QUESTION IV.

mixture: for this would be a primary instance of a <term>monetary crime</term> <note>original: "criminis monetarii"; referring to the illegal debasement or counterfeiting of currency</note>, according to the stated decree of Pope John XXII. The aforementioned Doctors of Law agree that such persons are to be punished with the penalty for adulterated coinage. In these cases, it would constitute a **Mortal Sin**. If, however, someone were to cast or produce it only for the sake of using it themselves for rings, chains, or their own household vessels: they must strive with all care and vigilance to ensure that no one is deceived by it, whether during their life or after their death. Otherwise, according to the extent of the negligence, a gravity of sin would arise here as well; and no matter how diligent one is, they would scarcely escape the fault of vanity. Certainly, one never escapes the charge of folly and imprudence if they seek profit from this art. For a merchant is always a fool who applies himself to that kind of business in which he sees no one—or perhaps only one in several thousand—make a profit, while all others (or a thousand to one) have consumed their entire resources. Since this is what happens with alchemists, the same must undoubtedly be felt regarding these losses of wealth. Nor are those safe in conscience who are led in this matter by mere vain curiosity, for *curiosity is a sin opposed to studiousness by excess: by which one desires immoderately to know what is not expedient, or more than is expedient for them, or in a manner that is not expedient; and it is one of the daughters of pride.* <insert>[See St. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica 2. 2. question 167. And a mind devoted to vanities and false insanities exposes itself to be deceived by the Devil; St. Augustine teaches that this is the beginning of all superstition in Book 2 of "On Christian Doctrine," chapters 22 and 23. And by the testimony of Divine Scripture, he who loves danger shall perish in it. For thus the Wise Man speaks: *In unnecessary matters, do not search in manifold ways, and in the many works of God, you shall not be curious. For suspicion (that is, vain hope and expectation) has supplanted many. And it has held their senses in vanity.* Finally, he adds: *he who loves danger shall perish in it.* Ecclesiasticus 3, from verse 24. Finally, in a word: *when the end is evil, the art is illicit: and as the end is, such is the practice.*]</insert>

<margin>D</margin> *If the manner (to which the means belong) of practicing the art is illicit, the art is also illicit; if the manner is vain, the art is vain; if it is sordid and defiled, the art will be defiled and impure.* <margin>d Book 3, chapter 3.</margin> To this must be referred superstitious means, such as if they arise from an express or tacit pact with a demon; which the author of *Demonomania* **d** <note>Jean Bodin's "De la démonomanie des sorciers" (1580)</note> has shown to be frequent, and experience certainly teaches: so much so that Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Raymond Lull, Geber, Roger Bacon, and Richard the Englishman have been vehemently suspected of this Black Magic. Paracelsus, Agrippa, and that William Constantine, and several others have been caught; and among the instructions for the Inquisitors of the Faith, there is one noting that heretical mages are recognized by this mark, in the *Directory for Inquisitors of the Faith, part 3, page 443 of the Roman edition, which was published by order of Gregory XIII.* There, the author of the book, Nicholas Eymeric, adds the reason: because for the most part, alchemists, when they cannot reach their intended goal, seek the help of a demon (I provided an example in the preceding question); they invoke and implore him, and in imploring they entreat him, and tacitly or expressly sacrifice to him.] <insert>[Experience adds to this, because whenever alchemy has flourished among many, sorceries and fortune-telling have also flourished among many, as we experience in this unhappy age. Also because there are those who employ vain observances of ceremonies that contribute nothing to the effect, such as a certain day, a tunic of raw linen, abstinence from certain foods, and other such inanities. But if hatred or incontinence is added to greed, it is very easy to recur to love potions and poisonings. One also sins not lightly in the teaching of the art, when they abuse the names and words of Holy Scripture for their own nomenclatures or precepts; and no less when they insert into their books figures or images that are either plainly obscene or even utterly blasphemous due to the abuse of sacred things. This was done by that printer of Basel who published the "Rosary" <note>The "Rosarium Philosophorum," a famous alchemical text</note>; he took icons of the Holy Trinity, of the Risen Lord Christ, and of the Coronation of the Blessed Virgin Mary—which heretical men have cast out of the churches—and transferred them to alchemical mysteries and transformations. It criticizes famous alchemists and the use of sacred Christian imagery in alchemical texts as blasphemous.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the moral and practical boundaries of alchemy and chemical arts, specifically addressing the circumstances of time and the social standing of the practitioner.</meta>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

This is not surprising. For the Sacramentarians <note>A term used here for those with unorthodox views on the sacraments, likely implying a lack of spiritual grounding</note> are everywhere perverse. Finally, sins are often committed through overly curious experiments, while they follow now this method, now that, and thus they never know what they are always trying to learn.

<margin>E</margin>
The circumstance of time must also be carefully weighed. For the loss of time is most precious, and an account of time badly spent must be rendered to God. Therefore, those sin who devote too much time to these arts; likewise those who do so at a time when the requirements of their office, or some other debt of charity or justice, demands otherwise. For example: does someone hold a magistracy? He cannot take away the hours necessary for the proper exercise of his duty. Is he the head of a family? The proper management of the household should not suffer any disadvantage. Is he a member of the clergy? The requirements of his order must be placed before his furnaces and his own pleasure. Does the necessity of a neighbor demand your help? Charity commands you to be present for him; if you do not, you sin. Finally, I shall say it in one word: whatever time one may honestly give to leisure, that much may be allowed for these studies—and no more. I call "leisure" that rest and vacation from the actions and studies to which one is bound.

<margin>F</margin>
Finally, the condition of the persons involved introduces the most significant distinction here; for this usually intends an illicit end, gives rise to errors in method, and makes the timing inopportune. Above all, this art must be judged illicit for the poor; indeed, for all private citizens, it is full of danger. This becomes manifest, first because such people usually look for profit, and thus they are excluded by the reasoning already mentioned regarding the "circumstance of the end." For they intend to use that gold for commerce, medicines, or coinage; or if they do not use it themselves, there is an open danger that their heirs will. These people are impoverished by this art; they do not provide the necessities for their families; they are the cause of their wives and daughters being forced to support themselves by shameful gain. When the means and instruments fail due to poverty, they easily progress to superstitious practices, and even to the pacts we mentioned regarding the "circumstance of method." Indeed, the chemical writers themselves have testified to this. For <margin>* The Rosary of the Philosophers, folio 224.</margin> Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the legendary Arab alchemist</note> says: *Therefore, this science is not suitable for the poor and needy; rather, it is their enemy.* Secondly, in the *Politics*, the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> says, *It is impossible for a person in want to practice philosophy.* (Meaning here: to study alchemy. For those common writers in the books of the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <margin>Book on the Perfect Mastery.</margin> call only alchemists "philosophers"—quite arrogantly.) And the same Geber says elsewhere: [*The practitioner of this science must be of the most subtle wit, and must know and recognize the natures of metals and their generations, infirmities, and imperfections in their mines before he approaches this art. Let no practitioner approach its investigation who is possessed of a coarse and dull wit, nor one who is greedy, nor a miser regarding costs and expenses.*] Nor should he be a man of double mind, without spirit or backbone, or variable in mind, nor too hasty; but rather *a son of doctrine, a man adorned with the most subtle wit, sufficiently wealthy, generous, healthy, firm in purpose and constant, patient, mild, long-suffering, and temperate.*] Therefore, for the miser and the poor man, this science has an inaccessible and forbidden threshold, according to its Prince, Geber. And note that he requires one to be "sufficiently wealthy": that is, one who has a surplus beyond necessities for this study. He also excludes the simple and those of small judgment when he says in the same place that one must not be "without spirit or backbone"; for such people are most easily circumvented by the devil and other impostors; therefore, this art is forbidden to them because of the danger. He also excludes those who love to deceive others when he adds: "nor a man of double mind," because the ruin of others, which they seek, bars them. He excludes the intemperate, such as the lustful and those who love revenge, since he wants them to be long-suffering and temperate; otherwise, there is a danger that they might resort to hostile or amatory sorcery. He excludes the variable, inconstant, and impatient: because these people, when they do not succeed, easily flee to demonic assistance. He excludes those of blunt wit, because they waste their oil and labor; likewise those unskilled in true philosophy, for the same reason. <margin>g Ibid. folio 224.</margin> Arnold of Villanova <note>A famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</note> confirms this in these words: [*Whoever wishes to reach this science and is not a philosopher is a fool: because this science is only concerning the secrets of the philosophers.*] Therefore, on account of failed attempts and the loss of time and expense, see also to these...

<meta>catchword: see.</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the moral restrictions on practicing alchemy, arguing that it is forbidden to the poor, the unlearned, and the impatient due to the high risk of demonic influence or financial ruin. It emphasizes that alchemy should only be a leisure activity for the wealthy and virtuous who have already fulfilled their social and religious duties.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, ethics of science, Geber, Arnold of Villanova, poverty and crime, time management, demonic pacts, qualifications for alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
BOOK I. CHAPTER V. QUESTION IV. 69

It seems illicit; and since most common alchemists are of such a sort, it follows that for most, it is illicit in the "court of heaven" <note>Latin: *in foro poli*; referring to the judgment of God and conscience rather than earthly law</note>, which is what we are now discussing. Finally, the masters of the art demand—and rightly so—that a man be humble, just, pious, and God-fearing. These words of Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> are cited $^h$: <margin>h From the fourth of the treatises</margin> *It is necessary for him who wishes to be introduced into this art and hidden wisdom to repel the vice of arrogance from himself, and to be pious and upright.* <margin>Rosarium, folio 223</margin> And later: *Son, before all things it is necessary to fear God, in whom the vision of your disposition is seen.* And that saying of a certain Alphidius: *Know, son, that you cannot possess this science until you purify your mind for God, and God knows that you have a steadfast and upright soul.* Since it bars the proud, it teaches that the art is illicit for the over-curious; since it requires piety, it rejects the superstitious; since it requires uprightness, the fear of God, and purity of soul, it detests all those vicious circumstances we have mentioned.

<margin>G</margin> Therefore, alchemy is not permitted to any others, by reason of circumstances, except those of this kind. From this, first, every individual can weigh within himself whether he is such a person, and whether any dangers—and which ones—threaten him from a defect in any of these circumstances; he ought to communicate these things with his Confessors and follow their judgment.

<margin>H</margin> Second, the confessor can see when such a person is free from sin, or when venial or even mortal sin is present; and when absolution should be granted, denied, or delayed. It should be delayed when there is a likely danger of relapse; denied when he sins gravely against justice or charity on account of this study and refuses to stop.

Third. Because curiosity is very often the cause of the study, and this vice creeps significantly from smaller to greater things, one must see diligently that no one wanders off into erroneous experiments and opinions. For example: let no one think that a true human being can be produced by this art—in a manner not instituted by nature for this generation—but by urine or another humor, cooked in glass vials by fire or the sun. Julius Camillus <note>An Italian philosopher known for his "Theater of Memory"</note> rashly asserted this, and Tommaso Garzoni <note>Author of *The Universal Piazza of All the Professions*</note> incautiously believed it in *Discourse 41*, and some attribute this invention to Arnaldus de Villa Nova. Whether this is true or a lie is not necessary to dispute, says Mariana <note>Juan de Mariana, a Spanish Jesuit historian</note> in *History of Spain, Book 14, Chapter 9*. For to assert or to experiment that such a thing could be done would be foolish, impious, erroneous, and blasphemous. This is because, since a human being presupposes a body and a rational soul, if what you produce is a human, it would follow that God—by the law and order He fixed for nature—would be bound to infuse a rational soul into this body, which is blasphemous; or that a rational soul is drawn out from the potentiality of matter, which is heretical; for then the soul would be subject to corruption and mortal. Furthermore, such a soul would not have original sin, because this body would not have been propagated through the generation of a man, nor would the human be conceived [in the natural way]; nor would he need the redemption of Christ—all of which are heretical.

<margin>I</margin> Fourth. From all this, it is clear that the law (which they say exists in England) is excellent: that no one, under penalty of death, is allowed to practice alchemy without the permission of the Prince. It would be expedient for this law to be carried elsewhere as well. For then the Prince, with selection, can permit such things after considering the aforementioned circumstances; for he must render an account to God if he permits it rashly and promiscuously, given the damages arising therefrom. Then also, most of the dangers we spoke of would cease.

<margin>K</margin> Fifth. It is clear (unless some circumstance prevents it) that this art is ordinarily permissible for Princes and the very wealthy, who possess such knowledge of philosophy and such wit that they are not open to frauds and impostures; and such piety that they abstain from offending God and neighbor; and such wealth that they do not burden their subjects for its sake; and such love for their subjects and fear of God that they do not neglect public governance or the relief of the poor for its sake, nor cast away the reins entrusted to them by God and hand them over entirely to others. For just as princes can relax their minds with other honest studies as much as is necessary, there is no doubt they can do so with this study. Truly, what Eymeric <note>Nicholas Eymeric, a famous Inquisitor</note> says regarding the judgments of heretical magic...

It specifically condemns the "blasphemous" idea of creating artificial life (homunculi) and suggests that alchemy should be strictly regulated by the state.</summary>

# INVESTIGATIONS INTO MAGIC

<margin>Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica 2.2.q.77.a.2.</margin> Regarding what had been said about the art of alchemy, the commentator Lucas de Penna <note>A 14th-century jurist known for his work on the Justinian Code</note> noted excellently in his commentary that such a presumption of legitimacy is not to be granted to common men, though it may have a place among leading men and the very wealthy. And Cardinal Cajetan <note>Tommaso de Vio (1469–1534), a major theologian and commentator on Aquinas</note> wrote eloquently and prudently: *this art either belongs to no men at all; or it belongs only to Princes, after hearing the counsel of wise men.* It is certainly manifest that many of the dangers mentioned previously do not apply to Princes.

As for others, there is no reason for them to flatter themselves or think their consciences are safe based on the opinion of Oldradus <note>Oldradus de Ponte, a famous 14th-century lawyer who argued alchemy could be legal</note> and other legal experts. For those men were dealing only with the external court <note>The secular or public legal system, as opposed to the "internal court" of the soul/conscience</note>, which was the subject of their inquiry. And most of them did not discuss the matter deeply; but, just as sheep follow the goat who leads the flock, so they followed Oldradus with closed eyes as if he were an oracle (whose arguments we have already dismantled with what has been said). They added nothing to his words (such as *Johannes Andreae, Abbas, Albericus, and Guido Papa*). Most of them also speak ambiguously, saying it is legal if they make true gold, and do so by natural art (*as do Oldradus, Isernia, Baldus, Bruno, and Fabianus*). Yet not one of them all, except for Thomas Arconcinus, made mention of the Papal Decree <note>The decree "Spondent pariter" issued by Pope John XXII in 1317, which banned alchemy and ordered the confiscation of alchemical gold</note> beginning with the word *Spondent*; they either did not remember it, or they concealed it in bad faith. And although that canon is not observed in common practice, the opinion and judgment of such a great Pope ought to be enough for the common people (that is, anyone who lacks the conditions required above) to believe they must abstain from this study. The Supreme Roman Pontiff judged that it should be condemned in the gravest words, and from it the Vicar of Christ and successor of Peter studiously discourages them. But let these things suffice concerning a 
->subject that is not commonly encountered.<-
